Existence of four minimal spheres in S 3 S^{3} with a bumpy metric
Zhichao Wang, Xin Zhou

Abstract
. We prove that in the three dimensional sphere with a bumpy metric or a metric with positive Ricci curvature, there exist at least four distinct embedded minimal two-spheres. This confirms a conjecture of S. T. Yau in 1982 for bumpy metrics and metrics with positive Ricci curvature. The proof relies on a multiplicity one theorem for the Simon-Smith min-max theory.



0.
Introduction

In his famous 1982 Problem Section
[
50
]
, S. T. Yau posed the following problem.

Problem

(
[
50
]
*Problem 89)
.

Prove that there are four distinct embedded minimal spheres in any manifold diffeomorphic to
S^{3}
.

In this paper, we provide a solution of this problem when the metric is bumpy or has positive Ricci curvature. Note that a metric
g
on a given closed manifold
M
is called bumpy if every closed embedded minimal hypersurface is non-degenerate. White
[
48
]
proved that the set of bumpy metrics is generic in the Baire sense. Our first main result is as follows, and we refer to Theorem
8.9
for a more general statement and the proof therein.

Theorem A
.

Assume that
g
is a bumpy metric or a metric with positive Ricci curvature on
S^{3}
. Then there exist at least four distinct embedded minimal two-spheres in
(S^{3},g)
.

Around the time when Yau first posed this problem, Simon-Smith
[
42
]
proved the existence of at least one embedded minimal sphere in
S^{3}
with an arbitrary metric, using a variant of the min-max theory for minimal hypersurfaces developed by Almgren
[
3
,
4
]
and Pitts
[
34
]
; see also Schoen-Simon
[
38
]
and Colding-De Lellis
[
6
]
. Later on, White
[
48
]
, using degree methods, proved the existence of at least two embedded minimal spheres when the metric has positive Ricci curvature, and at least four embedded minimal spheres when the metric is sufficiently close to the round metric. Recently, Haslhofer-Ketover
[
13
]
proved the existence of at least two embedded minimal spheres for bumpy metrics, by combining the Simon-Smith min-max theory with the mean curvature flow; a key ingredient of their proof is the Catenoid estimates first appeared in Ketover-Marques-Neves
[
19
]
. We also remark that branched immersed minimal spheres were obtained by Sacks-Ulenbeck
[
35
]
using min-max theory for harmonic maps; see also Colding-Minicozzi
[
8
]
.

The motivation of Yau’s conjecture is tightly related to the topology of the space of embedded spheres in
S^{3}
. By Hatcher’s proof of the Smale conjecture
[
14
]
*Appendix (14), the space of embedded spheres in
S^{3}
deformation retracts to the space of great spheres, which is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
, so the area functional when restricted to this space, should have four nontrivial critical points, that is, embedded minimal spheres. One can simply apply the Simon-Smith min-max theory to the four naturally defined homotopy classes of sweepouts associated with the
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
-structure; see Section
8.3
for detailed discussions. However, the major challenge is that the min-max theory may produce minimal spheres counted with integer multiplicity, so we may not obtain new minimal spheres when applying to new sweepouts. As the major advancement of this paper, we prove a new multiplicity one theorem for the Simon-Smith min-max theory. We refer to Theorem
7.3
for the detailed statement and the proof therein.

Theorem B
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed, orientable, three dimensional Riemannian manifold. Then the min-max varifold associated with any homotopy class of smooth sweepouts of a fixed orientable genus-
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
surface
\Sigma_{0}
is supported on a pairwise disjoint collection of connected, closed, embedded, minimal surface
\{\Gamma_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
with integer multiplicities
\{m_{1},\cdots,m_{N}\}
, so that

•

if
\Gamma_{j}
is unstable and two-sided, then
m_{j}=1
, and

•

if
\Gamma_{j}
is one-sided, then its connected double cover is stable.

Moreover the weighted total genus of
\Sigma_{0}
is bounded by
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
.

Remark 0.1
.

We remark that, as compared with the Multiplicity One Theorem
[
54
]
*Theorem C for the Almgren-Pitts theory where all non-degenerate components have multiplicity one, Theorem
B
only shows that unstable components have multiplicity one. The proof of Theorem
A
, when stable minimal spheres exist, follows by combining a variant of Theorem
B
with A. Song’s cylindrical manifold construction
[
44
]
.

We have observed tremendous development of the Almgren-Pitts min-max theory since the celebrated resolution of the Willmore Conjecture by Marques-Neves
[
28
]
. In particular, Yau’s another famous problem
[
50
]
*Problem 88 on the existence of infinitely many closed minimal surfaces was solved by combining the works of Marques-Neves
[
30
]
and Song
[
44
]
. A key ingredient in this program was the volume spectrum first introduced by Gromov
[
11
]
and later by Guth
[
12
]
. The Weyl law for the volume spectrum, proved by Liokumovich-Marques-Neves
[
24
]
, has led to surprising density and equidistribution results for closed minimal hypersurfaces, by Irie-Marques-Neves
[
16
]
and Marques-Neves-Song
[
27
]
respectively; see also
[
43
]
. After the resolution of the Multiplicity One Conjecture by the second-named author
[
54
]
(see also
[
5
]
), Marques-Neves finished their program on establishing a Morse theory for the area functional
[
31
]
; see also
[
29
,
26
]
. We refer to the survey articles
[
32
,
55
]
for more detailed history on this exciting field.
After we finished the work, there was a nice application of our multiplicity one theorem by Liokumovich-Ketover in their proof of Smale’s conjecture for lens spaces
[
18
]
.

0.1.
Sketch of the proof

We first describe the proof for Theorem
B
. We follow similar strategy as the proof of the Multiplicity One Conjecture in the Almgren-Pitts setting
[
54
]
, that is, to use prescribing mean curvature functionals
\mathcal{A}^{h}
(
1.1
) to approximate the area functional. However, there are several major challenges, mainly caused by the fact that the local
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-isotopy minimizing problem has only
C^{1,1}
-solutions, as proved recently by Sarnataro-Stryker
[
36
]
, and the solution may have a large portion of self-touching set, where the mean curvature vanishes. The challenges and new ideas invoked are summarized as follows.

•

A notion of strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity is introduced to prevent the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-min-max solutions to degenerate to integer multiple of minimal surfaces in our special scenario.

•

A new notion of critical set is introduced to include pairs of varifolds and Caccioppoli sets, and a stronger tightening process is developed to show that all pairs in the min-max critical sets are
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary.

•

A new scheme of proving
C^{1,1}
regularity of the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-min-max solutions is established, using chains of replacements, without invoking unique continuation.

•

A new argument for compactness is used, without proving the optimal Morse index bounds, to show the
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon h}
-min-max solutions converge in
C^{1,1}_{loc}
to embedded minimal surfaces, when
\varepsilon\to 0
. The genus bound of the limit minimal surfaces also follows easily from our construction of the approximating
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon h}
-min-max solutions.

•

A special prescribing function
h
can be chosen, which is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to the first eigenfunctions of all possible limit minimal surfaces (which is a finite set by bumpiness), so that when combined with the strong
\mathcal{A}^{\epsilon h}
-stationarity, one can show that the limit minimal surface with multiplicity greater than one must be stable.

Now we dip into some details of these new ideas. The
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-functional is defined for a pair of
C^{1,1}
-surface
\Sigma
and a Caccioppoli set
\Omega
enclosed by
\Sigma
(
1.7
). A
C^{1,1}
-pair
(\Sigma,\Omega)
which is merely stationary for the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-functional could be just an even multiple of a closed minimal surface with
\Omega=\emptyset
or
M
. A pair
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is called strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary (see Definition
1.15
) if near a touching point, moving the top/bottom sheet away from other sheets increases the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-functional up to the first order, or simply the top/bottom sheet solves the obstacle problem for
\mathcal{A}^{h}
w.r.t. other sheets. Therefore, if a sequence of such pairs
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to a minimal surface
\Sigma_{\infty}
with multiplicity
m\geq 2
, and if the prescribing functions
h_{k}
change sign along
\Sigma_{\infty}
, we know that
\Sigma_{k}
cannot be an
m
-sheeted minimal surface. Otherwise, by Corollary
1.22
,
\Omega
coincides with
M
near points where
h>0
and with
\emptyset
near points where
h<0
, which is not possible.

We set up our min-max problem using smooth sweepouts of surfaces of a fixed genus in the relative setting following
[
6
,
54
]
. We then extend the critical sets (Definition
2.3
) so as to include pairs
(V,\Omega)
in our newly defined
\VC
-space (Section
1.1
). As the main advantage to get back the
\Omega
-factor in the critical set, it makes sense to say
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity for critical pairs. In particular, we re-design the tightening process in Section
2.2
to show that every critical pair is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary. This is an improvement as compared with the previous CMC/PMC min-max theory
[
51
,
52
]
, where it was only shown that the critical varifolds have uniformly bounded first variation.

We then introduce the notion of
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing using smooth isotopies, and use the combinatorial argument originally due to Almgren-Pitts to show the existence of a min-max pair, which is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing in every small annuli. As a by-product of this step, we can show that there exists an integer
L=L(m)
depending only on the dimension of the parameter space, such that for any
L(m)
-number of admissible collection of concentric annuli, the min-max pair is stable in at least one of them. This will play the role of Morse index upper bound when proving the desired compactness results.

To prove the
C^{1,1}
-regularity and strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity for the min-max pairs, we do induction on density (which are integers). Denote by
\mathcal{S}(V,\leq m-1)
and
\mathcal{S}(V,m)
the subsets of the support
\spt\|V\|
with density less than
m
or equal to
m
respectively. Suppose we have proved the regularity in
\mathcal{S}(V,\leq m-1)
. Fix a
q\in\mathcal{S}(V,m)
and a small neighborhood
U_{0}
; we cover
\closure(U_{0})\cap\mathcal{S}(V,m)
by balls of a small radius
\bm{r}
, and then take successive
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacements over these small balls. By a gluing procedure similar to
[
51
]
*§6, we can obtain a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable
C^{1,1}
replacement in
U_{0}
. Letting the radii
\bm{r}\to 0
, the union of these
\bm{r}
-balls will converge to
\closure(U_{0})\cap\mathcal{S}(V,m)
, and by the compactness theory for stable
C^{1,1}
surfaces, these replacements will converge to a pair
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
which is
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable in
U_{0}
. Note that
\spt\|V^{*}\|\subset\spt\|V\|
. To show that
V^{*}
is identical to
V
in
U_{0}
, we can first choose
U_{0}
small enough so that the volume ratio of
V
for some fixed radius
s\gg\bm{r}
centered at any point in
\closure(U_{0})\cap\mathcal{S}(V,m)
is close to
m
. Since
V^{*}
and
V
have the same mass in any open set containing
\closure(U_{0})
, we can show the density of
V^{*}
at any point in
\closure(U_{0})\cap\mathcal{S}(V,m)
is less than or equal to
m
(using the monotonicity formula and the fact that
V^{*}
is an integral varifold), and hence
V^{*}=V
and the
C^{1,1}
-regularity and strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity are proved in
\mathcal{S}(V,m)
. Note that we do not need any unique continuation type result in this procedure.

Next, we consider those
C^{1,1}
min-max pairs
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
associated with the
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}
-functionals for some sequence
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
. The
C^{1,1}_{loc}
convergence (away from a finite set) to a smoothly embedded minimal surface
\Sigma_{\infty}
follows from the fact that each
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
is stable in at least one of any
L(m)
-admissible concentric annuli. We can further choose a diagonal sequence (of closed embedded surfaces of a given genus) converging to
\Sigma_{\infty}
which are
(\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k(j)}h},\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing in small annuli (
\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j}\to 0
). By choosing
h
to vanish along
\Sigma_{\infty}
except for finitely many sub-disks, this diagonal subsequence is
(\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing for the area functional away from these sub-disks, and hence the curve lifting argument in
[
42
,
9
,
20
]
can be applied so as to show the desired genus bound for
\Sigma_{\infty}
.

To prove Theorem
B
, if the convergence
\Sigma_{k}\to\Sigma_{\infty}
has multiplicity greater than one, we can construct a nontrivial nonnegative supsolution to the Jacobi operator
L_{\Sigma_{\infty}}
of
\Sigma_{\infty}
. First by our choice of the prescription function
h
, we know that
\Sigma_{k}
cannot be an integer multiple of minimal surfaces, so we can consider the height difference between the top and bottom sheets. Unlike in the proof of the Almgren-Pitts Multiplicity One Theorem
[
54
]
, where both the top and bottom sheets satisfy the prescribing curvature equation, the mean curvature in our current setting may vanish in a large subset. Nevertheless, the key observation is that the height difference, which is nontrivial, will converge after normalization to a nontrivial weak supersolution
\varphi_{\infty}\geq 0
of

L_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\varphi_{\infty}\geq 2\cdot c\cdot h|_{\Sigma_{\infty}}\varphi_{\infty},

for some constant
c\geq 0
. Since we can take
h|_{\Sigma_{\infty}}
to be
L^{2}
-orthogonal to the first eigenfunction
\phi_{1}
of
L_{\Sigma_{\infty}}
, applying the integration-by-part formula will show the stability of
\Sigma_{\infty}
, that is,
\lambda_{1}(L_{\Sigma_{\infty}})\geq 0
.

All the above arguments provide an outline of proof for Theorem
B
for relative min-max. One can extend this to classical min-max (for free homotopy of sweepouts) using the double-cover-lifting argument as in
[
54
]
.

We now describe how to prove Theorem
A
using Theorem
B
. If
(S^{3},g)
does not contain any stable minimal spheres, Theorem
B
applied to the four natural families of sweepouts of embedded spheres gives at least four distinct embedded minimal spheres with multiplicity one. When
(S^{3},g)
admits a non-degenerate stable minimal sphere
S^{2}
, we can cut
(S^{3},g)
along this sphere to obtain a three-ball
(B^{3},g)
with a stable minimal boundary. We then glue the boundary
S^{2}
with a cylindrical end modeled by
S^{2}\times[0,\infty)
as Song
[
44
]
. The Simon-Smith min-max theory when applied to compact approximations of this noncompact Lipschitz manifold produces embedded minimal spheres in
(B^{3},g)
in the same way as
[
44
]
. One can check that there are two family of sweepouts of embedded spheres in these compact approximations with uniformly bounded width, using the method in
[
13
]
. We can prove a variant of Theorem
B
in this non-compact setting, so as to produce at least two distinct embedded minimal spheres in
(B^{3},g)
. Since there are two such three balls after cutting, we prove the existence of at least five embedded minimal spheres in this case.

0.2.
Outline of the paper

We collect preliminary materials in Section
1
; then we set up the relative min-max problem and establish the tightening process in Section
2
. In Section
3
, we introduce several concepts related to the almost minimizing property and prove the existence of almost minimizing pairs. Section
4
is the first essential part of this paper, where we prove the
C^{1,1}
regularity of min-max pairs. In Section
5
, we prove the convergence of min-max pairs to minimal surfaces for a sequence
\{\varepsilon_{k}h\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\lim\varepsilon_{k}=0
, as well as genus bound for the limit minimal surface. Section
6
, another essential part of this paper, is devoted to the construction of supersolutions. In Section
7
, we prove Theorem
B
, and in Section
8
, we prove Theorem
A
.

1.
Preliminaries

In this part, we collect all necessary preliminary materials. After introducing basic notations, we will introduce the
\VC(M)
space as the closure of the natural embedding
\mathcal{C}(M)\to\mathcal{V}(M)\times\mathcal{C}(M)
under the product metric in Section
1.1
. Then we will define
C^{1,1}
-almost embedded surfaces and boundaries, the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-functional and its associated stationarity in Section
1.2
, and then a crucial notion of strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity and its corollaries in Section
1.3
. Lastly, we will recall stable compactness for
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary boundaries in Section
1.4
, and the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-isotopy minimizing problem in Section
1.5
.

Notations

We will not specify the ambient manifold to the three-sphere before the last section.

•

(M^{3},g)
denotes a closed, oriented, 3-dimensional Riemannian manifold isometrically embedded in some
\mathbb{R}^{L}
, and
U\subset M
an open subset (
U
may be equal to
M
).

•

\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)
(
p\in M
,
0<s<r
) denotes an annulus given by
B(p,r)\setminus\closure(B(p,s))
.

•

h\in C^{\infty}(M)
denotes a smooth mean curvature prescribing function.

•

\mathcal{C}(M)
or
\mathcal{C}(U)
denotes the space of sets
\Omega\subset M
or
\Omega\subset U\subset M
with finite perimeter (Caccioppoli set); see
[
41
]
*§14.

•

\mathcal{V}(M)
or
\mathcal{V}(U)
denotes the space of
2
-varifolds in
M
or
U
.

•

\mathfrak{X}(U)
denotes the space of smooth vector fields compactly supported in
U
.

•

\Diff_{0}(M)
denotes the connected component of the diffeomorphism group of
M
containing identity, and
\mathfrak{Is}(U)
denotes the set of isotopies of
M
supported in
U
.

•

A collection of connected
C^{1}
-embedded surfaces
\{\Gamma^{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}\subset U
with
\partial\Gamma^{i}\cap U=\emptyset
is said to be ordered, denoted by

\Gamma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell},

if for each
i
,
\Gamma^{i}
separates
U
into two connected components
U^{i}_{+},U^{i}_{-}
, (
U\setminus\Gamma^{i}=U^{i}_{+}\sqcup U^{i}_{-}
), such that
\Gamma^{j}\subset\closure(U^{i}_{-})
for
j=1,\cdots,i-1
, and
\Gamma^{j}\subset\closure(U^{i}_{+})
for
j=i+1,\cdots,\ell
.

1.1.
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-functional and
\mathcal{VC}
-space

The
prescribing mean curvature functional
associated with
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
in
[
52
]
*(0.2) naturally extends to all pairs
(V,\Omega)\in\mathcal{V}(M)\times\mathcal{C}(M)
as:

(1.1)

\mathcal{A}^{h}(V,\Omega)=\|V\|(M)-\int_{\Omega}h\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{3}.

We can naturally define push-forward by diffeomorphisms in
\mathcal{V}(M)\times\mathcal{C}(M)
. Note that given
(V,\Omega)\in\mathcal{V}(M)\times\mathcal{C}(M)
and
F:(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times M\to M
a smooth map with
F^{t}\in\Diff_{0}(M)
, then
t\mapsto\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(F^{t}_{\#}(V,\Omega)\big)
is a smooth function. As a result, we can define
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity for pairs in
\mathcal{V}(M)\times\mathcal{C}(M)
.

Definition 1.1

(
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary pairs)
.

A pair
(V,\Omega)\in\mathcal{V}(M)\times\mathcal{C}(M)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
U
if for any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(U)
with
\phi^{t}
the associated flow,

(1.2)

\begin{split}\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{V,\Omega}(X):&=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\phi^{t}_{\#}(V,\Omega)\big)\\
&=\int_{G_{2}(M)}\mathrm{div}_{S}X(x)\,\mathrm{d}V(x,S)-\int_{\partial\Omega}\langle X,\nu_{\partial\Omega}\rangle h\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\partial\Omega}=0.\end{split}

An
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary pair
(V,\Omega)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stable in U
, if for any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(U)
,

(1.3)

\delta^{2}\mathcal{A}^{h}_{V,\Omega}(X,X):=\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Big|_{t=0}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\phi^{t}_{\#}(V,\Omega)\big)\geq 0.

Remark 1.2
.

Note that
\mathcal{A}^{h}
and its variations
\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}
,
\delta^{2}\mathcal{A}^{h}
are also naturally defined in
\mathcal{V}(U)\times\mathcal{C}(U)
.

We are mainly interested in a subspace of
\mathcal{V}(M)\times\mathcal{C}(M)
which arises as the completion under weak topology of the “diagonals”
\Delta(M)=\{(|\partial\Omega|,\Omega)\in\mathcal{V}(M)\times\mathcal{C}(M):\Omega\in\mathcal{C}(M)\}
.

Definition 1.3
.

Motivated by Almgren’s VZ-space
[
4
]
, we have the following.

(1)

The
\VC
-space
, denoted by
\VC(M)
, is the space of all pairs
(V,\Omega)\in\mathcal{V}(M)\times\mathcal{C}(M)
such that there is a sequence
\{\Omega_{k}\}\subset\mathcal{C}(M)
with
|\partial\Omega_{k}|\to V
in
\mathcal{V}(M)
and
\Omega_{k}\to\Omega
in
\mathcal{C}(M)
.

(2)

Given two pairs
(V,\Omega)
and
(V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
in
\VC(M)
, the
\mathscr{F}
-distance between them
is

\mathscr{F}\big((V,\Omega),(V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})\big):=\mathbf{F}(V,V^{\prime})+\mathcal{F}(\Omega,\Omega^{\prime}),

where
\mathbf{F}
and
\mathcal{F}
are respectively the varifold
\mathbf{F}
-metric and the flat metric.

The next lemma follows from the lower semi-continuity of measure in weak convergence.

Lemma 1.4
.

For every
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
, we have that
\spt(\partial\Omega)\subset\spt(\|V\|)
, and
\|\partial\Omega\|\leq\|V\|
as measures.

Then it is clear that we have the following.

Lemma 1.5
.

Suppose that
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
is an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
U
. Denote
c=\sup_{x\in M}|h(x)|
. Then
V
has
c
-bounded first variation in
U
.

We also have the following.

Lemma 1.6
.

Given any
L>0
, the space

(1.4)

A^{L}=\{(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M):\|V\|(M)\leq L\}

is a compact metric space under the
\mathscr{F}
-metric.

It is also clear that for fixed
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, the map
(V,\Omega)\to\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{V,\Omega}(X)
is continuous under the
\mathscr{F}
-metric, so we have the following.

Lemma 1.7
.

The set

(1.5)

A^{L}_{0}=\{(V,\Omega)\in A^{L}:(V,\Omega)\text{ is $\mathcal{A}^{h}$-stationary}\}

is a compact subset of
A^{L}
under the
\mathscr{F}
-metric.

1.2.
C^{1,1}
almost embedded
h
-surfaces

Definition 1.8

(
C^{1,1}
almost embedding)
.

We say that a
C^{1,1}
immersed surface
\phi:\Sigma\to U
with
\phi(\partial\Sigma)\cap U=\emptyset
is a
C^{1,1}
almost embedded surface
in
U
, if at any point
p\in\phi(\Sigma)
near which
\phi
is not an embedding, there exists a neighborhood
W\subset U
of
p
, such that

•

\Sigma\cap\phi^{-1}(W)
is a disjoint union of connected components
\sqcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\Gamma^{i}
;

•

\phi:\Gamma^{i}\to W
is a
C^{1,1}
embedding for each
i
;

•

for each
i
, any other component
\phi(\Gamma^{j})
, (
j\neq i
), lies on one side of
\phi(\Gamma^{i})
in
W
.

We will denote
\phi(\Sigma)
by
\Sigma
and
\phi(\Gamma^{i})
by
\Gamma^{i}
in appropriate context. The subset of points in
\Sigma
where
\Sigma
is not embedded will be called the
touching set
, and denoted by
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
. The
regular set

\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
will be denoted by
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
.

Remark 1.9
.

Note that the touching set
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
is a relatively closed subset of
\Sigma
, and regular set
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
is relatively open in
\Sigma
.

Definition 1.10

(
C^{1,1}
boundary)
.

We say that a
C^{1,1}
almost embedded surface
\phi:\Sigma\to U
is a
C^{1,1}
(almost embedded) boundary
in
U
, if
\Sigma
is oriented, and there exists
\Omega\in\mathcal{C}(U)
, such that

(1.6)

\phi_{\#}(\llbracket\Sigma\rrbracket)=\partial\Omega\text{ as 2-currents in $U$};

here
\llbracket\Sigma\rrbracket
denotes the fundamental class of
\Sigma
.

Lemma 1.11
.

Let
(\Sigma,\Omega)
be a
C^{1,1}
-boundary in
U
. Then there exists a natural choice of unit normal
\nu_{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
(as an immersed surface), such that if
\Omega\notin\{\emptyset,U\}
, then
\nu_{\Sigma}
coincides with
\nu_{\partial\Omega}
along
\spt(\partial\Omega)
. Moreover, if
\Sigma
decomposes to ordered sheets
\Gamma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell}
in any open subset
W\subset U
, then
\nu_{\Sigma}
must alternate orientations along
\{\Gamma^{i}\}
.

Proof.

The orientation of
\Sigma
induces a choice of unit normal
\nu_{\Sigma}
. If a connected component
\Sigma_{0}
of
\Sigma
(as an immersed surface) intersects
\spt(\partial\Omega)
, we may possibly flip
\nu_{\Sigma}
to
-\nu_{\Sigma}
to let
\nu_{\Sigma}=\nu_{\partial\Omega}
along
\spt(\partial\Omega)
. If this connected component
\Sigma_{0}
has an integer multiplicity (as a subset of
U
), we can order these sheets by keeping
\nu_{\Sigma}
in the first sheet and flipping
\nu_{\Sigma}
alternatively for other sheets. If a connected component
\Sigma_{0}
does not intersect
\spt(\partial\Omega)
, it must have an even multiplicity by (
1.6
), and we can arbitrarily order them by flipping
\nu_{\Sigma}
alternatively. Thus
\nu_{\Sigma}
has been chosen.

We now check that the orientations of ordered sheet decomposition
\{\Gamma^{i}\}
must alternate. Write
\nu
for
\nu_{\Sigma}
. Assume for contradiction that
\nu|_{\Gamma^{i+1}}
and
\nu|_{\Gamma^{i}}
point to the same direction. If
\Gamma^{i+1}
is not identical to
\Gamma^{i}
, this violates the assumption (
1.6
). Assume now
\Gamma^{i+1}=\Gamma^{i}
. To show that
\nu
alternates, we need to enlarge the open subset
W
and track the connected components containing
\Gamma^{i+1}=\Gamma^{i}
until either one sheet separates from the other, or we find a connected component of
\Sigma
with multiplicity greater than one. The first case follows from the former discussion, and the later case follows from our choice of
\nu_{\Sigma}
above.
∎

The
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-functional is naturally defined on a
C^{1,1}
-boundary as follows:

(1.7)

\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma,\Omega)=\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma)-\int_{\Omega}h\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{3}.

Lemma 1.12
.

Let
(\Sigma,\Omega)
be a
C^{1,1}
-boundary in
U
. For any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(U)
, the first variation is

(1.8)

\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{\Sigma,\Omega}(X)=\int_{\Sigma}\mathrm{div}_{\Sigma}X-\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle h\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}.

Proof.

Note that by (
1.2
), the integral of
\langle X,\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle
in
\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}
is only defined on
\partial\Omega
. Nevertheless, we can use the
C^{1,1}
-structure to rewrite it over
\Sigma
. Indeed, we only need to check (
1.8
) locally. Given any
p\in\Sigma
, there is a neighborhood
W\subset U
of
p
, such that
\Sigma
decomposes into ordered sheets
\Gamma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell}
. For each
\Gamma^{i}
, we choose
\Omega^{i}
to be the connected component of
W\setminus\Gamma^{i}
such that
\nu_{\Sigma}=\nu_{\partial\Omega^{i}}
. Then by the Constancy Theorem
[
41
]
*26.27, we know that
\llbracket\Omega\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}W\rrbracket-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\llbracket\Omega^{i}\rrbracket=m\llbracket W\rrbracket
for some integer
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, for any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(W)
, we know
\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{\Sigma,\Omega}(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{\Gamma^{i},\Omega^{i}}(X)
, which is exactly (
1.8
).
∎

Definition 1.13

(
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary)
.

A
C^{1,1}
-boundary
(\Sigma,\Omega)
in
U
is called a
C^{1,1}
(almost embedded)
h
-boundary
in
U
,
if
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
U
;
that is, for any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(U)
,
\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{\Sigma,\Omega}(X)=0
.

Lemma 1.14
.

Assume that
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is a
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary. Then the regular set
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
is smoothly embedded, and its mean curvature
H
(w.r.t. the unit normal
\nu_{\Sigma}
) is prescribed by
h
; that is,

H=h|_{\Sigma},\quad\text{ on }\mathcal{R}(\Sigma).

Proof.

It follows from the first variation formula (
1.8
) and standard elliptic regularity theory. ∎

1.3.
Strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity

Near a touching point, while the above notion says that the sheets as a union is stationary for
\mathcal{A}^{h}
, a relatively stronger notion says that the top and bottom sheets are stationary for
\mathcal{A}^{h}
w.r.t. deformations pointing away from all other sheets. This is the following
strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary property
.

Definition 1.15

(Strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity)
.

A
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is said to be
strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
U
, if the following holds:

For every
p\in\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)\cap U
, that is,
\ell:=\Theta^{2}(\Sigma,p)\geq 2
,
there exists a small neighborhood
W\subset U
of
p
, and decomposition
\Sigma\cap W=\cup_{i=1}^{\ell}\Gamma^{i}
into
\ell\geq 2
connected disks with a natural ordering
\Gamma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell}
. Denote by
W^{1}
and
W^{\ell}
the bottom and top components of
W\setminus\Sigma
. We require for
i=1,\ell
and all
X\in\mathfrak{X}(W)
pointing into
W^{i}
along
\Gamma^{i}
,

(1.9)

\begin{split}\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{\Gamma^{i},W^{i}}(X)\geq 0,&\quad\text{ when $W^{i}\subset\Omega$},\\
\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{\Gamma^{i},W\setminus W^{i}}(X)\geq 0,&\quad\text{ when $W^{i}\cap\Omega=\emptyset$}.\end{split}

Remark 1.16
.

This notion simply means that moving the top/bottom sheet of
\Sigma
near
p
away from all other sheets increases the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-functional up to the first order. If a
C^{1,1}
-boundary
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is the limit of an isotopic
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-minimizing sequence of embedded surfaces, then
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary; see Theorem
1.25
.

The strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary property can deduce more information of
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
as follows.

Lemma 1.17
.

As above, let
(\Sigma,\Omega)
be a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
U
.

i)

If
p\in\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
and
\Theta^{2}(\Sigma,p)
is odd, then there exists a neighborhood
W
of
p
, such that
\Sigma\cap W
is a minimal surface with multiplicity
\Theta^{2}(\Sigma,p)
, and
h(x)=0
for all
x\in\Sigma\cap W
.

ii)

The generalized mean curvature of
\Sigma
as an immersion vanishes
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-almost everywhere in
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
.

Remark 1.18
.

Item
i)
implies that if
h\neq 0
in a neighborhood of
p\in\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
, then
\Theta^{2}(\Sigma,p)
is an even number.

Proof.

We continue to use notations in Definition
1.15
. Let
p\in\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
with
\Theta^{2}(\Sigma,p)=\ell\geq 2
. Assume that the ordered sheets
\Gamma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell}
in
W\subset U
are graphs of
C^{1,1}
-functions
u^{1}\leq\cdots\leq u^{\ell}
over a common domain
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with

u^{1}(0)=\cdots=u^{\ell}(0)=0.

We first prove the first half of Item i), which describes the structure of
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
with odd density.

Claim 1
.

If
p\in\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
and
\ell
is odd, then
\Sigma\cap W
is an embedded disk with multiplicity
\ell
.

Proof of Claim
1
.

By Lemma
1.11
, the orientations of
\Gamma^{i}
must alternate, and hence only one of
W^{1},W^{\ell}
lies in
\Omega
(as
\ell
is odd). Assume without loss of generality
W^{1}\subset\Omega
and
W^{\ell}\cap\Omega=\emptyset
. Choose the unit normal vector fields
\nu^{1},\nu^{\ell}
along
\Gamma^{1},\Gamma^{\ell}
pointing away from
\Omega
respectively. By (
1.9
), we have

\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\mathrm{div}_{\Gamma^{1}}X-h\cdot\langle X,\nu^{1}\rangle\geq 0,\quad\text{ for all $X\in\mathfrak{X}(W)$ with $\langle X,\nu^{1}\rangle_{\Gamma^{1}}\leq 0$},

\int_{\Gamma^{\ell}}\mathrm{div}_{\Gamma^{\ell}}X-h\cdot\langle X,\nu^{\ell}\rangle\geq 0,\quad\text{ for all $X\in\mathfrak{X}(W)$ with $\langle X,\nu^{\ell}\rangle_{\Gamma^{\ell}}\geq 0$}.

This implies that the generalized mean curvatures (w.r.t.
\nu^{1},\nu^{\ell}
respectively) satisfy:

(1.10)

H_{\Gamma^{1}}\leq h|_{\Gamma^{1}}\quad\text{ and }\quad H_{\Gamma^{\ell}}\geq h|_{\Gamma^{\ell}}.

Note that
\nu^{1},\nu^{\ell}
all point upward as
\ell
is odd. Subtracting the two inequalities in (
1.10
), the height difference
\varphi=u^{\ell}-u^{1}\in C^{1,1}(\mathcal{W})
satisfies a differential inequality almost everywhere:

L_{\mathcal{W}}\varphi\geq h(x,u^{\ell}(x))-h(x,u^{1}(x))=c(x)\varphi(x),

where
L_{\mathcal{W}}
is a positive elliptic operator on
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Since
\varphi\geq 0
and
\varphi=0
somewhere, by the Harnack estimates for strong solutions
[
10
]
*Theorem 9.22, we must have
\varphi\equiv 0
.
This proves Claim
1
.
∎

We now determine the generalized mean curvature of each slice on
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
. By basic function theory applying to the functions
u^{1}\leq\cdots\leq u^{\ell}
, we know that the Hessians
\{\Hess u^{i}\}
are identical almost everywhere along
\{u^{1}=\cdots=u^{\ell}\}
, and so the generalized mean curvature
H^{i}
of
\Gamma^{i}
(w.r.t. a common unit normal) are identical almost everywhere along
\Gamma^{1}\cap\cdots\cap\Gamma^{\ell}
. We next show that these generalized mean curvatures are zero almost everywhere in
\Gamma^{1}\cap\cdots\cap\Gamma^{\ell}
.

Since
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and
\Gamma^{i}
is a
C^{1,1}
-surface, we have by (
1.8
),

(1.11)

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma^{i}}\mathrm{div}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\int_{\Gamma^{i}}(-1)^{i-1}h(x)\langle X,\nu^{i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}(x),\quad\forall X\in\mathfrak{X}(W),

where
\nu^{i}
denotes the upward-pointing unit normal of
\Gamma^{i}
. Also the generalized mean curvature
H^{i}
of
\Gamma^{i}
(w.r.t.
\nu^{i}
) satisfies

\int_{\Gamma^{i}}\mathrm{div}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}=\int_{\Gamma^{i}}H^{i}\langle X,\nu^{i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2},\quad\forall X\in\mathfrak{X}(W).

This together with (
1.11
) gives that

(1.12)

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}H^{i}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}(-1)^{i-1}h(x),

for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e.
x\in\Gamma^{1}\cap\cdots\cap\Gamma^{\ell}
.
Recall that for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e.
x\in\Gamma^{1}\cap\cdots\cap\Gamma^{\ell}
, we have

H^{1}(x)=\cdots=H^{\ell}(x),\quad\text{and }\quad\nu^{1}(x)=\cdots=\nu^{\ell}(x).

•

If
\ell
is even
, we also know that the sum of the right hand side of (
1.12
) vanishes
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e. on
\Gamma^{1}\cap\cdots\cap\Gamma^{\ell}
.
By (
1.12
) again, we have that for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e.
x\in\Gamma^{1}\cap\cdots\cap\Gamma^{\ell}
,

\displaystyle H^{1}(x)=\cdots=H^{\ell}(x)=0.

•

If
\ell
is odd
, then by Claim
1
, We have
\Gamma^{1}=\cdots=\Gamma^{\ell}=:\overline{\Gamma}
in
W
. Then (
1.12
) gives that for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e.
x\in\overline{\Gamma}
,

H^{1}(x)=\cdots=H^{\ell}(x)=\frac{1}{\ell}h(x).

Together with (
1.10
), we have that for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e.
x\in\overline{\Gamma}
,

h(x)\geq H^{1}(x)=\frac{1}{\ell}h(x)=H^{\ell}(x)\geq h(x).

It follows that for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e.
x\in\overline{\Gamma}
,

H^{1}(x)=\cdots=H^{\ell}(x)=h(x)=0.

This finishes the proof of
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e. vanishing of
H
on
\{\Theta^{2}(\Sigma,p)=\ell\}
for
\ell\geq 2
, and hence on all
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
.
∎

By combining Lemma
1.14
and Lemma
1.17
, we have the following characterization of the mean curvature of strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary
h
-boundaries.

Corollary 1.19
.

As above, let
\nu
be the unit outer normal of
\Sigma
induced by
\Omega
by Lemma
1.11
. Then the generalized mean curvature
H
of
\Sigma
w.r.t.
\nu
satisfies:

H(p)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}h(p)&\text{ when }p\in\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)\cap U\\
0&\text{ for $\mathcal{H}^{2}$-a.e. }p\in\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)\cap U\end{array}\right..

We can also deduce the following important corollary of strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity, which asserts when the top/bottom sheets can have touching subsets.

Proposition 1.20
.

As above, given
p\in\Sigma\cap U
, assume that
\Sigma
decomposes into ordered sheets
\Gamma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell}
in a neighborhood
W\subset U
of
p
. Then the following holds (note that all generalized mean curvatures are defined w.r.t.
\nu
induced from
\Omega
by Lemma
1.11
):

(1)

Assume
h>0
in
W
.

i)

If
\Omega
does not contain the region above
\Gamma^{\ell}
, then
\Gamma^{\ell}
belongs to
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
and
H^{\ell}=h|_{\Gamma^{\ell}}
.

ii)

If
\Omega
contains the region above
\Gamma^{\ell}
, then
\Gamma^{\ell}
may contain a subset of
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
, and in this case
H^{\ell}\leq h|_{\Gamma^{\ell}}
.

(2)

Assume
h<0
in
W
.

i)

If
\Omega
contains the region above
\Gamma^{\ell}
, then
\Gamma^{\ell}
belongs to
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
and
H^{\ell}=h|_{\Gamma^{\ell}}
.

ii)

If
\Omega
does not contain the region above
\Gamma^{\ell}
, then
\Gamma^{\ell}
may contain a subset of
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
, and in this case
H^{\ell}\geq h|_{\Gamma^{\ell}}
.

Remark 1.21
.

Since we may flip the ordering, all the above statements for
\Gamma^{\ell}
have corresponding statements for
\Gamma^{1}
. For instance, in Case (
1
)(i), if
\ell
is an even number, then
\Omega
does not contain the region below
\Gamma^{1}
, so
\Gamma^{1}
belongs to
\mathcal{R}(\Sigma)
and
H^{1}=h|_{\Gamma^{1}}
; if
\ell
is odd,
\Omega
contains the region below
\Gamma^{1}
, so
\Gamma^{1}
may contain a subset of
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
, and in this case we only have
H^{1}\leq h|_{\Gamma^{1}}
.

Proof.

By possibly simultaneously flipping
(\Omega,h)
to
(M\setminus\Omega,-h)
, we only need to prove Case
1
. For Case
1
(i), if for contrary
\Gamma^{\ell}\cap\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)\neq\emptyset
, then by the assumption of orientations and Corollary
1.19
, we must have

H^{\ell}\leq h|_{\Gamma^{\ell}},\text{ and }H^{\ell}=0<h|_{\Gamma^{\ell}}\text{ along }\Gamma^{\ell}\cap\mathcal{S}(\Sigma).

However, under these assumptions, for any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(W)
with
\langle X,\nu\rangle>0
along
\Gamma^{\ell}
, (note that
\nu
points into
W^{\ell}
using notations in Definition
1.15
),
we have

\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{\Gamma^{\ell},W^{\ell}}(X)=\int_{\Gamma^{\ell}}(H^{\ell}-h)\langle X,\nu\rangle d\mathcal{H}^{2}<0,

which contradicts with the strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary assumption.

Case
1
(ii) follows directly from Corollary
1.19
using the above argument.
∎

Finally, we have the following direct corollary which forbids a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1,}

h
-boundary to collapse to an even multiple of minimal surfaces in certain situation.

Corollary 1.22
.

As above, assume that
p
lies in the interior of
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
and
h\neq 0
near
p
. By Lemma
1.17
, there exists some neighborhood
W\subset U
of
p
, such that
\Sigma\cap W=m[\Gamma]
for some
m\in 2\mathbb{N}
and some minimal surface
\Gamma
. Then

i)

if
h>0
in
W
, then
\Omega\cap W=W
;

ii)

if
h<0
in
W
, then
\Omega\cap W=\emptyset
.

1.4.
Stability and compactness

In this subsection, we recall the compactness of stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundaries in
[
36
]
*§16 and §17, which are natural generalizations of
[
37
,
39
,
52
]
to the
C^{1,1}
-PMC setting. We will further show that the strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary property is preserved under suitable notion of convergence.

Definition 1.23

(stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary)
.

Let
(\Sigma,\Omega)
be a
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in an open set
U\subset M
.
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is
stable
in
U
if for any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(U)
with
\phi^{t}
the associated flow (see also (
1.3
)),

\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\phi^{t}(\Sigma,\Omega)\big)\geq 0.

If in addition
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary, by direct calculation, this is equivalent to,

\int_{\Sigma}|\nabla^{\perp}X^{\perp}|^{2}-\mathrm{Ric}(X^{\perp},X^{\perp})-|A^{\Sigma}|^{2}|X^{\perp}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\geq\int_{\partial\Omega}\langle X^{\perp},\nabla h\rangle\langle X,\nu\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2},

where
X^{\perp}
is the normal part of
X
w.r.t.
\Sigma
,
\mathrm{Ric}
is the Ricci curvature of
(M,g)
, and
A^{\Sigma}
is the second fundamental form of
\Sigma
(as an immersion) w.r.t. the unit outward normal
\nu
.

Proposition 1.24
.

Let
h_{j},h\in C^{2}(M)
be such that
\|h_{j}-h\|_{C^{2}}\to 0
. Let
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of stable
C^{1,1}

h_{j}
-boundary in
U
satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma_{j}\cap U)\leq\Lambda
for some
\Lambda>0
. Then there exists a stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary
(\Sigma,\Omega)
, so that
(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})
subsequently converges to
(\Sigma,\Omega)
in the following sense:

(1)

\Sigma_{j}
converges to
\Sigma
in
U
as varifolds and also in the sense of
C^{1,\alpha}_{loc}
for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
;

(2)

\Omega_{j}
converges to
\Omega
as currents in
\mathcal{C}(U)
.

Furthermore, we also have the following.

(i)

If
h\equiv 0
, then
\Sigma_{j}
converges to
\Sigma
in
U
in the
C^{1,1}_{loc}
topology.

(ii)

If
(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})
is strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h_{j}}
-stationary in
U
, then
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
U
.

Proof.

The subsequential convergence (1) and (2) are essentially proved by Sarnataro-Stryker
[
36
]
*Lemma 16.3 and Theorem 17.3. Note that they require relatively stronger assumption (their Theorem 1.1) on the regularity of
(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})
to derive the stability inequality
[
36
]
*(17.1) and show it can be passed to limit under
C^{1,\alpha}_{loc}
-convergence. This part can be replaced by the following fact: for a fixed
X\in\mathfrak{X}(U)
, the maps
(\Sigma,\Omega,h)\mapsto\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{\Sigma,\Omega}(X)
and
(\Sigma,\Omega,h)\mapsto\delta^{2}\mathcal{A}^{h}_{\Sigma,\Omega}(X,X)
are both continuous w.r.t. the product topology in
\mathcal{V}(M)\times\mathcal{C}(M)\times C^{2}(M)
. Therefore, the blowup limit in their proof of Theorem 17.3 is stationary and stable. All other parts therein work well under our stable
C^{1,1}

h_{j}
-boundary assumptions. By this fact, we also know that
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is a stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary.

Assume that
h\equiv 0
, then
\Sigma
is a smooth minimal surface, and
\Sigma_{j}
can be written as ordered
C^{1,1}
graphs over
\Sigma
for all large
j
. Moreover, the
C^{1,1}_{loc}
norm is bounded by the
C^{1,\alpha}
norm which converges to
0
by Proposition
A.2
; see also
[
36
]
*Corollary 11.2. Thus we conclude Item (i) – the
C^{1,1}_{loc}
convergence.

It remains to prove Item (ii) – the strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity. Fix
p\in\Sigma\cap U
with
\ell:=\Theta^{2}(\Sigma,p)\geq 2
. Let
W\subset U
be a neighborhood of
p
so that
\Sigma
has a decomposition
\Sigma\cap W=\cup_{i=1}^{\ell}\Gamma^{i}
into
\ell
ordered sheets

\Gamma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell}.

Denote by
W^{1}
and
W^{\ell}
the bottom and top components of
W\setminus\Sigma
(as in Definition
1.15
).
Without loss of generality, we assume that
W^{1}\subset\Omega
. Take an arbitrary
X\in\mathfrak{X}(W)
pointing into
W^{1}
along
\Gamma^{1}
.

Recall that
\Sigma_{j}
converges to
\Sigma
in the sense of
C^{1,\alpha}
. Thus for all sufficiently large
j
,
\Sigma_{j}
has a decomposition
\Sigma_{j}=\cup_{i=1}^{\ell}\Gamma_{j}^{i}
into
\ell
ordered sheets

\Gamma^{1}_{j}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell}_{j}.

Denote by
W^{1}_{j}
and
W^{\ell}_{j}
the bottom and top components of
W\setminus\Sigma_{j}
. Since
W_{j}^{1}\to W^{1}
and
\Omega_{j}\to\Omega
in
\mathcal{C}(W)
, we must have
W_{j}^{1}\subset\Omega_{j}
.

Fix an open subset
W^{\prime}\subset\subset W
with
\spt(X)\subset W^{\prime}
. Since
\Gamma^{1}_{j}
converges to
\Gamma^{1}
in
C^{1,\alpha}_{loc}(U)
, we may write
\Gamma^{1}_{j}
as a
C^{1,1}
-graph
u_{j}
over
\Gamma^{1}\cap W^{\prime}
for all
j
large, and
\|u_{j}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
. For each such
j
, we can find a
C^{1,1}
-homeomorphism
\phi_{j}:W^{\prime}\to W^{\prime}
, such that
\phi_{j}(\Gamma^{1}\cap W^{\prime})=\Gamma_{j}^{1}\cap W^{\prime}
,
\phi_{j}(W^{1}\cap W^{\prime})=W_{j}^{1}\cap W^{\prime}
, and
\phi_{j}
converges to the identity map in
C^{1,\alpha}
. Note that
(\phi_{j})_{*}X
must point into
W_{j}^{1}
along
\Gamma_{j}^{1}\cap W^{\prime}
. By the strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity of
\Sigma_{j}
, we have

\int_{\Gamma_{j}^{1}}\mathrm{div}_{\Gamma_{j}^{1}}\big((\phi_{j})_{*}X\big)+h_{j}\langle(\phi_{j})_{*}X,\nu_{j}\rangle\geq 0,

where
\nu_{j}
is the unit normal of
\Gamma_{j}^{1}
pointing into
W_{j}^{1}
. Then by the
C^{1,\alpha}
-convergence
\Gamma^{1}_{j}\to\Gamma^{1}
in
W^{\prime}
, we conclude by taking
j\to\infty
that,

\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\mathrm{div}_{\Gamma^{1}}(X)+h\langle X,\nu\rangle\geq 0,

where
\nu
be the unit normal of
\Gamma^{1}
pointing into
W^{1}
. The desired inequality for
\Gamma^{\ell}
can be proved by the same argument. Hence Proposition
1.24
is proved.
∎

1.5.
Isotopy minimizing problem

In this part, we recall the regularity result for
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-isotopic minimizing problem covered in
[
36
]
*Theorem 1.1, which generalized
[
2
,
33
]
to the PMC setting.
Let
\bm{r}_{0}=\bm{r}_{0}(M,g,\sup|h|)>0
(see
[
36
]
*§14) be a sufficiently small constant and
U\subset B_{\bm{r}_{0}}(p)\subset M
be an open set. Let
\mathcal{R}\in\mathcal{C}(U)
be such that
\Sigma:=\partial\mathcal{R}\cap U
is a smoothly embedded surface.

Let
\{\phi_{k}\}\subset\mathfrak{Is}(U)
be a sequence of isotopies, such that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\phi_{k}(\Sigma,\mathcal{R})\big)=\inf\{\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\phi(\Sigma,\mathcal{R})\big):\phi\in\mathfrak{Is}(U)\}.

Then up to a subsequence, we can assume that there is a pair
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(U)
such that

(V,\Omega)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\big(\phi_{k}(\Sigma),\phi_{k}(\mathcal{R})\big)\quad\text{under the $\mathscr{F}$-metric}.

In the following of this section, we use
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
to denote
(\phi_{k}(\Sigma),\phi_{k}(\mathcal{R}))
.

Theorem 1.25
.

As above,
(V,\Omega)
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
U
.

Proof.

All the above conclusions besides strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity were already proved by
[
36
]
*Theorem 1.1. We will prove the strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity by assuming that
\Sigma\cap U
is a union of disjoint disks. Then the general case follows from the
\gamma
-reduction process in
[
36
]
*§13, which is a generalization of
[
33
]
*§3.

Fix
p\in\spt\|V\|
with
\Theta^{2}(\|V\|,p)=\ell\geq 2
. Then there exists
r=r(p)>0
, such that
V
decomposes into ordered sheets in
B_{2r}(p)

\Gamma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell},

where each
\Gamma^{i}
is a
C^{1,1}
-graph over a small disk in the tangent plane of
V
at
p
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\Omega
does not intersect the region above
\Gamma^{\ell}
in
B_{r}(p)
.

By the Replacement Lemma
[
36
]
*Lemma 8.2, one can assume that
\Sigma_{k}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{2r}(p)
consists of finitely many pairwise disjoint disks
\Gamma_{k}^{1},\cdots,\Gamma_{k}^{m_{k}}
with
\partial\Gamma_{k}^{i}\cap B_{2r}(p)=\emptyset
, and

\lim_{k\to\infty}[\Gamma_{k}^{i}]\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{r}(p)=[\Gamma^{i}]\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{r}(p),\quad\text{for }i=1,\cdots,\ell.

Now let
X\in\mathfrak{X}(B_{r}(p))
be such that
\langle X,\nu\rangle\geq 0
, where
\nu
is the upward normal to
\Gamma_{\ell}
. Denote by
\{\phi^{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathrm{Diff}_{0}\big(B_{r}(p)\big)
the flow generated by
X
. Fix
t\in[0,1]
. Then by replacing
\Gamma_{k}^{\ell}
with
\phi^{t}(\Gamma_{k}^{\ell})
, we obtain an immersed surface
\hat{\Sigma}_{k}
(which may have self-intersections since we only moved one sheet). Applying resolution of overlaps lemma
[
36
]
*Lemma 7.3 to
\hat{\Sigma}_{k}
, there exists a smoothly embedded surface
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k}
with
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k}=\partial\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}
in
U
for some
\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}\in\mathcal{C}(U)
, such that
(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})
can be obtained from
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
through some
\hat{\phi}_{k}\in\mathfrak{Is}\big(B_{r}(p)\big)
, and

\mathbf{F}([\Sigma_{k}],[\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k}])+\mathbf{M}\big(\llbracket\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}\rrbracket-\llbracket\Omega_{k}\rrbracket-T(\phi^{t}(\Gamma_{k}^{\ell}),\Gamma_{k}^{\ell})\big)<\frac{1}{k};

here
\llbracket\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}\rrbracket
,
\llbracket\Omega_{k}\rrbracket
and
T(A,B)
are 3-currents (mod 2) so that
\partial T(A,B)=\llbracket A\rrbracket-\llbracket B\rrbracket
for any two disks
A,B
with
\partial A=\partial B
. Note that
T\big(\phi^{t}(\Gamma_{k}^{\ell}),\Gamma_{k}^{\ell}\big)
converges to
T\big(\phi^{t}(\Gamma^{\ell}),\Gamma^{\ell}\big)
, and the interior of
T\big(\phi^{t}(\Gamma^{\ell}),\Gamma^{\ell}\big)
does not intersect
\Omega
(the limit of
\Omega_{k}
), since
X
points upward. Then we conclude that, as
k\to\infty
,

(1.13)

\mathcal{H}^{3}\big(\Omega_{k}\cap T(\phi^{t}(\Gamma_{k}^{\ell}),\Gamma_{k}^{\ell})\big)\to 0,\text{ and hence }\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}\to\Omega\cup T\big(\phi^{t}(\Gamma^{\ell}),\Gamma^{\ell}\big)\text{ in $\mathcal{C}(U)$}.

Observe that
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k}
is a slight perturbation of
\hat{\Sigma}_{k}=(\Sigma_{k}\setminus\Gamma_{k}^{\ell})\sqcup\phi^{t}(\Gamma_{k}^{\ell})
; this yields that

\Big|\mathcal{A}^{h}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})-\big(\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma_{k}\setminus\Gamma_{k}^{\ell})+\mathcal{H}^{2}(\phi^{t}(\Gamma_{k}^{\ell}))-\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}}h\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{3}\big)\Big|\to 0,\text{ as }k\to\infty.

This together with (
1.13
) gives

(1.14)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{A}^{h}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})=\|V\|(U)-\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma^{\ell})+\mathcal{H}^{2}(\phi^{t}(\Gamma^{\ell}))-\int_{\Omega}h\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{3}-\int_{T\big(\phi^{t}(\Gamma^{\ell}),\Gamma^{\ell}\big)}h\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{3}.

Since
(V,\Omega)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-minimizing, then

\mathcal{A}^{h}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})\geq\mathcal{A}^{h}(V,\Omega)=\|V\|(U)-\int_{\Omega}h\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{3}.

Combining with
1.14
, we conclude that

\mathcal{H}^{2}(\phi^{t}(\Gamma^{\ell}))\geq\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma^{\ell})+\int_{T\big(\phi^{t}(\Gamma^{\ell}),\Gamma^{\ell}\big)}h\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{3}.

By taking the derivative w.r.t. to
t
, this gives the desired inequality of the strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity for
\Gamma^{\ell}
. The same argument will also give the desired inequality for
\Gamma^{1}
. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.25
.
∎

2.
Min-max and tightening

In this section, we will set up the relative min-max problem for the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-functional in the space of separating surfaces in Section
2.1
. We will also establish the pull-tight process and prove the tightening theorem in Section
2.2
.

2.1.
Min-max problem

Fix a connected closed surface
\Sigma_{0}
of genus
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. A smooth embedding
\phi:\Sigma_{0}\to M
is said to be
separating
if
M\setminus\phi(\Sigma_{0})
is the disjoint union of two nonempty domains
\Omega^{1},\Omega^{2}
enjoying a common smooth boundary
\phi(\Sigma_{0})
. We will write the image as
\Sigma=\phi(\Sigma_{0})
. When we write a pair
(\Sigma,\Omega)
, where
\Omega
is an arbitrary choice of
\{\Omega^{1},\Omega^{2}\}
, we assume that
\Sigma
carries the orientation induced by the outer normal
\nu
of
\Omega
, and say that
\Omega
is bounded by
\Sigma
, or
\Sigma
bounds
\Omega
.

We denote

(2.1)

\mathscr{E}=\left\{(\Sigma,\Omega):\text{ $\Sigma$ is a separating embedding of $\Sigma_{0}$ which bounds $\Omega$}\right\},

endowed with oriented smooth topology in the usual sense, that is,
(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})
converges to
(\Sigma_{\infty},\Omega_{\infty})
if
\Sigma_{j}
converges in the smooth topology to
\Sigma_{\infty}
and
\Omega_{j}
converges to
\Omega_{\infty}
in
\mathcal{C}(M)
.

Let
X
be a finite dimensional cubical complex, and
Z\subset X
be a subcomplex. Let
\Phi_{0}:X\to\mathscr{E}
be a continuous map. We let
\Pi
be the set of all continuous maps
\Phi:X\to\mathscr{E}
which is homotopic to
\Phi_{0}
relative to
\Phi_{0}|_{Z}:Z\to\mathscr{E}
. We call such a
\Phi
an
(X,Z)
-sweepout
, or simply a
sweepout
.

Definition 2.1
.

Given
(X,Z)
and
\Phi_{0}
as above,
\Pi
is called the
(X,Z)
-homotopy class
of
\Phi_{0}
.

We can now set up the relative min-max problem for the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-functional as usual.

Definition 2.2
.

The
h
-width
of
\Pi
is defined by:

\mathbf{L}^{h}=\mathbf{L}^{h}(\Pi)=\inf_{\Phi\in\Pi}\sup_{x\in X}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi(x)\big).

Definition 2.3
.

A sequence
\{\Phi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Pi
is called a
minimizing sequence
if

\mathbf{L}^{h}(\Phi_{i}):=\sup_{x\in X}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi_{i}(x)\big)\to\mathbf{L}^{h},\text{ when }i\to\infty.

A subsequence
\{\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j}):x_{j}\in X\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is called a
min-max (sub)sequence
if

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\big)\to\mathbf{L}^{h},\text{ when }j\to\infty.

The
critical set
of a minimizing sequence
\{\Phi_{i}\}
is defined by

\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})=\left\{(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)\left|\,\begin{aligned} &\exists\text{ a min-max subsequence }\{\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\}\text{ such}\\
&\text{that }\mathscr{F}\big(\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j}),(V,\Omega)\big)\to 0\text{ as }j\to\infty\end{aligned}\right\}\right..

We have the following min-max theorem, and the proof will be given in Section
4.4
.

Theorem 2.4

(PMC Min-Max Theorem)
.

With all notions as above, suppose

(2.2)

\mathbf{L}^{h}(\Pi)>\max\left\{\max_{x\in Z}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi_{0}(x)\big),0\right\}.

Then there exist a minimizing sequence
\{\Phi_{i}\}\subset\Pi
, and
a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary
(\Sigma,\Omega)
lying in the critical set
\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})
, such that

\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma,\Omega)=\mathbf{L}^{h}(\Pi).

Remark 2.5
.

The strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity is an essential part of the regularity result. This will play a crucial role in our new multiplicity one theorem.

2.2.
Tightening

Take
L=\mathbf{L}^{h}+\sup_{M}|h(p)|\cdot\mathrm{Vol}(M)+1
. Recall that
A^{L}
and
A^{L}_{0}
are defined in (
1.4
) and(
1.5
) respectively. Fix a compact subset
B\subset\mathscr{E}\cap A^{L}
, which we usually take to be
B=\Phi_{0}(Z)
. Following the procedure in
[
51
]
*Section 4 (see also
[
54
]
*§1.2), we will describe the tightening process in four steps.

Step 1: Annular decomposition
.

Consider the concentric annuli around
A^{L}_{0}\cup B
under the
\mathscr{F}
-metric,

(2.3)

\begin{split}&A_{0}=A^{L}_{0}\cup B,\\
&A_{1}=\{(V,\Omega)\in A^{L}:\mathscr{F}\big((V,\Omega),A_{0}\big)\geq\frac{1}{2}\},\\
&A_{j}=\{(V,\Omega)\in A^{L}:\frac{1}{2^{j}}\leq\mathscr{F}\big((V,\Omega),A_{0}\big)\leq\frac{1}{2^{j-1}}\},\quad j\in\mathbb{N},\,j\geq 2.\end{split}

By a straightforward contradiction argument using the compactness of
A_{j}
, we can find some
c_{j}>0
depending only on
j
, such that for any
(V,\Omega)\in A_{j}
, there exists
\mathcal{X}_{V,\Omega}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, such that

\|\mathcal{X}_{V,\Omega}\|_{C^{1}(M)}\leq 1,\quad\delta A^{h}_{V,\Omega}(\mathcal{X}_{V,\Omega})\leq-c_{j}<0.

Step 2: A map from
A^{L}
to
\mathfrak{X}(M)
.

We will construct a map
\mathcal{X}:A^{L}\to\mathfrak{X}(M)
which is continuous under the
C^{1}
topology on
\mathfrak{X}(M)
. In this part, we will use
\mathscr{B}_{r}(V,\Omega)
to denote the open ball in
(\VC(M),\mathscr{F})
centered at
(V,\Omega)
with radius
r>0
.

As mentioned in Section
1.1
, for a fixed
\mathcal{X}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, the map
(V,\Omega)\mapsto\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{V,\Omega}(\mathcal{X})
is continuous under the
\mathscr{F}
-metric. Therefore, for any
(V,\Omega)\in A_{j}
, there exists
0<r_{V,\Omega}<\frac{1}{2^{j+1}}
, such that for any
(V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{r_{V,\Omega}}(V,\Omega)
, we have

(2.4)

\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime}}(\mathcal{X}_{V,\Omega})\leq\frac{1}{2}\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{V,\Omega}(\mathcal{X}_{V,\Omega})\leq-\frac{1}{2}c_{j}<0.

Now
\{\mathscr{B}_{r_{V,\Omega}/2}(V,\Omega):(V,\Omega)\in A_{j}\}
forms an open covering of
A_{j}
. By the compactness of
A_{j}
, we can find a finite subset
\{\mathscr{B}_{r_{j,i}}(V_{j,i},\Omega_{j,i}):(V_{j,i},\Omega_{j,i})\in A_{j},1\leq i\leq q_{j}\}
where
r_{j,i}
is the radius associated with
(V_{j,i},\Omega_{j,i})
, such that,

i)

the balls
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{j,i}
(with half radii of
\mathscr{B}_{j,i}
) covers
A_{j}
;

ii)

the balls
\mathscr{B}_{j,i}
are disjoint from
A_{k}
for
|k-j|\geq 2
, (this can be easily achieved by possibly shrinking
r_{V,\Omega}
).

Here and in the following we use
\mathscr{B}_{j,i}
,
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{j,i}
, and
\mathcal{X}_{j,i}
to denote
\mathscr{B}_{r_{j,i}}(V_{j,i},\Omega_{j,i})
,
\mathscr{B}_{r_{j,i}/2}(V_{j,i},\Omega_{j,i})
, and
\mathcal{X}_{V_{j,i},\Omega_{j,i}}
respectively.

Now we construct a partition of unity
\{\varphi_{j,i}:j\in\mathbb{N},1\leq i\leq q_{j}\}
sub-coordinate to the covering
\{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{j,i}\}
by

\varphi_{j,i}(V,\Omega)=\frac{\psi_{j,i}(V,\Omega)}{\sum\{\psi_{p,q}(V,\Omega):p\in\mathbb{N},1\leq q\leq q_{p}\}},

where
\psi_{j,i}(V,\Omega)=\mathscr{F}\big((V,\Omega),A^{L}\setminus\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{j,i}\big)
.

We define the desired map
\mathcal{X}:A^{L}\to\mathfrak{X}(M)
by

(2.5)

\mathcal{X}(V,\Omega)=\mathscr{F}\big((V,\Omega),A_{0}\big)\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N},1\leq i\leq q_{j}}\varphi_{j,i}(V,\Omega)\mathcal{X}_{j,i}.

Since in a sufficiently small neighborhood of any
(V,\Omega)\in A^{L}
, the above sum has only finitely many summands, we know

Lemma 2.6
.

The map constructed above is continuous under the
C^{1}
topology on
\mathfrak{X}(M)
. Moreover, the restriction
\mathcal{X}:A^{L}\setminus(A^{L}_{0}\cup B)\to\mathfrak{X}(M)
is continuous under the smooth topology on
\mathfrak{X}(M)
.

Step 3: A map from
A^{L}
to the space of isotopies
.

We will associate each
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
with an isotopy of
M
in a continuous manner in the same way as
[
51
]
*Section 4.3. The isotopies will be flows
\{\Phi_{V,\Omega}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}\in\Diff_{0}(M)
associated with
\mathcal{X}_{V,\Omega}
for each
(V,\Omega)
. For our purpose, we will need to specify how far (some
T_{V,\Omega}>0
) we can flow along each
\mathcal{X}_{V,\Omega}
, that is,
t\in[0,T_{V,\Omega}]
. Nevertheless, by Lemma
2.6
, the family of vector fields obtained in this way are only continuous in the
C^{1}
-topology. We will carefully smooth out these families to make them continuous in the smooth topology at the end.

Given
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
, write
(V_{t},\Omega_{t})=\Phi_{V,\Omega}(t)_{\#}(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
. We will show that the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-values
\{\mathcal{A}^{h}(V_{t},\Omega_{t})\}
can be deformed down by a fixed amount depending only on
\mathscr{F}\big((V,\Omega),A_{0}\big)
. To show this, given any
(V,\Omega)\in A_{j}
, there are only finitely many balls
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{k,i}
that contains
(V,\Omega)
by our construction, so we let
\rho_{V,\Omega}
be the smallest radii of those balls
\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{k,i}
with
(V,\Omega)\in\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{k,i}
. Since for each
j
, only balls in the collection
\{\widetilde{\mathscr{B}}_{k,i}:j-1\leq k\leq j+1,1\leq i\leq q_{k}\}
may intersect
A_{j}
nontrivially, we know that
\rho_{V,\Omega}\geq r_{j}>0
for some
r_{j}
depending only on
j
. By (
2.3
), (
2.4
) and the definition of
\mathcal{X}(V,\Omega)
in (
2.5
), we have for any
(V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})\in\mathscr{B}_{\rho_{{}_{V,\Omega}}}(V,\Omega)
that

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime}}\big(\mathcal{X}(V,\Omega)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathscr{F}\big((V,\Omega),A_{0}\big)\cdot(-\frac{1}{2})\cdot\min\{c_{j-1},c_{j},c_{j+1}\}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{2^{j+1}}\min\{c_{j-1},c_{j},c_{j+1}\}.

Therefore, we can find two continuous functions
g,\rho:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
such that
\lim_{t\to 0}g(t)=0
,
\lim_{t\to 0}\rho(t)=0
, and for any
(V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})\in A^{L}
,

(2.6)

\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime}}\big(\mathcal{X}(V,\Omega)\big)\leq-g\big(\mathscr{F}((V,\Omega),A_{0})\big),\quad\text{if }\mathscr{F}\big((V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime}),(V,\Omega)\big)\leq\rho\big(\mathscr{F}((V,\Omega),A_{0})\big).

Next, we will construct a continuous time function
T:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
, such that
T(t)\to 0
as
t\to 0
, and for any
(V,\Omega)\in A^{L}
, denoting
\gamma=\mathscr{F}\big((V,\Omega),A_{0}\big)
,

•

(V_{t},\Omega_{t})
(obtained by deformations using isotopies
\Phi_{V,\Omega}(t)
) belongs to
\mathscr{B}_{\rho(\gamma)}(V,\Omega)
for all
t\in[0,T(\gamma)]
.

To check this, for any
(V,\Omega)\in A_{j}
, denoting
\rho=\rho\big(\mathscr{F}((V,\Omega),A_{0})\big)
, there exists
T_{V,\Omega}>0
, such that
(V_{t},\Omega_{t})
belongs to
\mathscr{B}_{\rho}(V,\Omega)
for all
t\in[0,T_{V,\Omega}]
. By the compactness of
A_{j}
and the continuity
\big(t,(V,\Omega)\big)\mapsto(V_{t},\Omega_{t})
, we can choose
T_{V,\Omega}
such that
T_{V,\Omega}\geq T_{j}>0
for all
(V,\Omega)\in A_{j}
and for some
T_{j}
depending only on
j
. The desired function
T(\gamma)
can be obtained by interpolation between
T_{j}
’s.

In summary, for any
(V,\Omega)\in A^{L}\setminus A_{0}
, denoting
\gamma=\mathscr{F}\big((V,\Omega),A_{0}\big)>0
, we can define

(2.7)

\Psi_{V,\Omega}(t,\cdot)=\Phi_{V,\Omega}\big(T(\gamma)t,\cdot\big),\quad\text{ for }t\in[0,1],

and
\mathcal{L}:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
, with
\mathcal{L}(\gamma)=T(\gamma)g(\gamma)
; then
\lim_{\gamma\to 0}T(\gamma)=0
. We can deform any
(V,\Omega)\in A^{L}\setminus A_{0}
through a continuous family
\{(V_{t},\Omega_{t})=\Psi_{V,\Omega}(t)_{\#}(V,\Omega):t\in[0,1]\}\subset\mathscr{B}_{\rho(\gamma)}(V,\Omega)
, such that, by (
2.6
),

(2.8)

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}^{h}(V_{1},\Omega_{1})-\mathcal{A}^{h}(V,\Omega)&\leq\int_{0}^{T(\gamma)}\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{V_{t},\Omega_{t}}\big(\mathcal{X}(V,\Omega)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq-T(\gamma)\cdot g(\gamma)\\
&=-\mathcal{L}(\gamma)<0.\end{split}

Step 4: Smoothing out families of vector fields
.

We will use the construction above to prove the following
pull-tight
result.

Theorem 2.7

(Pull-tight)
.

Let
\Pi
be an
(X,Z)
-homotopy class generated by some continuous
\Phi_{0}:X\to\mathscr{E}
relative to
\Phi_{0}|_{Z}
. Given a minimizing sequence
\{\Phi^{*}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Pi
associated with
\mathcal{A}^{h}
, there exists another minimizing sequence
\{\Phi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Pi
, such that
\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})\subset\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}^{*}\})
and every element
(V,\Omega)\in\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})
is either
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary, or belongs to
B=\Phi_{0}(Z)\subset\mathscr{E}
.

Proof.

We can assume without loss of generality that
\mathbf{L}^{h}(\Phi^{*}_{i})\leq\mathbf{L}^{h}+1
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, so clearly we have
\Phi^{*}_{i}(x)\in A^{L}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in X
.

For each
\Phi^{*}_{i}:X\to\mathscr{E}
, we can associate it with a family of vector fields:

\mathcal{X}_{i}:X\to\mathfrak{X}(M),\text{ such that }\mathcal{X}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{X}(\Phi^{*}_{i}(x));

then this map is continuous under the
C^{1}
-topology on
\mathfrak{X}(M)
by Lemma
2.6
. Moreover, by our construction,

\mathcal{X}_{i}(x)=0,\quad\text{ for any }x\in Z.

Define
\Psi_{i}:X\to\mathfrak{Is}(M)
such that
\Psi_{i}(x)=\Psi_{\Phi^{*}_{i}(x)}
via (
2.7
) if
\Phi^{*}_{i}(x)\notin A^{L}_{0}\cup B
, and
\Psi_{i}(x)=\mathrm{Id}
if
\Phi^{*}_{i}(x)\in A^{L}_{0}\cup B
. Note that
x\mapsto\Psi_{i}(x)
is only continuous under the
C^{1}
-topology on
\mathfrak{Is}(M)
. Write
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i}(x)=\Psi_{i}(1,\Phi^{*}_{i}(x))
. Using (
2.8
), we have that

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\widetilde{\Phi}_{i}(x)\big)-\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi^{*}_{i}(x)\big)\leq-\mathcal{L}\big(\mathscr{F}(\Phi^{*}_{i}(x),A^{L}_{0}\cup B)\big).

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we can smooth out
\mathcal{X}_{i}
to some
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{i}:X\to\mathfrak{X}(M)
which is continuous under the smooth topology, and such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(x)=0
for any
x\in Z
, and
\|\mathcal{X}_{i}-\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\|_{C^{1}}\leq\frac{1}{i}
. Note that by (
1.2
),

|\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{V,\Omega}(\mathcal{X})-\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{V,\Omega}(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}})|\leq C\big(\|V\|(M)+\|\partial\Omega\|(M)\big)\cdot\|\mathcal{X}-\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\|_{C^{1}}\leq C^{\prime}\|\mathcal{X}-\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\|_{C^{1}},

for some universal constant
C,C^{\prime}>0
independent of the choice
(V,\Omega)\in A^{L}
.
Now define
\widetilde{\Psi}_{i}:X\to\mathfrak{Is}(M)
using
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
instead of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
in (
2.7
), then
x\mapsto\widetilde{\Psi}_{i}(x)
is continuous under the smooth topology on
\mathfrak{Is}(M)
, and
\widetilde{\Psi}_{i}|_{Z}\equiv\mathrm{Id}
. Writing
\Phi_{i}(x)=\widetilde{\Psi}_{i}(1,\Phi^{*}_{i}(x))
, then
\Phi_{i}
is homotopic to
\Phi^{*}_{i}
in
\mathscr{E}
relative to
\Phi_{0}|_{Z}
, so
\Phi_{i}
belongs to
\Pi
. Now by (
2.8
) we have that

(2.9)

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi_{i}(x)\big)-\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi^{*}_{i}(x)\big)\leq-\mathcal{L}\big(\mathscr{F}(\Phi^{*}_{i}(x),A^{L}_{0}\cup B)\big)+\frac{C^{\prime\prime}}{i},

for some universal
C^{\prime\prime}>0
.

Suppose that
\{\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\}
is a min-max subsequence, then
\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\big)\to\mathbf{L}^{h}
. By (
2.9
) and the fact that
\{\Phi^{*}_{i}\}
is a minimizing sequence, we know that
\{\Phi^{*}_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\}
is also a min-max subsequence. Then the left hand side of (
2.9
), when applied to the two min-max subsequences, will converge to
0
, and hence

\mathscr{F}(\Phi^{*}_{i_{j}}(x_{j}),A^{L}_{0}\cup B)\to 0,\text{ when }j\to\infty.

By the definition of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
, this implies that
\mathcal{X}_{i_{j}}(x_{j})
converges to
0
in the
C^{1}
-topology, and so is
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{j}}(x_{j})
. Hence we have
\mathscr{F}\big(\Phi^{*}_{i_{j}}(x_{j}),\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\big)\to 0
as
j\to\infty
, and this proves that
\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})\subset\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi^{*}_{i}\})
. Moreover, we also have

\mathscr{F}(\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j}),A^{L}_{0}\cup B)\to 0,\text{ when }j\to\infty.

This implies that elements in
\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})
is either
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary (lying in
A^{L}_{0}
) or belongs to
B=\Phi_{0}(Z)
.
∎

3.
Almost minimizing

In this part, we adapt the almost minimizing property to the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-functional using embedded separating surfaces; see Section
3.1
. As the main difference compared with
[
6
]
where they need successive replacements in annuli, we need the existence of chains of replacements in open subsets; see Definition
3.6
. We then prove the existence
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing pairs using a combinatorial arguments originally due to Almgren-Pitts in Section
3.2
.

3.1.
Definitions

Definition 3.1

(c.f.
[
6
]
*Definition 3.2)
.

Given
\epsilon,\delta>0
, an open set
U\subset M
, and an embedded separating surface
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathscr{E}
, we say that
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon,\delta)
-almost minimizing in
U
if there does not exist any isotopy
\psi\in\mathfrak{Is}(U)
, such that

\mathcal{A}^{h}(\psi(t,\Sigma,\Omega))\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma,\Omega)+\delta\quad\text{ for all }t\in[0,1];

\mathcal{A}^{h}(\psi(1,\Sigma,\Omega))\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma,\Omega)-\epsilon.

Definition 3.2

(
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing pairs)
.

Given an open subset
U\subset M
, a pair
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
, and a sequence of embedded separating surfaces
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{E}
.
We say that
(V,\Omega)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing w.r.t.
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}
in
U
, if there exist
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
and
\delta_{j}\to 0
, such that

•

(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\to(V,\Omega)
in the
\mathscr{F}
-metric as
j\to\infty
;

•

(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing in
U
.

Sometime we also say
(V,\Omega)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing in
U
without referring to the sequence
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}
.

We now show that
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing implies
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stable.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
be
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing in
U
, then

i)

(V,\Omega)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
U
;

ii)

(V,\Omega)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stable in
U
.

Proof.

Suppose on the contrary that
(V,\Omega)
is not
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
U
. Then there exists
X\in\mathfrak{X}(U)
with associated flow
\phi^{t}
, such that for all
t\in[0,1]
,

\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big((\phi^{t})_{\#}(V,\Omega)\big)<0.

Let

\epsilon:=\mathcal{A}^{h}(V,\Omega)-\mathcal{A}^{h}\big((\phi^{1})_{\#}(V,\Omega)\big)>0.

Observe that there exists
\eta>0
small enough such that for all
(V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})\in\mathcal{VC}(M)
,

\mathscr{F}\big((V,\Omega),(V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})\big)<\eta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big((\phi^{t})_{\#}(V^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})\big)<0,\quad\forall\,t\in[0,1].

By assumption, there exists
(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})(\in\mathscr{E})\to(V,\Omega)
which is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing in
U
with
\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j}\to 0
. Since
(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})
converges to
(V,\Omega)
, then for all sufficiently large
i
,
\mathscr{F}\big((V,\Omega),(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\big)<\eta
; this implies that

\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big((\phi^{t})_{\#}(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\big)<0,\quad\forall\,t\in[0,1].

Moreover, by taking sufficiently large
j
, we have
\epsilon_{j}<\epsilon/2
and

\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})-\mathcal{A}^{h}\big((\phi^{1})_{\#}(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\big)>\epsilon/2.

This leads to a contradiction. Hence we have proved that
(V,\Omega)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
U
.

The proof of Item (ii) follows in the same way by using the second variations. We omit the details here.
∎

Next, we introduce the notion of
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacements.

Definition 3.4
.

Given an open subset
U\subset M
and
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
, a pair
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})\in\VC(M)
is said to be an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacement
of
(V,\Omega)
in
U
if

(V^{*},\Omega^{*})=(V,\Omega)\text{ outside }\closure(U),\quad\mathcal{A}^{h}(V^{*},\Omega^{*})=\mathcal{A}^{h}(V,\Omega),\text{ and }

(V^{*},\Omega^{*})\text{ is a strongly $\mathcal{A}^{h}$-stationary and stable $C^{1,1}$ $h$-boundary in $U$}.

Definition 3.5
.

As above,
(V,\Omega)
is said to have
(weak) good replacement property in
U
if for any
p\in U
, there exists
r_{p}>0
, such that
(V,\Omega)
has an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacement
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
in any open annulus
\mathrm{An}\subset\subset\mathrm{An}(p;0,r_{p})
.

The following stronger good replacement property plays a key role in our new proof of regularity of min-max pairs without invoking unique continuation.

Definition 3.6

(Replacement chain property)
.

Let
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
and
U\subset M
be an open set.
(V,\Omega)
is said to have the
replacement chain property in
U
if the following statement holds.
For any sequence of open subsets
B_{1},\cdots,B_{k}\subset\subset U
, there exist a sequence
(V,\Omega)=(V_{0},\Omega_{0}),(V_{1},\Omega_{1}),\cdots,(V_{k},\Omega_{k})
in
\VC(M)
satisfying that

(V_{j},\Omega_{j})
is an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacement of
(V_{j-1},\Omega_{j-1})
in
B_{j}
for
j=1,\cdots,k
,

and

(V_{j},\Omega_{j})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable in
U
.

Furthermore, if there is another sequence of open subsets
B_{1},\cdots,B_{k},B_{k+1}^{\prime},\cdots,B_{\ell}^{\prime}\subset\subset U
, then the sequence of replacements
(\widetilde{V}_{j},\widetilde{\Omega}_{j})
can be chosen so that

(\widetilde{V}_{j},\widetilde{\Omega}_{j})=(V_{j},\Omega_{j})\quad\forall j=1,\cdots,k.

Remark 3.7
.

By definition, we have,

i)

if
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
satisfies the replacement chain property in
U
, and
B\subset\subset U
is open, then an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacement
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
of
(V,\Omega)
in
B
also satisfies the replacement chain property;

ii)

if
(V,\Omega)
has the replacement chain property in
U
,
(V,\Omega)
itself is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable in
U
;

iii)

the replacement chain property implies the (weak) good replacement property.

3.2.
Existence of almost minimizing pairs

In this part, we use the Almgren-Pitts type combinatorial arguments to find an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-min-max pair
(V,\Omega)
which is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing in small annuli.

Let
\Pi
be an
(X,Z)
-homotopy class of
(X,Z)
-sweepouts generated by some continuous
\Phi_{0}:X\to\mathscr{E}
relative to
\Phi_{0}|_{Z}
. Suppose that the nontriviality condition (
2.2
) holds. Let
\{\Phi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Pi
be a pull-tight minimizing sequence obtained by Theorem
2.7
. Then every
(V,\Omega)\in\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary.

Theorem 3.8

(Existence of almost minimizing pairs)
.

As above, suppose (
2.2
) holds, then there exist an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary pair
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})\in\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})
, and a min-max subsequence
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})=\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{E}
, such that
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing in small annuli w.r.t.
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}
in the following sense: for any
p\in M
, there exists
r_{am}(p)>0
, such that for any annulus
\mathrm{An}=\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)
with
0<s<r<r_{am}(p)
,
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing w.r.t. to
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}
in
\mathrm{An}
.

We will adapt the proof for the area functional by Colding-Gabai-Ketover in
[
7
]
*Appendix. To do so, we introduce some notions.

Definition 3.9
.

Given an
L\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in M
, a collection of annuli centered at
p

\mathscr{C}=\{\mathrm{An}(p;s_{1},r_{1}),\cdots,\mathrm{An}(p;s_{L},r_{L})\}

is said to be
L
-admissible
if
2r_{j+1}<s_{j}
for all
j=1,\cdots,L-1
.

We say a pair
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing in
\mathscr{C}
w.r.t. a sequence
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}\subset\mathscr{E}
, if there exists
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
and
\delta_{j}\to 0
, such that

•

(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\to(V,\Omega)
in the
\mathscr{F}
-metric as
j\to\infty
;

•

for each
j
,
(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing in at least one annulus in
\mathscr{C}
.

Assume that the parameter space
X
is a cubical subcomplex of the cell complex
I(m,k_{0})
for some
m,k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
. Here
I(m,k)=I(1,k)\otimes\cdots I(1,k)
(
m
-times), where
I(1,k)
denotes the complex on
I=[0,1]
whose
1
-cells and
0
-cells are, respectively,

[1,3^{-k}],[3^{-k},2\cdot 3^{-k}],\cdots,[1-3^{-k},1]\text{ and }[0],[3^{-k}],\cdots,[1-3^{-k}],[1].

We refer to
[
54
]
*Appendix A for a summary of notions; (see also
[
30
]
*Section 2.1).

Lemma 3.10
.

As above, there exist an integer
L=L(m)
(depending only on the dimension of the large complex
I(m,k_{0})
where
X
is embedded to), and a min-max subsequence
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})=\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{E}
converging to an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary pair
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})\in\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})
such that
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing in every
L
-admissible collection of annuli w.r.t.
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}
.

The proof is essentially the same as that of
[
7
]
*Lemma A.1, and we provide some necessary details for completeness.

Proof of Lemma
3.10
.

The lemma will follow directly from the following claim.

Claim 2
.

There exists
L=L(m)\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exist
\delta>0
, an
i>\frac{1}{\epsilon}
, and an
x\in X
with

(3.1)

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi_{i}(x)\big)\geq\mathbf{L}^{h}-\epsilon,

such that for any
L
-admissible collection
\mathscr{C}
,
\Phi_{i}(x)
is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon,\delta)
-almost minimizing in at least one annulus in
\mathscr{C}
.

We can take

(3.2)

L(m)=(3^{m})^{3^{m}}.

If by contradiction that the claim does not hold, we can find
\epsilon_{0}>0
, such that for any
\delta>0
, any
i>1/\epsilon_{0}
, and any
x\in X
satisfying (
3.1
) (with
\epsilon_{0}
in place of
\epsilon
), there exists an
L
-admissible collection
\mathscr{C}_{i,x}
, such that
\Phi_{i}(x)
is not
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon_{0},\delta)
-almost minimizing in any annulus in
\mathscr{C}_{i,x}
. Fix a
\delta_{0}\ll\epsilon_{0}
, and an
i_{0}>1/\epsilon_{0}
; we let

S_{i_{0}}=\{x\in X:\Phi_{i}(x)\text{ satisfies \eqref{eq:Phi_i(x) Ah lower bound}}\}\subset X.

For simplicity we drop the sub-index
i_{0}
for a moment. Following the same argument in
[
7
]
*Lemma A.1, we can find a finite open cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{j}\}
of
S
, where each
\mathcal{U}_{j}\subset I(m,0)=[0,1]^{m}
is open, such that

(1)

each
\mathcal{U}_{j}
can be associated with some annulus
\mathrm{An}_{j}
belonging to some
\mathscr{C}_{x}
,
x\in S
, such that there exists a smooth isotopy
\psi_{j}\in\mathfrak{Is}(\mathrm{An}_{j})
, for any
y\in X\cap\mathcal{U}_{j}
,

•

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\psi_{j}(t,\Phi(y))\big)\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi(y)\big)+2\delta_{0}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, and

•

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\psi_{j}(1,\Phi(y))\big)\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi(y)\big)-\epsilon_{0}/2
;

(2)

each
\mathcal{U}_{j}
intersects at most
d(m)
many other elements in
\{\mathcal{U}_{j}\}
;

(3)

each
\mathcal{U}_{j}
can be associated with a smooth function
\phi_{j}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{U}_{j})
,
0\leq\phi_{j}\leq 1
, and for any
x\in S
, at least one
\phi_{j}(x)=1
;

(4)

if
\phi_{j}(x)
and
\phi_{j^{\prime}}(x)
are both nonzero for some
j
and
j^{\prime}
, we have
\mathrm{An}_{j}\cap\mathrm{An}_{j^{\prime}}=\emptyset
.

We can homotopically deform
\Phi
to
\Phi_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{\Phi}
in
\mathscr{E}
successively using
\{\psi_{j}\}
up to time
\phi_{j}(x)
at each
x\in X
, such that

\Phi_{j+1}(x)=\psi_{j}\big(\phi_{j}(x),\Phi_{j}(x)\big),\quad j=1,\cdots.

If we choose
\delta_{0}<\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{8d(m)}
, then we must have (resuming the subindex
i_{0}
)

\sup_{x\in X}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\widetilde{\Phi}_{i_{0}}(x)\big)\leq\sup_{x\in X}\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi_{i_{0}}(x)\big)-\epsilon_{0}/2+2d(m)\cdot\delta_{0}<\mathbf{L}^{h},

for
i_{0}
sufficiently large, which is a contradiction.
∎

As a direct corollary of the above result and Lemma
3.3
, we have that

Corollary 3.11
.

As above, the
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary pair
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})\in\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})
satisfies

(3.3)

\displaystyle\text{Property {\bf(R)}}:

\displaystyle\text{for every $L(m)$-admissible collection $\mathscr{C}$ of annuli, $(V_{0},\Omega_{0})$ is $\mathcal{A}^{h}$-stable}

\displaystyle\text{in at least one annulus in $\mathscr{C}$.}

Proof of Theorem
3.8
.

The statement in Theorem
3.8
follows by taking further subsequences of
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}
in Lemma
3.10
the same way as in
[
7
]
*Lemma A3. We provide the details using a general version of this argument given in Appendix
D
.

By the proof of Lemma
3.10
, there exist
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
and
\delta_{j}\to 0
such that

•

(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\to(V_{0},\Omega_{0})
in the
\mathscr{F}
-metric as
j\to\infty
;

•

for each
j
,
(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing in at least one annulus in any
\mathscr{C}
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{j}
be the collection of annuli
\mathrm{An}
where
(V_{j},\Omega_{j})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing. Then for every
L
-admissible collection
\mathscr{C}
of annuli, we have that
\mathscr{C}\cap\mathcal{P}_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Clearly, if
\mathrm{An}_{1}\subset\mathrm{An}\in\mathcal{P}_{j}
, then
\mathrm{An}_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{j}
. Thus
\{\mathcal{P}_{j}\}
satisfy the requirements in Proposition
D.1
, and hence there exists a subsequence (still denoted by
\{\mathcal{P}_{j}\}
) such that for each
p\in M
, there exists
r_{am}(p)>0
such that for each
0<s<r<r_{am}(p)
,
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)\in\mathcal{P}_{j}
for all sufficiently large
j
. This gives that
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing w.r.t.
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}
in each
