\mathrm{An}\subset\mathrm{An}(p,0,r_{am}(p))
for all sufficiently large
j
. This completes the proof of Theorem
3.8
.
∎

4.
Regularity of min-max pairs

In this section, we prove the main regularity results for
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-min-max pairs. We first prove that
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary pairs with the replacement chain property are
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in Section
4.1
. We develop a novel way of using chains of replacements to prove the regularity without invoking unique continuation. We then construct the replacements using the aforementioned regularity results in Section
4.2
, and prove the interior regularity in Section
4.3
and the full regularity in Section
4.4
.

4.1.
Initial regularity

We start with the following characterization of tangent varifolds for an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary pair satisfying the weak good replacement property.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
be
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in an open subset
U\subset M
. If
(V,\Omega)
has (weak) good replacement property in
U
, then
V
is integer rectifiable in
U
. In fact, for any
p\in\spt\|V\|\cap U
, every tangent varifold of
V
at
p
is an integer multiple of a plane in
T_{p}M
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as
[
6
]
*Lemma 6.4 and
[
36
]
*Lemma 20.2.
∎

The following lemma says that an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary,
C^{1}

h
-boundary
1
1

1

Note that we can define
C^{1}
-boundaries the same way as in Definition
1.10
.
that is strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and
C^{1,1}
outside a
C^{1}
interface is strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and
C^{1,1}
in the whole region. This result will be used to glue two of strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary
C^{1,1}

h
-boundaries that match in the
C^{1}
-manner along an interface.

Lemma 4.2
.

Given an open subset
W\subset M
, let
(\Sigma,\Omega)
be an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary
C^{1}
-boundary in
W
. Suppose that
\Sigma
decomposes into
C^{1}
-ordered sheets:

\Gamma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell}.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a
C^{1}
-embedded surface in
W
with
\partial\mathcal{T}\cap W=\emptyset
, which intersects transversely with
\Gamma^{1},\cdots,\Gamma^{\ell}
. Suppose in addition that
\Sigma\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}(W\setminus\mathcal{T})
is
C^{1,1}
and
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
W\setminus\mathcal{T}
. Then
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
W
.

Proof.

We first show that each sheet has bounded first variation in
W
, and hence has improved regularity. Let
\gamma^{i}:=\Gamma^{i}\cap\mathcal{T}
, which is a
C^{1}
curve by transversality for each
i
. Denote the two components of
\Gamma^{i}\setminus\gamma^{i}
as
\Gamma^{i}_{1}
and
\Gamma^{i}_{2}
and the exterior unit co-normal along
\gamma^{1}
by
\bm{\eta}^{i}_{1}
and
\bm{\eta}^{i}_{2}
respectively. Since
\Sigma^{i}
is
C^{1}
, we know that

\bm{\eta}^{i}_{1}=-\bm{\eta}^{i}_{2}\quad\text{along }\gamma^{i}.

Since
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
W\setminus\mathcal{T}
, the generalized mean curvature
H^{i}
of
\Gamma^{i}
(w.r.t. the unit outer normal
\nu
given in Lemma
1.11
) exists
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e. in
\Gamma^{i}\setminus\gamma^{i}
, and by Corollary
1.19
, is bounded
|H^{i}(x)|\leq|h(x)|
for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e.
x\in\Gamma^{i}\setminus\gamma^{i}
.

For any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(W)
, and any
1\leq i\leq\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma^{i}}\mathrm{div}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma^{i}_{1}}\mathrm{div}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}+\int_{\Gamma^{i}_{2}}\mathrm{div}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma^{i}_{1}}H^{i}\cdot\langle X,\nu\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}+\int_{\gamma^{i}}\langle X,\bm{\eta}^{i}_{1}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{\Gamma^{i}_{2}}H^{i}\cdot\langle X,\nu\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}+\int_{\gamma^{i}}\langle X,\bm{\eta}^{i}_{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma^{i}}H^{i}\cdot\langle X,\nu\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}.

Note that we used the divergence theorem for Lipschitz vector fields on a
C^{1,1}
-surface with
C^{1}
-boundary. Hence
\Gamma^{i}
has bounded first variation, and by the Allard regularity theorem
[
1
]
, (see also
[
41
]
*Theorem 24.2),
\Gamma^{i}
is
C^{1,\alpha}
. Moreover, by Proposition
A.1
, the
C^{1,1}
-regularity for multilayer
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary boundaries, we know that
\Gamma^{i}
is also in
C^{1,1}
.

We next prove strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity by a cutoff trick. We only need to check the Definition
1.15
near any points
p\in\Sigma\cap\mathcal{T}
. Assume without loss of generality that
\Theta^{2}(\Sigma,p)=\ell
, that is, all the
\ell
sheets touch together at
p
. We will prove the desired inequality for
\Gamma^{1}
and that for
\Gamma^{\ell}
follows in a similar manner. Assume that
0\leq\eta_{k}\leq 1
is a sequence of cutoff functions, such that
\spt(\eta_{k})\subset W\setminus\mathcal{T}
and
\eta_{k}(x)\to 1
as
k\to\infty
for any
x\in W\setminus\Gamma
. Denote by
W^{1}
the bottom component of
W\setminus\Sigma
. Given any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(W)
supported in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
p
, such that
X
points toward
W^{1}
along
\Gamma^{1}
, then
X
is a legitimate test vector field in Definition
1.15
. Since
\Gamma^{1}
is a
C^{1,1}
-surface, we have by the divergence theorem,

\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\mathrm{div}X=\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\mathrm{div}X^{\perp}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\eta_{k}\mathrm{div}X^{\perp}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\mathrm{div}(\eta_{k}X^{\perp})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\mathrm{div}(\eta_{k}X).

Then we have the following two cases.

•

If
W^{1}\subset\Omega
, then we have

\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\mathrm{div}X=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\mathrm{div}(\eta_{k}X)\geq\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\eta_{k}\langle X,\nu\rangle\cdot h=\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\langle X,\nu\rangle\cdot h.

•

If
W^{1}\cap\Omega=\emptyset
, then we have

\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\mathrm{div}X=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\mathrm{div}(\eta_{k}X)\geq\lim_{k\to\infty}-\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\eta_{k}\langle X,\nu\rangle\cdot h=-\int_{\Gamma^{1}}\langle X,\nu\rangle\cdot h.

Here we use the fact that
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
W\setminus\mathcal{T}
in the “
\geq
” above. Therefore, we have checked the requirement in Definition
1.15
for
\Gamma^{1}
and finished the proof.
∎

In the next lemma, we will show that a replacement
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
will glue nicely with the original pair
(V,\Omega)
along regular part under natural assumptions.

Lemma 4.3

(Gluing Lemma)
.

Suppose
(V,\Omega)\in\mathcal{VC}(M)
satisfies the replacement chain property in an open set
U\subset M
. Assume further that
(V,\Omega)
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in an open subset
W\subset U
. Let
B\subset\subset U
be an open geodesic ball such that

1)

H_{\partial B}>\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}
, and

2)

\partial B
intersects with
\spt\|V\|
transversely in
W
.

Let
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
be an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacement of
(V,\Omega)
in
B
(which also satisfies the replacement chain property in
U
by Remark
3.7
). Then
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
W\cup B
.

Proof.

By assumption,
V\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}W
and
V^{*}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B
are induced by
C^{1,1}
-almost embedded surfaces
\Sigma\subset W
and
\Gamma\subset B
respectively;
(\Sigma,\Omega)
and
(\Gamma,\Omega^{*})
form strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
W
and
B
respectively. By Remark
3.7
, both
(V,\Omega)
and
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
are
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable in
U
, and Proposition
4.1
applies to both
V
and
V^{*}
.

We divide the proof into the following four steps.

Step 1:
\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap\partial B\subset\spt\|V\|\cap\partial B
, and
\Sigma\cap\partial B=[\closure(\Gamma)\setminus B]\cap W
.

The first part is a standard application of the Maximum Principle. Suppose on the contrary that
x\in\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap\partial B
, but
x\notin\spt\|V\|\cap\partial B
. Since
\spt\|V\|=\spt\|V^{*}\|
outside
\closure(B)
, this implies that in a sufficiently small neighborhood
\widetilde{W}
of
x
,
\spt\|V^{*}\|
is contained in
\widetilde{W}\cap\closure(B)
. The
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary condition implies that
V^{*}\scalebox{1.8}{$\llcorner$}\widetilde{W}
has
\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}
-bounded first variation in
\widetilde{W}
, then the Maximum Principle (c.f.
[
51
]
*Proposition 2.13,
[
49
]
*Theorem 5) and the mean curvature assumption
1)
of
\partial B
imply that
\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap\widetilde{W}\cap\partial B=\emptyset
, which contradicts the choice of
x
.

To show the second part, since
\Sigma\cap\partial B=\spt\|V\|\cap W\cap\partial B
and
\Gamma=\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap B
, we only need to show
\Sigma\cap\partial B\subset\closure(\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap B)\setminus B
. Given any
x\in\Sigma\cap\partial B
, we know by Proposition
4.1
that each tangent varifold of
V^{*}
at
x
is an integer multiple of a plane
P
. As
V
and
V^{*}
are identical outside
\closure(B)
,
P
contains half of the tangent plane
T_{x}\Sigma
, so we must have
P=T_{x}\Sigma
. By the assumption,
P
intersects
\partial B
transversely, so
x
must be a limit point of
\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap B
, and hence
x\in\closure(\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap B)\setminus B
.
\Box

Step 2: Let
x_{i}\in\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap U
be a sequence of points with
x_{i}\to x\in U
and
r_{i}\to 0
. Denote by
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x,r}:\mathbb{R}^{L}\to\mathbb{R}^{L}
the dilation map
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x,r}(y)=\frac{y-x}{r}
. Then the blow up limit
\overline{V}=\lim(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i})_{\#}V^{*}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}
denotes
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x_{i},r_{i}}
, is induced by an embedded minimal surface in
T_{x}M
.
(Note that this conclusion depends only on the replacement chain property. We refer to a similar result for CMC min-max varifold in
[
51
]
*Lemma 5.10.)

Clearly
\overline{V}
must be stationary in
T_{x}M
. We will prove that the blow up limit has replacement chain property in any fixed bounded open subset
W\subset T_{x}M
for the area functional.
2
2

2

Note that Definition
3.6
can be straightforwardly adapted to the area functional.
Then the desired regularity result follows from that of min-max varifolds
[
6
]
*Proposition 6.3.
3
3

3

Note that our replacement chain property is stronger than the good replacement property
[
6
]
*Definition 6.2.

We start by showing this for a single open subset. Since
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}(M)\to T_{x}M
locally uniformly, we can identify
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}(M)
with
T_{x}M
on compact subsets for
i
large. For any open subset
\mathcal{B}\subset W\subset T_{x}M
, denote
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{i}:=\bm{\mu}_{i}^{-1}(\mathcal{B})\subset U
. Then for sufficiently large
i
, there is an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacement
(V_{i},\Omega_{i})
of
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
in
\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime}
. Up to a subsequence we have

\widetilde{V}=\lim_{i\to\infty}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i})_{\#}V_{i}\quad\text{ in }\mathcal{V}(T_{x}M).

We can deduce the following:

•

\widetilde{V}
and
\overline{V}
are identical outside
\closure(\mathcal{B})
.

•

\mathcal{A}^{h}(V_{i},\Omega_{i})=\mathcal{A}^{h}(V^{*},\Omega^{*})

\Longrightarrow

\|\widetilde{V}\|(W)=\|\overline{V}\|(W)
.

•

By the replacement chain property,
(V_{i},\Omega_{i})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable in
U
, hence
\widetilde{V}
is stationary and stable in
T_{x}M
.

•

Note that
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i})_{\#}(V_{i}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\mathcal{B}}_{i}^{\prime})
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{r_{i}h}
-stationary, stable
C^{1,1}

(r_{i}h)
-boundary in
{\mathcal{B}}
. Also the mass of
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i})_{\#}(V_{i}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\mathcal{B}}_{i}^{\prime})
is uniformly bounded by the monotonicity formula. Thus Proposition
1.24
implies that
\widetilde{V}
is induced by a stable embedded minimal surface in
\mathcal{B}
.

Thus,
\widetilde{V}
is a replacement of
\overline{V}
in
\mathcal{B}
for the area functional.

When there is a list of open sets
\mathcal{B}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{B}_{a}\subset T_{x}M
, we can take a chain of replacements
(V_{i,k},\Omega_{i,k})
successively in
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i})^{-1}(\mathcal{B}_{k})
. By similar arguments as above, we can show that the weak limits
\{\widetilde{V}_{k}:=\lim_{i\to\infty}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i})_{\#}V_{i,k}\}
is a chain of replacements for
\overline{V}
. This completes the proof of Step 2.
\Box

Denote by

\gamma:=\Sigma\cap\partial B.

Then
\gamma
is a
C^{1,1}
-curve by transversality. Now let us fix an arbitrary point
p\in\gamma
.

Step 3: Suppose that
x_{i}\in\Gamma
and
x_{i}\to p
. Then

\lim_{i\to\infty}|\langle\nu_{\Gamma}(x_{i}),\nu_{\Sigma}(p)\rangle|=1,

where
\nu_{\Sigma}
and
\nu_{\Gamma}
are respectively the unit normal of
\Sigma
and
\Gamma
(which are both
C^{1,1}
surfaces).

Take
z_{i}\in\gamma
such that

r_{i}:=\operatorname{dist}_{M}(x_{i},\gamma)=\operatorname{dist}_{M}(x_{i},z_{i}).

Note that
V^{*}
has a unique tangent plane in a neighborhood of
p
. To check this, by the regularity assumption of
V^{*}
, we only need to check the tangent plane of
V^{*}
at any
x\in\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap\partial B\subset\Sigma
near
p
. Indeed, any tangent plane
P
of
V^{*}
at
x
must contain half of
T_{x}\Sigma
, and hence
P=T_{x}\Sigma
. Thus the unit normal vector
\nu^{*}
of
V^{*}
is well-defined. Clearly,
\nu^{*}(z_{i})=\pm\nu_{\Sigma}(z_{i})
, where the sign depends on the choice of the orientation. Since
x_{i}\to p
, we have
z_{i}\to p
and
\nu^{*}(z_{i})=\pm\nu_{\Sigma}(z_{i})\to\pm\nu_{\Sigma}(p)=\pm\nu^{*}(p)
. By Step 2,
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x_{i},r_{i}})_{\#}V^{*}
converges to an embedded minimal surface
Q
in
T_{p}M
. Moreover, by the regularity of
\Sigma
, we know that
Q
contains only several half-planes parallel to
T_{p}\Sigma
in a half-space of
T_{p}M
(one side of
\lim_{i\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x_{i},r_{i}}(\partial B)
); (we refer to
[
51
]
*Claim 3(B) on page 477 for more details of the converging scenario for
\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x_{i},r_{i}}(\Sigma)\}
.) Thus by the half space theorem in
[
15
]
*Theorem 1,
Q
consists of a union of parallel planes counted with integer multiplicity. Moreover, since
\Gamma
is a stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
B
, the convergence
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{x_{i},r_{i}})_{\#}V^{*}\to Q
is
C^{1,\alpha}
in
B_{1/2}(0)
. Thus
\nu_{\Gamma}(x_{i})
converges to
\nu_{Q}(=\nu_{\Sigma}(p))
, which is the unit normal of
Q
. Hence we have finished Step 3.
\Box

Step 4: Graphical decomposition of
\Gamma
around
p
.

Take a geodesic ball
B_{r}(p)\subset\subset W
. We can assume that the conclusion in Step 3 holds for any
q\in\gamma\cap B_{r}(p)
. We will show that
V^{*}
has
C^{1}
-graphical decomposition in a smaller neighborhood of
p
. By the
C^{1,1}
-regularity of
\Sigma
and possibly shrinking
r
, we may assume that
\Sigma\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{10r}(p)
has an ordered decomposition

\Sigma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Sigma^{\ell}.

We start by introducing a family of cylindrical neighborhoods of
p
. We can identify
B_{10r}(p)
with the corresponding ball in
T_{p}M
. Denote by
P=T_{p}\Sigma
the tangent plane,
\bm{\pi}
the projection to
P
, and
\mathcal{B}_{r}(p):=B_{r}(p)\cap P
. Given
s>0
, denote

\mathcal{K}_{r,s}(p):=\{x\in\bm{\pi}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}_{r}(p));\operatorname{dist}_{M}(x,P)\leq sr\}.

Fix a constant
\delta>0
small enough. Since
\Sigma
is
C^{1,1}
near
p
, by taking small enough
r
,
\Sigma
will be sufficiently flat in the sense that:
\Sigma\cap\mathcal{K}_{r,3\delta}(p)=\Sigma\cap\mathcal{K}_{r,\delta}(p)
. Since the tangent varifold of
V^{*}
at
p
is the same as
T_{p}\Sigma
, we can also assume that

\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap\mathcal{K}_{r,3\delta}(p)=\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap\mathcal{K}_{r,\delta}(p).

By the argument in Step 3, we have for each
x\in\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap\mathcal{K}_{r,\delta}(p)
, the tangent plane of
\|V^{*}\|
at
x
is unique and the unit normal
\nu^{*}
satisfies

(4.1)

|\langle\nu^{*}(x),\nu(p)\rangle|>1-\epsilon.

Here
\epsilon\to 0
as
r\to 0
by Step 3.

Next we use the argument in
[
47
]
*Theorem 3.2 (see also
[
23
]
*Lemma 2.1) to construct the desired graphical decomposition. We claim that
\bm{\pi}
maps
\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap\mathcal{K}_{\delta,r}(p)
onto
\mathcal{B}_{r}(p)
. Suppose not, then there exist
q\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(p)
,
t>0
and
y\in\mathcal{K}_{r,\delta}(p)\cap\spt\|V^{*}\|
such that

\bm{\pi}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{B}_{t}(q)\big)\cap\mathcal{K}_{r,\delta}(p)\cap\spt\|V^{*}\|=\emptyset,\quad\text{ and }\bm{\pi}(y)\in\partial\mathcal{B}_{t}(q).

Thus, the tangent plane of
V^{*}
at
y
must be parallel to that of the vertical cylinder
\bm{\pi}^{-1}(\partial\mathcal{B}_{t}(q))
, which contradicts (
4.1
).

Next we define the graph functions over
\mathcal{B}_{r}(p)
inductively. Let
d^{s}
be the signed distance function to
\mathcal{B}_{r}(p)
. Define

u_{1}(x):=\inf\{d^{s}(z):\,z\in\bm{\pi}^{-1}(\{x\})\cap\spt\|V^{*}\|\}.

Then by the previous paragraph,
u_{1}
is well-defined for all
x\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(p)
. Moreover, by Step 1 and 3,
u_{1}
is a
C^{1}
-function. Denote by
\Gamma^{1}
the graph of
u_{1}
. Then
\widetilde{V}^{*}:=V^{*}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}\mathcal{K}_{r,\delta}(p)-[\Gamma^{1}]
is still an integer rectifiable varifold so that the tangent varifold of
y\in\spt\|\widetilde{V}^{*}\|
is a unique plane which satisfies (
4.1
). Then we define
u_{j}
and
V^{*}(j)
inductively as follows:

\displaystyle V^{*}(j):=V^{*}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}\mathcal{K}_{r,\delta}(p)-\sum_{i\leq j}[\Gamma^{i}],\quad\text{ where }\Gamma^{i}:=[\mathrm{graph}\,u_{i}];

\displaystyle u_{j+1}(x):=\inf\left\{d^{s}(z):\,z\in\bm{\pi}^{-1}(\{x\})\cap\spt\|V^{*}(j)\|\right\}.

By the same argument for
u_{1}
, one can prove that if
\spt\|V^{*}(j)\|
is non-empty, then
u_{j+1}
is well-defined for all
x\in\mathcal{B}_{r}(p)
. By the decomposition of
\Sigma
in
B_{10r}(p)
, we conclude that
V^{*}
consists of exactly
\ell
number of
C^{1}
-graphs in
\mathcal{K}_{r,\delta}(p)
:

\Gamma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell}.

This finishes Step 4.
\Box

So far, we have proved that
V^{*}
is induced by a union of ordered
C^{1}
-surfaces near
p
, and hence
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{r}(p)
is an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary
C^{1}
-boundary. Moreover, by assumption,
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}(B_{r}(p)\setminus\partial B)
is
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary. Thus by Lemma
4.2
,
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{r}(p)
is
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary. Since the strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity is a local notion, this implies that
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}(W\cup B)
is
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary. This completes the proof of Lemma
4.3
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the first main regularity result. We use the existence of chains of replacements in a totally new way, as compared with Pitts
[
34
]
*Chap. 7.

Theorem 4.4

(First Regularity)
.

Given an open set
U\subset M
, let
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
satisfy the replacement chain property in
U
(see Definition
3.6
). Then
(V,\Omega)
is induced by a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
U
.

Proof.

By the replacement chain property, we know that
(V,\Omega)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable in
U
, so we only need to prove the
C^{1,1}
-regularity and strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity.

By Proposition
4.1
, we know that
\Theta^{2}(\|V\|,x)\in\mathbb{N}
for any
x\in\spt\|V\|\cap U
. We will prove the desired regularity by doing induction on
\Theta^{2}(\|V\|,x)
. Fix an integer
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Define

\mathcal{S}(V,m)=\left\{x\in\spt\|V\|\cap U:\Theta^{2}(\|V\|,x)=m\right\},

\mathcal{S}(V,\leq m)=\cup_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{S}(V,i),

and

\mathcal{S}(V,>m)=\spt\|V\|\cap U\setminus\mathcal{S}(V,\leq m).

By the upper semi-continuity of the density function
\Theta^{2}(\|V\|,\cdot)
(which holds as
V
has uniformly bounded first variation via Lemma
1.5
), we know that for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathcal{S}(V,\leq m)/\mathcal{S}(V,>m)
is a relatively open/closed subset of
\spt\|V\|\cap U
.

By the Allard regularity, standard elliptic regularity, and Lemma
1.14
, we know that
\mathcal{S}(V,1)
constitutes a smoothly embedded surface
\Sigma_{1}
, whose mean curvature w.r.t. the outer normal of
\Omega
is prescribed by
h
.

Suppose by induction that
V\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}\mathcal{S}(V,\leq m-1)
is induced by a
C^{1,1}
-almost embedded surface
\Sigma_{m-1}
, and
(\Sigma_{m-1},\Omega)
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary, stable,
h
-boundary in
U\setminus\mathcal{S}(V,>m-1)
for some
m\geq 2
. We will prove the same regularity for
V\scalebox{1.8}{$\llcorner$}\mathcal{S}(V,\leq m)
so as to finish the induction.

Now we fix
q\in\mathcal{S}(V,m)
and a sufficiently small constant
\delta>0
. By the monotonicity formula and the fact that
\mathcal{S}(V,\leq m)
is relatively open in
\spt\|V\|
, we can find

•

s_{0}>0
, and

•

a geodesic ball with sufficiently small radius
U_{0}\subset U
of
q
with
\spt\|V\|\cap\closure(U_{0})\subset\mathcal{S}(V,\leq m)
(note that this implies
U_{0}\setminus\mathcal{S}(V,m)
is an open subset of
U_{0}
),

such that for all
x\in\spt\|V\|\cap\closure(U_{0})
and
s\leq s_{0}
,

(4.2)

\frac{\|V\|(B_{s}(x))}{\pi s^{2}}\leq m+\delta.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that

\closure(U_{0})\subset\cap_{x\in U_{0}}B_{s_{0}/2}(x).

Now fix
\bm{r}\in(0,s_{0}/4)
small enough (we will let
\bm{r}\to 0
in the end), and let
B_{1},\cdots,B_{N}
be finitely many geodesic balls centered on
\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\closure(U_{0})
with radius
\bm{r}
in
M
, so that

\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\closure(U_{0})\subset\cup_{i=1}^{N}B_{i},\quad\text{and}\quad B_{i}\cap\big(\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\closure(U_{0})\big)\neq\emptyset.

We can also assume that the mean curvature
H_{\partial B_{i}}>\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}
.

By slightly enlarging
B_{1}
to
\widetilde{B}_{1}
, we may assume that
\partial\widetilde{B}_{1}
is transverse to
\Sigma_{m-1}
(if
\partial\widetilde{B}_{1}\cap\Sigma_{m-1}\neq\emptyset
). Let
(V_{1},\Omega_{1})
be an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacement of
(V,\Omega)
in
\widetilde{B}_{1}
. Then
(V_{1},\Omega_{1})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}\widetilde{B}_{1}
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
\widetilde{B}_{1}
. Furthermore, by Remark
3.7
,
(V_{1},\Omega_{1})
also satisfies the replacement chain property in
U
, so
(V_{1},\Omega_{1})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable in
U
, and hence
V_{1}
is integer rectifiable in
U
by Proposition
4.1
. Moreover, by Lemma
4.3
, it is also
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
\widetilde{B}_{1}\cup[U_{0}\setminus\mathcal{S}(V,m)]
.

Next we construct a sequence
(V_{2},\Omega_{2}),\cdots,(V_{N},\Omega_{N})\in\VC(M)
and a sequence of balls
\widetilde{B}_{2},\cdots,\widetilde{B}_{N}
inductively, so that for
j=2,\cdots,N
,

•

\widetilde{B}_{j}\supset B_{j}
is a ball with radius slightly larger than
\bm{r}
;

•

(V_{j},\Omega_{j})
is an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacement of
(V_{j-1},\Omega_{j-1})
in
\widetilde{B}_{j}
;

•

(V_{j},\Omega_{j})
satisfies the replacement chain property in
U
and hence is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary, stable and integer rectifiable in
U
;

•

(V_{j},\Omega_{j})
is
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in

\widetilde{B}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\widetilde{B}_{j}\cup[U_{0}\setminus\mathcal{S}(V,m)].

In fact, assume that we have finished the construction at the
(j-1)
-th step, we can take a slightly larger ball
\widetilde{B}_{j}\supset B_{j}
so that
\partial\widetilde{B}_{j}
is transverse to
\spt\|V_{j-1}\|
in
\widetilde{B}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\widetilde{B}_{j-1}\cup[U_{0}\setminus\mathcal{S}(V,m)]
. By the replacement chain property of
(V_{j-1},\Omega_{j-1})
, we can always find an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacement
(V_{j},\Omega_{j})
of
(V_{j-1},\Omega_{j-1})
in
\widetilde{B}_{j}
. Then by Remark
3.7
and Lemma
4.3
,
(V_{j},\Omega_{j})
is
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in

\widetilde{B}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\widetilde{B}_{j}\cup[U_{0}\setminus\mathcal{S}(V,m)]
, so that we can continue the induction process.

Since
U_{0}\subset\widetilde{B}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\widetilde{B}_{N}\cup[U_{0}\setminus\mathcal{S}(V,m)]
, we know that
(V_{N},\Omega_{N})
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary, stable,
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
U_{0}
, and

(4.3)

\mathcal{A}^{h}(V,\Omega)=\mathcal{A}^{h}(V_{1},\Omega_{1})=\cdots=\mathcal{A}^{h}(V_{N},\Omega_{N}).

So far, for each
\bm{r}>0
, we can cover the
\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\mathrm{Clos}(U_{0})
by finitely many small balls with radius
\leq 2\bm{r}
and then use the replacement chain property to get a pair in
\VC(M)
, denoted by
(V_{\bm{r}},\Omega_{\bm{r}})
, which is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary, stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
U_{0}
. Note that
(V_{\bm{r}},\Omega_{\bm{r}})
is still
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and integer rectifiable in
U
, and

(V_{\bm{r}},\Omega_{\bm{r}})=(V,\Omega)\text{ outside }\closure(\mathscr{B}_{\bm{r}}),\text{ where }\mathscr{B}_{\bm{r}}=\cup_{j=1}^{N}\widetilde{B}_{j}.

By the standard compactness theory for integral varifolds with uniformly bounded first variation and for sets of finite perimeter, we have (up to a subsequence)

(V_{\bm{r}},\Omega_{\bm{r}})\to(V^{*},\Omega^{*})\text{ under the $\mathscr{F}$-metric},

where
V^{*}
is integer rectifiable in
U
. Moreover, by Proposition
1.24
,
(V_{\bm{r}},\Omega_{\bm{r}})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}U_{0}
converges to a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary, stable,
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary
\Sigma^{*}\subset U_{0}
(associated with
\Omega^{*}
) in the sense of
C^{1,\alpha}_{loc}
, that is,
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}U_{0}=(\Sigma^{*},\Omega^{*}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}U_{0})
. Observe that
\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\closure(U_{0})
is a compact and
2
-rectifiable subset of
M
,
and for each
\bm{r}>0
and for any
x\in\widetilde{B}_{j}
, we have
\operatorname{dist}_{M}(x,\mathcal{S}(V,m))\leq 2\bm{r}
. It follows that

\mathscr{B}_{\bm{r}}\to\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\closure(U_{0}),\quad\text{ as }\bm{r}\to 0.

Hence we have that

(4.4)

\spt\|V^{*}\|\subset\spt\|V\|,\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega^{*}=\lim_{r\to 0}\Omega_{r}=\Omega.

Moreover,

(4.5)

V^{*}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}\big(U\setminus[\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\closure(U_{0})]\big)=V\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}\big(U\setminus[\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\closure(U_{0})]\big).

Note that (
4.3
) gives

\mathcal{A}^{h}(V^{*},\Omega^{*})=\mathcal{A}^{h}(V,\Omega).

Together with (
4.4
) and (
4.5
), we obtain

(4.6)

\|V^{*}\|\big(\mathrm{Clos}(U_{0})\big)=\|V\|\big(\mathrm{Clos}(U_{0})\big).

Since
\closure(U_{0})\subset B_{s_{0}}(y)
for all
y\in\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\closure(U_{0})
, by (
4.2
), (
4.5
) and (
4.6
), we have

\frac{\|V^{*}\|(B_{s_{0}}(y))}{\pi s_{0}^{2}}=\frac{\|V\|(B_{s_{0}}(y))}{\pi s_{0}^{2}}<m+\delta.

By the monotonicity formula and the fact that
\Theta^{2}(\|V^{*}\|,y)\in\mathbb{N}
for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e.
y\in\spt\|V^{*}\|\cap U
, we have that
\Theta^{2}(\|V^{*}\|,y)\leq m
for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e.
y\in\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\closure(U_{0})
. Recall that for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e.
y\in\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\closure(U_{0})
,
\Theta^{2}(\|V\|,y)=m
.
Thus we obtain

\Theta^{2}(\|V^{*}\|,y)\leq\Theta^{2}(\|V\|,y),\quad\text{for $\mathcal{H}^{2}$-a.e. }y\in\mathcal{S}(V,m)\cap\mathrm{Clos}(U_{0}).

Together with (
4.4
), (
4.5
) and (
4.6
), we conclude that
V^{*}=V
. This gives the desired regularity of
V
near
q
. By the arbitrariness of
q
, we proved the desired regularity of
\mathcal{S}(V,\leq m)
. This completes the induction and the theorem is proved.
∎

4.2.
Construction of replacements

Given an embedded separating surface
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathscr{E}
, an open set
U\subset M
and
\delta>0
, set

\mathfrak{Is}^{h}_{\delta}(U)=\{\psi\in\mathfrak{Is}(U);\ \mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\psi(t,\Sigma,\Omega)\big)\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma,\Omega)+\delta,\ \forall\,t\in[0,1]\}

and

m_{\delta}:=\inf\{\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\psi(1,\Sigma,\Omega)\big);\psi\in\mathfrak{Is}^{h}_{\delta}(U)\}.

We say that a sequence
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{E}
is
minimizing in Problem
(\Sigma,\Omega,\mathfrak{Is}_{\delta}^{h}(U))
if there exists a sequence
\{\psi_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{Is}_{\delta}^{h}(U)
with

(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})=\psi_{k}(1,\Sigma,\Omega),\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma,\Omega)\geq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\to m_{\delta},\text{ as }k\to\infty.

We will use the following lemma, which says that any isotopy in a sufficiently small ball that does not increase
\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
can be realized by an isotopy in
\mathfrak{Is}^{h}_{\delta}(U)
.
The proof will be given in Appendix
B
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose that
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is minimizing in Problem
(\Sigma,\Omega,\mathfrak{Is}^{h}_{\delta}(U))
. Then given
U^{\prime}\subset\subset U
, there exists
\rho_{0}>0
such that for
k
sufficiently large, the following holds: for any
B_{2\rho}(x)\subset U^{\prime}
(
\rho<\rho_{0}
) and
\varphi\in\mathfrak{Is}(B_{\rho}(x))
with
\mathcal{A}^{h}(\varphi(1,\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k}))\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
, there
exists an isotopy
\Phi\in\mathfrak{Is}(B_{2\rho}(x))
such that

\displaystyle\Phi(1,\cdot)=\varphi(1,\cdot),\ \text{ and }\ \mathcal{A}^{h}(\Phi(t,\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})+\delta,\ \text{for all }\,0\leq t\leq 1.

Moreover, the constant
\rho_{0}
depends on
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma)
,
\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}
,
U^{\prime}
,
M
and
\delta
, but does not depend on the minimizing sequence
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\}
.

Now we will use the First Regularity Theorem
4.4
to prove the regularity for constrained
h
-minimizing problems. The key step is to verify the replacement chain property where we need Lemma
4.5
.

Proposition 4.6

(Regularity of constrained
h
-minimizer)
.

Assume that
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\mathscr{E}
is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon,\delta)
-almost minimizing in
U
. Suppose that
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\}
is minimizing in Problem
(\Sigma,\Omega,\mathfrak{Is}_{\delta}^{h}(U))
. Then
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
converges (subsequentially without relabeling) to some
(\hat{V},\hat{\Omega})\in\VC(M)
with

(4.7)

\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma,\Omega)-\epsilon\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\hat{V},\hat{\Omega})\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma,\Omega);

and moreover,
(\hat{V},\hat{\Omega})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}U
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
U
.

Proof.

Clearly (
4.7
) follows from the definition of
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon,\delta)
-almost minimizing property. Since
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\}
is minimizing in Problem
(\Sigma,\Omega,\mathfrak{Is}_{\delta}^{h}(U))
, we conclude that
(\hat{V},\hat{\Omega})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable in
U
. The proof is similar to that in Lemma
3.3
.

We next prove the regularity. Fix
p\in U
. Denote by

r_{1}:=\operatorname{dist}_{M}(p,\partial U).

Let
\rho_{0}
be the constant in Lemma
4.5
for the Problem
(\Sigma,\Omega,\mathfrak{Is}_{\delta}^{h}(U))
and
U^{\prime}=B_{r_{1}/4}(p)
, and

r_{0}:=\min\{\rho_{0},r_{1}/4\}.

We will prove that
(\hat{V},\hat{\Omega})
satisfies the replacement chain property in
B_{r_{0}}(p)
. Then by Theorem
4.4
,
(\hat{V},\hat{\Omega})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{r_{0}}(p)
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary. By the arbitrariness of
p
, it follows that
(\hat{V},\hat{\Omega})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}U
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary.

Consider a finite collection of open subsets
B_{1},\cdots,B_{j}\subset\subset B_{r_{0}}(p)
. Let
\{(\Sigma_{k,\ell},\Omega_{k,\ell})\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
be
a

minimizing

sequence

of

Problem

(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k},\mathfrak{Is}^{h}(B_{1}))
, i.e. there exist
\{\Psi_{k,\ell}\}\subset\mathfrak{Is}(B_{1})
such that

(\Sigma_{k,\ell},\Omega_{k,\ell})=\Psi_{k,\ell}(1,\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k}),\quad\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{k,\ell},\Omega_{k,\ell})=\inf\{\mathcal{A}^{h}(\psi(1,\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k});\psi\in\mathfrak{Is}(B_{1})\}.

Denote by
(\widetilde{V}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})
the limit of
(\Sigma_{k,\ell},\Omega_{k,\ell})
as
\ell\to\infty
. Then
(\widetilde{V}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{1}
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
B_{1}
by Theorem
1.25
. Denote by
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\Omega})
the limit of
(\widetilde{V}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})
as
k\to\infty
. We can take sufficiently large
\ell(k)
so that

(\Sigma_{k,\ell(k)},\Omega_{k,\ell(k)})\to(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\Omega})\quad\text{ as }k\to\infty.

By the definition of
(\Sigma_{k,\ell},\Omega_{k,\ell})
, we have that
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\Omega})=(\hat{V},\hat{\Omega})
on
U\setminus\mathrm{Clos}(B_{1})
. Then by the regularity of
(\widetilde{V}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{1}
and Proposition
1.24
,
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\Omega})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{1}
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary.

Note that by Lemma
4.5
, each
(\Sigma_{k,\ell(k)},\Omega_{k,\ell(k)})
can be constructed from
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
via an isotopy
\Phi_{k}\in\mathfrak{Is}(B_{2r_{0}}(p))
satisfying

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi_{k}(t,\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\big)\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})+\delta,\text{ for all }0\leq t\leq 1.

Since
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\}
is a minimizing sequence in Problem
(\Sigma,\Omega,\mathfrak{Is}_{\delta}^{h}(U))
, there exist isotopies
\{\Phi_{k}^{\prime}\}\subset\mathfrak{Is}(U)
, such that

(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})=\Phi_{k}^{\prime}(1,\Sigma,\Omega),\quad\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma,\Omega)\text{ and}

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi_{k}^{\prime}(t,\Sigma,\Omega)\big)\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma,\Omega)+\delta,\text{ for all }0\leq t\leq 1.

By concatenating
\Phi_{k}^{\prime}
with
\Phi_{k}
, we know that
(\Sigma_{k,\ell(k)},\Omega_{k,\ell(k)})
is obtained from
(\Sigma,\Omega)
via an isotopy
\widetilde{\Phi}_{k}\in\mathfrak{Is}(U)
with

\widetilde{\Phi}(1,\Sigma,\Omega)=(\Sigma_{k,\ell(k)},\Omega_{k,\ell(k)}),\quad\mathcal{A}^{h}(\widetilde{\Phi}(t,\Sigma,\Omega))\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma,\Omega)+\delta,\text{ for all }0\leq t\leq 1.

Thus
\{(\Sigma_{k,\ell(k)},\Omega_{k,\ell(k)})\}
is also minimizing in Problem
(\Sigma,\Omega,\mathfrak{Is}_{\delta}^{h}(U))
, which implies that
\mathcal{A}^{h}(\hat{V},\hat{\Omega})=\mathcal{A}^{h}(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\Omega})
.

All of these give that
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\Omega})
is an
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-replacement of
(\hat{V},\hat{\Omega})
in
B_{1}
. Observe that the replacement
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\Omega})
is also the limit of a minimizing sequence in Problem
(\Sigma,\Omega,\mathfrak{Is}_{\delta}^{h}(U))
. Therefore
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\Omega})
has a replacement in
B_{2}
. Continuing the process, we have proved that
(\hat{V},\hat{\Omega})
satisfies the replacement chain property in
B_{r_{0}}(p)
. This finishes the proof of Proposition
4.6
.
∎

4.3.
Interior regularity for almost minimizing pairs

In this part, we use the regularity of constrained
h
-minimizer (Proposition
4.6
) to construct
C^{1,1}
-replacements of a given
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing pair. Then the First Regularity Theorem
4.4
applies to give the full regularity.

Theorem 4.7

(Regularity of
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing pairs)
.

Given an open set
U\subset M
, let
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
be
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing in
U
; that is,

•

there exists a sequence
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{E}
that converges to
(V,\Omega)
under the
\mathscr{F}
-metric;

•

(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing in
U
for some
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
and
\delta_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
.

Then
(V,\Omega)\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}U
is induced by a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary.

Proof.

Clearly,
(V,\Omega)\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}U
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable by Lemma
3.3
. To prove the desired regularity, we will show that given any
U^{\prime}\subset\subset U
,
(V,\Omega)
satisfies the replacement chain property in
U^{\prime}
; then it follows from Theorem
4.4
.

Fix
U^{\prime}\subset\subset U
, and let
B_{1},\cdots,B_{N}\subset\subset U^{\prime}
be an arbitrary collection of open subsets. We now construct the desired chain of replacements as follows. We start with
B_{1}
. Let
\{(\Sigma_{j,\ell},\Omega_{j,\ell})\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
be
a minimizing sequence for Problem
(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j},\mathfrak{Is}_{\delta_{j}}^{h}(B_{1}))
. Then by Proposition
4.6
,
(\Sigma_{j,\ell},\Omega_{j,\ell})
converges to some
(\hat{V}_{j},\hat{\Omega}_{j})\in\mathcal{VC}(M)
which is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary and stable
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary in
B_{1}
, and satisfies

(4.8)

\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})-\epsilon_{j}\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\hat{V}_{j},\hat{\Omega}_{j})\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j}).

We remark that

(4.9)

(\hat{V}_{j},\hat{\Omega}_{j})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}(M\setminus\mathrm{Clos}(B_{1}))=(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}(M\setminus\mathrm{Clos}(B_{1})).

Now letting
j\to\infty
, by Proposition
1.24
,
(\hat{V}_{j},\hat{\Omega}_{j})
converges to
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
, where
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{1}
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
stationary, stable,
C^{1,1}

h
-boundary, and
\hat{V}_{j}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{1}\to V^{*}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{1}
in the sense of
C^{1,\alpha}_{loc}
.

We now verify that
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
is a replacement of
(V,\Omega)
in
B_{1}
. Note that by (
4.9
),

(V^{*},\Omega^{*})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}(M\setminus\mathrm{Clos}(B_{1}))=(V,\Omega)\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}(M\setminus\mathrm{Clos}(B_{1})).

Letting
j\to\infty
, we obtain from (
4.8
) that

\mathcal{A}^{h}(V^{*},\Omega^{*})=\mathcal{A}^{h}(V,\Omega).

So far, we have verified that
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
is a replacement of
(V,\Omega)
in
B_{1}
.

In the next, we will show that
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
is also the limit of a sequence
\{(\Sigma_{j}^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime}_{j})\}\subset\mathscr{E}
which is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing in
U
. Then by the same argument, one can construct a replacement of
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
in
B_{2}
. Moreover, we can continue the process and then the lemma is proved.

Indeed, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we can take
\ell(j)
sufficiently large so that
(\Sigma_{j,\ell(j)},\Omega_{j,\ell(j)})
converges to
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
as
j\to\infty
, and

\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{j,\ell(j)},\Omega_{j,\ell(j)})\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j}).

Together with the fact that
(\Sigma_{j,\ell(j)},\Omega_{j,\ell(j)})
can be constructed from
(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})
via an isotopy in
\mathfrak{Is}_{\delta_{j}}^{h}(B_{1})
, we can then conclude that
(\Sigma_{j,\ell(j)},\Omega_{j,\ell(j)})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing in
U
(similarly as in Proposition
4.6
). This completes the proof of Theorem
4.7
.
∎

4.4.
Proof of Theorem
2.4

We will show that the pair
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})
obtained in Theorem
3.8
satisfies the regularity conclusion of Theorem
2.4
. We can find finitely many balls
\{B_{r_{i}}(p_{i})\}_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{m}}
, where
r_{i}
is the almost minimizing radius given by Theorem
3.8
, to cover
M
. Then for each small enough open set
U
lying in some
\mathrm{An}(p_{i};0,r_{i})
,
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-almost minimizing w.r.t.
\{(\Sigma_{j},\Omega_{j})\}
in
U
, and by Theorem
4.7
,
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}U
is a
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary boundary. So we know that
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})
is a
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary boundary in
M\setminus\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{\mathfrak{m}}\}
.

Next, we prove the
C^{1,1}
-version of removable singularity result, that is,
(V_{0},\Omega_{0})
extends as a
C^{1,1}
and strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary boundary across each
p_{i}
. The argument will be the same way as in the smooth case; see
[
51
]
*Step 4, page 479. We write
V_{0}
as
\Sigma_{0}
. Fix
p_{i}
and we drop the index for a moment. Assume that
\Theta^{2}(\|V_{0}\|,p)=m\in\mathbb{N}
. By Lemma
3.3
,
(\Sigma_{0},\Omega_{0})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stable in any annulus
\mathrm{An}\subset\mathrm{An}(p;0,r_{p})
. By Proposition
1.24
and Proposition
4.1
, we have that for any sequence
r_{j}\to 0
,

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{p,r_{j}}(\Sigma_{0})\to m\cdot S,

in
C^{1,1}_{loc}(T_{p}M\setminus\{0\})
for some 2-plane
S\subset T_{p}M
. (Note that a prior,
S
may depend on
\{r_{j}\}
.) Therefore, there exists
\sigma_{0}>0
small enough, such that for any
0<\sigma\leq\sigma_{0}
,
\Sigma_{0}
has an
m
-sheeted, ordered,
C^{1,1}
-graphical decomposition in
\mathrm{An}(p;\sigma/2,\sigma)
:

\Sigma_{0}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}\mathrm{An}(p;\sigma/2,\sigma)=\cup_{i=1}^{m}\Gamma^{i}(\sigma).

By shrinking
\sigma\to 0
, we can continue each sheet
\Gamma^{j}(\sigma_{0})
in
\mathrm{An}(p;\sigma_{0}/2,\sigma_{0})
to the whole punctured ball
B_{\sigma_{0}}(p)\setminus\{p\}
, and we denote this sheet by
\Gamma^{j}
. By Corollary
1.19
, each
\Gamma^{j}
has
c
-bounded first variation in
B_{\sigma_{0}}(p)\setminus\{p\}
for
c=\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}
. By a standard cutoff trick, each
\Gamma^{j}
can be extended as a varifold with
c
-bounded first variation in
B_{\sigma_{0}}(p)
. It is easy to see that each tangent varifold
C^{j}
of
\Gamma^{j}
at
p
is an integer multiple of some
2
-plane by the
C^{1,1}_{loc}
convergence. Moreover, the multiplicity of
C^{j}
has to be one as
\Theta^{2}(\|V_{0}\|,p)=m
. By the Allard regularity theorem, each
\Gamma^{j}
extends to a
C^{1,\alpha}
-surface across
p
. Since their union
\Sigma_{0}\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}B_{\sigma_{0}}(p)=\cup\Gamma^{j}
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary,
\Sigma_{0}
must be
C^{1,1}
by the regularity result Proposition
A.1
. The strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity also extends across
p
by the same standard cutoff trick. This completes the proof of Theorem
2.4
.

5.
Passing to limit

Given an
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
and a sequence of positive numbers
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, for simplicity, we write
\mathcal{A}^{k}
for
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}
in this part. Assume that
X\subset I(m,k_{0})
is a cubic complex and
Z\subset X
is a sub-complex. For a given
(X,Z)
-homotopy class
\Pi
generated by some fixed continuous
\Phi_{0}:X\to\mathscr{E}
relative to
\Phi_{0}|_{Z}
, we consider the min-max problems associated with
\Pi
for each
\mathcal{A}^{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We assume that the nontriviality condition (
2.2
) holds for all
k
. For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, applying Theorem
3.8
to the
\mathcal{A}^{k}
-functional, we obtain a min-max pair
(V_{k},\Omega_{k})\in\VC(M)
and an associated min-max sequence
\{(\Sigma_{k,j},\Omega_{k,j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{E}
, such that
(V_{k},\Omega_{k})
is
\mathcal{A}^{k}
-stationary and
\mathcal{A}^{k}
-almost minimizing in small annuli w.r.t.
\{(\Sigma_{k,j},\Omega_{k,j})\}
. By Theorem
2.4
,
(V_{k},\Omega_{k})
is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{k}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}

(\varepsilon_{k}h)
-boundary
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
with
\mathcal{A}^{k}(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})=\mathbf{L}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}(\Pi)
.

Let

\text{$V_{\infty}\in\mathcal{V}(M)$ be a subsequential varifold limit of $\{\Sigma_{k}\}$}.

Then it is clear that
V_{\infty}
is stationary for the area functional.

In this section, we will show that
V_{\infty}
is induced by a closed embedded minimal surface, and the weighted genus bound (see (
5.3
)) holds for specially chosen
h
.

5.1.
Strong convergence

In this part, we will show the smooth regularity of
V_{\infty}
and the
C^{1,1}_{loc}
-subsequential convergence of
\Sigma_{k}
to
V_{\infty}
. By Corollary
3.11
, we know that for every
L=L(m)
-admissible (see (
3.2
)) collection
\mathscr{C}
of annuli,
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
is
\mathcal{A}^{k}
-stable in at least one annulus in
\mathscr{C}
. Therefore, we know that
V_{\infty}
also satisfies
4
4

4

It is clear that the varifold limits of
\mathcal{A}^{k}
-stable pairs as
k\to\infty
are
\mathcal{A}^{0}
-stable.

(5.1)

\displaystyle\text{Property {\bf(R')}}:

\displaystyle\text{for every $L(m)$-admissible collection $\mathscr{C}$ of annuli,}

\displaystyle\text{$V_{\infty}$ is stable (for area) in at least one annulus in $\mathscr{C}$.}

Proposition 5.1
.

There exists a subsequence (without relabelling) of
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, such that

(5.2)

\displaystyle\text{Property {\bf(S)}}:

\displaystyle\text{given any $p\in M$, there exists $r_{p}>0$, such that}

\displaystyle\text{for each $\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)$ with $0<s<r<r_{p}$, }

\displaystyle\text{$(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})$ is $\mathcal{A}^{k}$-stable in $\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)$ for all sufficiently large $k$.}

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{k}
be the collection of annulli
\mathrm{An}
so that
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
is
\mathcal{A}^{k}
-stable in
\mathrm{An}
. Obviously if
\mathrm{An}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
, any sub-annulus also belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{k}
. Also we know
\mathcal{P}_{k}\cap\mathscr{C}\neq\emptyset
for any
L
-admissible collection
\mathscr{C}
of annuli. By Proposition
D.1
, there exists a subsequence of
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\}
, such that for any
p\in M
, there exists
r_{p}>0
, so that for any
0<s<r<r_{p}
,
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
for sufficiently large
k
. This is exactly Property
(S)
.
∎

Theorem 5.2
.

As above,
\spt\|V_{\infty}\|
is a closed embedded minimal surface
\Sigma_{\infty}
. Moreover, there exists a finite set of points
\mathcal{Y}\subset M
, such that up to a subsequence,
\{\Sigma_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
(without relabeling) converges in
C^{1,1}_{loc}
to
\Sigma_{\infty}
in any compact subset of
M\setminus\mathcal{Y}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
1.24
,
\Sigma_{k}
convergences subsequentially in
C^{1,\alpha}_{loc}
to a
C^{1,1}
almost embedded surface
\Sigma_{\infty}\subset M\setminus\mathcal{Y}
in any compact subsets of
M\setminus\mathcal{Y}
. Then
\Sigma_{\infty}
is smoothly embedded as
V_{\infty}
is stationary. Hence the convergence is in
C^{1,1}_{loc}
by Proposition
1.24
(i). To show the removable singularity of
\Sigma_{\infty}
, for a fixed
p\in\mathcal{Y}
, given any
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)\subset\mathrm{An}(p;0,r_{p})
, by Property
(S)
(
5.2
), we can use the
\mathcal{A}^{k}
-stability for the sequences
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
to deduce that
\Sigma_{\infty}
is stable in
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)
. Then
\Sigma_{\infty}
extends smoothly across
p
by the standard removable singularity theorem (for instance
[
38
]
).
∎

5.2.
Passing almost minimizing to limit and topology bound

Now we will prove that the support
\Sigma_{\infty}
of
V_{\infty}
, which is a closed embedded minimal surface, has total genus less than
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
– the genus of elements in
\mathscr{E}
. To approach it, we will find a diagonal subsequence of elements in
\mathscr{E}
approaching
V_{\infty}
which satisfies certain almost minimizing in small annuli property.

Proposition 5.3
.

There exist a subsequence
\{(\Sigma_{k,j(k)},\Omega_{k,j(k)})\}
and
\epsilon_{k}\to 0
,
\delta_{k}\to 0
, such that
\Sigma_{k,j(k)}
converges to
V_{\infty}
as varifolds, and

(1)

for any
L(m)
-admissible collection
\mathscr{C}
,
(\Sigma_{k,j(k)},\Omega_{k,j(k)})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{k},\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
-almost minimizing in at least one of
\mathscr{C}
;

(2)

given any
p\in M
, there exists
r_{p}>0
such that for each
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)
with
0<s<r<r_{p}
,
(\Sigma_{k,j(k)},\Omega_{k,j(k)})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{k},\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
-almost minimizing in
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)
for all
k
sufficiently large.

Proof.

Note that
(V_{k},\Omega_{k})
is
\mathcal{A}^{k}
-almost minimizing w.r.t.
\{(\Sigma_{k,j},\Omega_{k,j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
. Thus there exist
\epsilon_{k,j},\delta_{k,j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
so that
(\Sigma_{k,j},\Omega_{k,j})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{k},\epsilon_{k,j},\delta_{k,j})
-almost minimizing in any
L(m)
-admissible collection of annuli. By taking
j(k)
sufficiently large, we have that for any
L(m)
-admissible collection
\mathscr{C}
,
(\Sigma_{k,j(k)},\Omega_{k,j(k)})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{k},\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
-almost minimizing in
\mathscr{C}
, where
\epsilon_{k}:=\epsilon_{k,j(k)}
and
\delta_{k}:=\delta_{k,j(k)}
.

Now let
\mathcal{P}_{k}
be the collection of annuli
\mathrm{An}
so that
(\Sigma_{k,j(k)},\Omega_{k,j(k)})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{k},\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
-almost minimizing in
\mathrm{An}
. Thus by Proposition
D.1
, there exists a subsequence (without relabeling) so that given
p\in M
, there exists
r_{p}>0
such that for each
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)
with
0<s<r<r_{p}
,
(\Sigma_{k,j(k)},\Omega_{k,j(k)})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{k},\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
-almost minimizing in
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)
for all sufficiently large
k
. This finishes the proof of Proposition
5.3
.
∎

Note that
V_{\infty}
may not be
\mathcal{A}^{0}
-almost minimizing (
\mathcal{A}^{0}
is the area functional) in small annuli (which is the requirement in
[
20
]
to obtain the genus bound). However, we can take a special
h
so that as
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
, the min-max solution
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
w.r.t.
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}
-functional will converge to a minimal surface which still has the genus bound.

Theorem 5.4

(Genus bound)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed, oriented, three dimensional Riemannian manifold, and
V_{\infty}
be as above. Suppose that there are finitely many pairwise disjoint balls
B_{1},\cdots,B_{\alpha}\subset M
such that

(1)

\spt\|V_{\infty}\|\cap B_{j}
is a disk for
j=1,\cdots,\alpha
;

(2)

h\equiv 0
in a small neighborhood of
\spt\|V_{\infty}\|\setminus\cup_{j}B_{j}
.

Assume that
V_{\infty}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}[\Gamma_{i}]
,
where
\{\Gamma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
is a pairwise disjoint collection of connected, closed, embedded, minimal surfaces.
Denote by
I_{O}\subset\{1,\cdots,N\}
(resp.
I_{U}
) the collection of
i
such that
\Gamma_{i}
is orientable (resp. non-orientable). Then we have

(5.3)

\sum_{i\in I_{O}}m_{i}\cdot\mathfrak{g}(\Gamma_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in I_{U}}m_{i}\cdot(\mathfrak{g}(\Gamma_{i})-1)\leq\mathfrak{g}_{0},

where
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{g}(\Gamma_{i})
are the genus of
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Gamma_{i}
, respectively.

Proof.

Let
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
be a collection of simple closed curves contained in
\cup_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma_{i}
. Since
\spt\|V_{\infty}\|\cap B_{j}
is a disk, then we can perturb
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
(not relabelled) in the same isotopy class so that
\cup_{i}\gamma_{i}
does not intersect
\cup_{j}B_{j}
. Thus we have that
\varepsilon_{k}h\equiv 0
in a neighborhood of
\cup_{i}\gamma_{i}
. Note that by Proposition
5.3
, given
p\in M
, there exists
r_{p}>0
such that for each
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)
with
0<s<r<r_{p}
,
(\Sigma_{k,j(k)},\Omega_{k,j(k)})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h},\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
-almost minimizing in
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)
for all sufficiently large
k
, where
\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
. In particular, by possibly perturbing
\{\gamma_{i}\}
and shrinking
r_{p}
, we can assume that
(\Sigma_{k,j(k)},\Omega_{k,j(k)})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{0},\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
-almost minimizing in
B_{r_{p}}(p)
for any
p\in\cup_{i}\gamma_{i}
.
Then one can lift the curves by the same argument (see
[
20
]
*Remark 1.3) in
[
20
]
*Proposition 2.2, which yields the desired genus bounds; see also
[
9
]
. Hence Theorem
5.4
is proved.
∎

6.
Existence of supersolution

In the above section, we proved that a sequence of
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}
-min-max pairs
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to a limit minimal surface
\Sigma_{\infty}
when
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
. In this part, assuming
h
changes sign on
\Sigma_{\infty}
, we show that if the convergence has multiplicity greater than one, then
\Sigma_{\infty}
admits a nonnegative weak supersolution to a variant of the Jacobi equation (see (
6.16
)). The idea is to subtract the mean curvature equations satisfied by the top and bottom sheets and take re-normalized limit. We will present all the subtle details as surfaces involved in our setting are merely
C^{1,1}
.

We start with some general assumptions. Let
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
be a sequence of strongly
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}

(\varepsilon_{k}h)
-boundary. Suppose that
\Sigma_{k}
converges as varifolds to a closed, embedded, minimal surface
\Sigma
with multiplicity
m\in\mathbb{N}
as
k\to\infty
(
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
). Furthermore, we assume that

(A)

h
changes sign on
\Sigma
, and

(B)

the convergence is
C^{1,1}_{loc}
in any compact subset away from a finite set
\mathcal{Y}\subset M
.

Fix a unit normal
\nu
of
\Sigma
. We will use Fermi coordinates of
\Sigma
given by the normal exponential map
(x,z)\to\exp_{x}\big(z\nu(x)\big)
,
(x,z)\in\Sigma\times(-\delta_{0},\delta_{0})
. By the
C^{1,1}_{loc}
-convergence, for any open subset
\mathcal{U}\subset\subset\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}
, and for all
k
large enough (depending only on
\mathcal{U}
),
\Sigma_{k}
has an
m
-sheeted ordered decomposition
\Gamma_{k}^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma_{k}^{m}
inside the thickened neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}=\mathcal{U}\times(-\delta,\delta)
, and each sheet
\Gamma_{k}^{\iota}
(
1\leq\iota\leq m
) is a normal graph of some
u_{k}^{\iota}\in C^{1,1}(\mathcal{U})
, such that

u_{k}^{1}\leq\cdots\leq u_{k}^{m},\text{ and }u_{k}^{\iota}\to 0\text{ in $C^{1,1}(\mathcal{U})$ as $k\to\infty$}.

By choosing
\mathcal{U}
large enough, we may also assume that
h
changes sign in
\mathcal{U}
.

We let
H_{k}^{\iota}
denote the generalized mean curvature of
\Gamma_{k}^{\iota}
w.r.t. the upward pointing normal (in the same direction as
\partial/\partial z
). By Corollary
1.19
, we have

(6.1)

|H_{k}^{\iota}|\leq\varepsilon_{k}|h|,\quad\text{$\mathcal{H}^{2}$-a.e. in $\Gamma_{k}^{\iota}$, for each $1\leq\iota\leq m$}.

Denote by
\nu_{k}
the unit outer normal of
\Sigma_{k}
induced from
\Omega_{k}
. By Lemma
1.11
,
\nu_{k}
alternates orientation among
\{\Gamma^{\iota}_{k}\}
. After possibly switching
\Omega_{k}
to
M\setminus\Omega_{k}
and
h
to
-h
simultaneously and up to a subsequence, we may always assume that
\nu_{k}
points upward along the top sheet
\Gamma^{m}_{k}
. That is,

\Omega_{k}
does not contain the region above the top sheet
\Gamma_{k}^{m}
.

Then we have by strong
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}
-stationarity that

(6.2)

H_{k}^{m}\geq\varepsilon_{k}h,\quad\text{$\mathcal{H}^{2}$-a.e. in $\Gamma_{k}^{m}$}.

In fact, by Proposition
1.20
, we know that in a neighborhood where
h>0
, we must have
H_{k}^{m}=\varepsilon_{k}h
, and the strictly inequality
H_{k}^{m}>\varepsilon_{k}h
can only happen in a neighborhood where
h<0
. Moreover, by Corollary
1.19
, we know that
H_{k}^{m-1}
is either equal to 0 or
-\varepsilon_{k}h
for all points in
\{h>0\}
. In particular,

(6.3)

H_{k}^{m-1}\leq 0\quad\text{ for all points in $\{h>0\}$}.

Applying similar arguments to
\Gamma_{k}^{1}
, we have for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e. in
\Gamma_{k}^{1}
,

(6.4)

H_{k}^{1}\leq\varepsilon_{k}h\quad\text{ if $m$ is odd};\quad H_{k}^{1}\leq-\varepsilon_{k}h\quad\text{ if $m$ is even}.

Let
f
be a
C^{1,1}
test function defined on
\Sigma
with compact support in
\mathcal{U}
. Denote by

\varphi_{k}:=u_{k}^{m}-u_{k}^{1}\geq 0.

The following are derived in Appendix
C
, in particular (
C.5
), (
C.6
), and (
C.8
).

Lemma 6.1
.

There exist Lipschitz 2-tensors
{\bm{\alpha}}_{k}
, vector fields
{\bm{\beta}}_{k}
, and functions
\zeta_{k}
,
\sigma^{1}_{k}
and
\sigma^{m}_{k}
defined on
\mathcal{U}
, so that

(6.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}}\langle\nabla\varphi_{k},\nabla f\rangle

\displaystyle-\big(\Ric(\nu,\nu)+|A^{\Sigma}|^{2}\big)\varphi_{k}\cdot f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}\bm{\alpha}_{k}(\nabla\varphi_{k},\nabla f)+\bm{\beta}_{k}\cdot(\varphi_{k}\nabla f+f\nabla\varphi_{k})+\zeta_{k}\varphi_{k}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}(x)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{U}}\big(H_{k}^{m}\cdot\sigma^{m}_{k}-H_{k}^{1}\cdot\sigma_{k}^{1}\big)\cdot f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}(x),

where

(6.6)

\|\bm{\alpha}_{k}\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})},\|\bm{\beta}_{k}\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})},\|\zeta_{k}\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})},\|\sigma^{m}_{k}-1\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})},\|\sigma^{1}_{k}-1\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})}\to 0\text{ as }k\to\infty.

Similarly, we also have for
v_{k}=u_{k}^{m}-u_{k}^{m-1}\geq 0
(note that
v_{k}\leq\varphi_{k}
),

(6.7)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}}\langle\nabla v_{k},\nabla f\rangle

\displaystyle-\big(\Ric(\nu,\nu)+|A^{\Sigma}|^{2}\big)v_{k}\cdot f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}\overline{\bm{\alpha}}_{k}(\nabla v_{k},\nabla f)+\overline{\bm{\beta}}_{k}\cdot(v_{k}\nabla f+f\nabla v_{k})+\overline{\zeta}_{k}v_{k}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}(x)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{U}}\big(H_{k}^{m}\cdot\sigma_{k}^{m}-H_{k}^{m-1}\cdot\sigma_{k}^{m-1}\big)\cdot f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}(x),

where

\|\overline{\bm{\alpha}}_{k}\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})},\|\overline{\bm{\beta}}_{k}\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})},\|\overline{\zeta}_{k}\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})},\|\sigma^{m}_{k}-1\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})},\|\sigma^{m-1}_{k}-1\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})}\to 0\text{ as }k\to\infty.

We claim that the
L^{2}
-magnitude of the height differences should always dominate the size of parameters. We will use the height difference
v_{k}
of the top two sheets.

Lemma 6.2
.

There exists
c>0
such that for all sufficiently large
k
,

(6.8)

\varepsilon_{k}\leq c\cdot\|v_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{U})},\quad\text{and hence}\quad\varepsilon_{k}\leq c\cdot\|\varphi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{U})}.

Proof.

Suppose on the contrary that up to a subsequence, as
k\to\infty
,

(6.9)

(\|v_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{U})})/\varepsilon_{k}\to 0.

Then for any
\eta\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathcal{U})
, by letting
f=\eta^{2}v_{k}
in (
6.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}}|\nabla v_{k}|^{2}\eta^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\leq C\int_{\mathcal{U}}|\nabla\eta|^{2}|v_{k}|^{2}+|v_{k}|^{2}\eta^{2}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{k}\eta^{2}v_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{10}\int_{\mathcal{U}}(|v_{k}||\nabla(\eta^{2}v_{k})|+\eta^{2}|v_{k}||\nabla v_{k}|)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}.

Simplifying it, we obtain

\int_{\mathcal{U}}|\nabla v_{k}|^{2}\eta^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\leq C\int_{\mathcal{U}}|\nabla\eta|^{2}|v_{k}|^{2}+|v_{k}|^{2}\eta^{2}+\varepsilon_{k}v_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}.

Thus given
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subset\subset\mathcal{U}
,

\int_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}|\nabla v_{k}/\varepsilon_{k}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\leq C(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{U}^{\prime},M,\sup|h|)\int_{\mathcal{U}}|v_{k}/\varepsilon_{k}|^{2}+1\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}.

It follows that up to a subsequence,
v_{k}/\varepsilon_{k}
weakly converges to some
w
weakly in
W^{1,2}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})
. Together with (
6.9
), we have that
w=0
.

Observe that by (
6.2
) and (
6.3
), if
h(x,0)>0
, then for sufficiently large
k
,

H_{k}^{m}(x,u_{k}^{m}(x))-H_{k}^{m-1}(x,u_{k}^{m-1}(x))\geq\varepsilon_{k}h(x,u_{k}^{m}(x)).

Letting
k\to\infty
in (
6.7
), we then get for any
f\in C^{2}_{c}(\{h>0\}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime})
with
f\geq 0
and
f>0
somewhere,

\int_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}hf\leq 0.

Note that we can choose
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
large so that
\{h>0\}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. This leads to a contradiction. Hence Lemma
6.2
is proved.
∎

Up to a subsequence without relabeling, we can assume that there exists
c\in[0,\infty)
,

(6.10)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon_{k}/\|\varphi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{U})}=c.

Let
\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}=\varphi_{k}/\|\varphi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{U})}
; (note that
\|\varphi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{U})}>0
). We consider the limit of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}
on
\mathcal{U}
as
k\to\infty
. By taking an exhaustion
\{\mathcal{U}_{k}\}
of
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}
, a diagonal argument will give a limit function defined on
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}
. We will also prove that such a limit is uniformly bounded (and hence non-trivial) and is a supersolution in the following sense.

Lemma 6.3
.

Up to subsequence,
\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}
converges to a uniformly bounded
C^{1,\alpha}_{c}
-function
\varphi:\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}\to[0,\infty)
with
\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y})}=1
. Furthermore, for any
f\in C^{1}_{c}(\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y})
and
f\geq 0
, we have

(1)

if
m\geq 3
is odd, then

(6.11)

\int_{\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}}\langle\nabla\varphi,\nabla f\rangle-\big(\Ric(\nu,\nu)+|A^{\Sigma}|^{2}\big)\varphi f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\geq 0;

(2)

if
m
is even, then

(6.12)

\int_{\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}}\langle\nabla\varphi,\nabla f\rangle-\big(\Ric(\nu,\nu)+|A^{\Sigma}|^{2}\big)\varphi f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\geq 2c\int_{\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}}hf\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}.

Proof.

Recall that by (
6.1
), we always have

(6.13)

|H_{k}^{m}|+|H_{k}^{1}|\leq\varepsilon_{k}\big(|h(x,u_{k}^{m}(x))|+|h(x,u_{k}^{1}(x))|\big).

Therefore, if we renormalize the weak equation (
6.5
) by
\|\varphi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{U})}
, the renormalized terms of
(H_{k}^{m}\cdot\sigma_{k}^{m}-H_{k}^{1}\cdot\sigma_{k}^{1})
will have uniform
L^{\infty}
upper bound by (
6.6
) and (
6.8
).
Then by applying the interior Hölder estimates
[
10
]
*Theorem 8.24 to the renormalized weak equation of (
6.5
), we have for any open domain
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subset\subset\mathcal{U}
,

\|\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}\|_{C^{\alpha}(\overline{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}})}\leq C(\|\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{U})}+1)\leq C,

where
C
is a constant independent of
k
. Applying the
C^{1,\alpha}
-estimates
[
10
]
*Theorem 8.32, we know that a subsequence of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}
converges to a nonnegative function
\varphi\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathcal{U})
in the sense of
C^{1,\alpha}_{loc}(\mathcal{U})
. Note that above argument works for any
\mathcal{U}\subset\subset\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}
. Taking an exhaustion of
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}
, we can extend
\varphi
to
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}
. Next we will show that
\varphi
is a supersolution.

Now we first consider the case when
m\geq 3
is odd.
By (
6.2
) and (
6.4
), we know that for
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-a.e.
x\in U
,

(6.14)

H_{k}^{m}-H_{k}^{1}\geq\varepsilon_{k}\big(h(x,u_{k}^{m}(x))-h(x,u_{k}^{1}(x))\big)\geq-C\varepsilon_{k}\varphi_{k},

for some constant
C
independent of
k
. Plugging (
6.14
) and (
6.6
) into (
6.5
), and then taking the limit, we have

\int_{\mathcal{U}}\langle\nabla\varphi,\nabla f\rangle-\big(\Ric(\nu,\nu)+|A^{\Sigma}|^{2}\big)\varphi f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}\geq 0,\quad\forall f\in C^{1}_{c}(U)\text{ and }f\geq 0.

Taking an exhaustion of
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}
, we can extend
\varphi
to
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}
satisfying (
6.11
).

Next we consider the case when
m\geq 2
is even.
Recall that by (
6.2
) and (
6.4
),

H_{k}^{m}-H_{k}^{1}\geq\varepsilon_{k}\big(h(x,u_{k}^{m}(x))+h(x,u_{k}^{1}(x))\big).

Plugging it into (
6.5
) and using (
6.6
), and then taking the limit, we have

(6.15)

\int_{\mathcal{U}}\langle\nabla\varphi,\nabla f\rangle-\big(\Ric(\nu,\nu)+|A^{\Sigma}|^{2}\big)\varphi f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}\geq 2c\int_{\mathcal{U}}hf\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n},\quad\forall f\in C^{1}_{c}(U)\text{ and }f\geq 0.

Using an exhaustion of
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}
again, we can extend
\varphi
to
\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}
satisfying (
6.12
).

It remains to show that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}
is pointwisely bounded independent of
k
. We sketch the proof when
m
is even and the other case is similar. Taking
r
small enough so that the constant mean curvature foliation
[
54
]
*Proposition D.1 exists in a neighborhood of
B_{3r}(y)\cap\Sigma
for each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
. Then by the argument above for
\mathcal{U}=\Sigma\setminus\cup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\overline{B}_{r}(y)
,
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=\Sigma\setminus\cup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\overline{B}_{2r}(y)
, there exists a function
\varphi\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})
satisfying (
6.15
). Note that by a standard argument
[
54
]
*Page 802, Part 7, one can prove that for any
x\in\mathrm{dom}(\widetilde{\varphi}_{k})\cap B_{3r}(y)\setminus\{y\}
,

\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}(x)\leq C(\sup_{\partial B_{3r}(y)}\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}+1).

Thus,
\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}
is uniformly bounded independent of
k
. This implies that the
L^{2}
-norm of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}
cannot concentrate near
\mathcal{Y}
, and hence we must have
\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y})}=1
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
6.3
.
∎

In the next, we prove that
\varphi\in W^{1,2}(\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y})
, which implies that
\varphi
can be extended across
\mathcal{Y}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\{(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of strongly
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}

\varepsilon_{k}h
-boundary in
(M,g)
with
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
as
k\to 0
. Suppose that
\Sigma_{k}
converges as varifolds to a closed, embedded, two-sided, minimal surface
\Sigma
with multiplicity
m\geq 2
. Suppose in addition that the convergence is
C^{1,1}_{loc}
away from a finite set
\mathcal{Y}
. Then
\Sigma
admits a nonnegative function
\varphi\in W^{1,2}(\Sigma)
with
\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=1
and a constant
c\geq 0
satisfying

(6.16)

\int_{\Sigma}\langle\nabla\varphi,\nabla f\rangle-\big(\Ric(\nu,\nu)+|A^{\Sigma}|^{2}\big)\varphi f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\geq\int_{\Sigma}2chf\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2},\ \forall f\in C^{1}(\Sigma)\text{ and }f\geq 0.

Here
c=0
if
m\geq 3
is odd.

Proof.

We will first show that there is a constant
C>0
such that

(6.17)

\int_{\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\leq C\int_{\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}}\varphi^{2}+1\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}.

Together with the fact that
\varphi
is uniformly bounded, we conclude that
\varphi
could be extended to be a function
\varphi\in W^{1,2}(\Sigma)
. Hence (
6.16
) can be derived from (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) using a standard log-cutoff trick.

In general, the supersolution inequalities (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) are not enough to derive the bound (
6.17
). Instead, we will use the identity (
6.5
) together with the mean curvature estimates (
6.13
) and the comparison estimates (
6.8
) to prove the desired bound (
6.17
). In fact, let
\{\eta_{r}\}_{r>0}
be a family of log-cut-off functions so that
\eta_{r}=0
in
\cup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}B_{r}(y)
, and as
r\to 0
,

0\leq\eta_{r}\leq 1,\quad\eta_{r}\to 1\text{ on }\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y},\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\Sigma}|\nabla\eta_{r}|^{2}\to 0.

Then by taking
f=\eta_{r}^{2}\varphi_{k}
in (
6.5
) and then applying (
6.13
) and (
6.8
), we obtain that for all sufficiently large
k
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}}|\nabla\varphi_{k}|^{2}\cdot\eta_{r}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathcal{U}}|\nabla\eta_{r}|^{2}|\varphi_{k}|^{2}+|\varphi_{k}|^{2}\eta_{r}^{2}+\varepsilon_{k}\varphi_{k}\cdot\eta_{r}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}+

\displaystyle\hskip 100.00015pt+\frac{1}{10}\int_{\mathcal{U}}\eta_{r}^{2}\varphi_{k}|\nabla\varphi_{k}|+\varphi_{k}|\nabla(\eta_{r}^{2}\varphi_{k})|\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathcal{U}}|\nabla\eta_{r}|^{2}|\varphi_{k}|^{2}+|\varphi_{k}|^{2}\eta_{r}^{2}+\varepsilon_{k}\varphi_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{U}}|\nabla\varphi_{k}|^{2}\cdot\eta_{r}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}.

Simplifying it and using (
6.10
), we obtain

\int_{\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}}|\nabla\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}|^{2}\cdot\eta_{r}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\leq C\int_{\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}^{2}(\eta_{r}^{2}+|\nabla\eta_{r}|^{2})+(c+1)\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}.

Taking
k\to\infty
, it follows that

\int_{\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\cdot\eta_{r}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\leq C\int_{\Sigma\setminus\mathcal{Y}}\varphi^{2}(\eta_{r}^{2}+|\nabla\eta_{r}|^{2})+1\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}.

Recall that
\varphi
is uniformly bounded. Hence, as
r\to 0
, (
6.17
) follows immediately. This completes the proof of Proposition
6.4
.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

Let
\Sigma_{k}
be the same as in Proposition
6.4
. Suppose that the limit surface
\Sigma
is one-sided. Then by the same argument, the connected double cover
\widetilde{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
admits a non-negative function
\varphi\in W^{1,2}(\widetilde{\Sigma})
satisfying (
6.16
).

7.
Multiplicity one for Simon-Smith min-max theory

In this section, we will prove two multiplicity one theorems in the Simon-Smith setting, that is, for relative min-max in the space of oriented separating surfaces in Section
7.1
, and for the classical min-max in the space of un-oriented surfaces in Section
7.2
.

7.1.
Multiplicity one for relative Simon-Smith min-max

In this part, we will show how to choose the correct prescribing function
h
so as prove the first multiplicity one type result. Recall that the space
\mathscr{E}
of embedded separating surfaces of genus
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is defined in (
2.1
).

We have the following compactness for minimal surfaces with bounded area and satisfying Property
{\bf(R^{\prime})}
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
L
be a positive integer and
\Lambda>0
be a constant. Let
\Sigma_{k}
be a sequence of closed, embedded, minimal surfaces satisfying

•

\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma_{k})\leq\Lambda
, and

•

Property
(R’)
(
5.1
) (with
\Sigma_{k}
in place of
V_{\infty}
and
L
given in the assumption).

Then
\Sigma_{k}
converges subsequentially to a closed, embedded, minimal surface
\Sigma
possibly counted with integer multiplicity in the sense of varifolds. Furthermore, if
\Sigma_{k}\neq\Sigma
for infinitely many
k
, then
\Sigma
is degenerate.

Proof.

The proof of convergence is the same as that of Theorem
5.2
. Indeed, we know that away from a finite set of points
\mathcal{Y}
, the convergence
\Sigma_{k}\to\Sigma_{\infty}
is locally smooth. If
\Sigma_{k}\neq\Sigma
for infinitely many
k
, then one can construct a nontrivial Jacobi field along
\Sigma_{\infty}
in the same way as
[
40
]
, and hence
\Sigma_{\infty}
is degenerate.
∎

Theorem 7.2

(Multiplicity one for relative min-max)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a three dimensional closed Riemannian manifold. Let
X\subset I(m,k_{0})
be a cubical complex and
Z\subset X
be a subcomplex. Let
\Phi_{0}:X\to\mathscr{E}
be a continuous map and
\Pi
be the
(X,Z)
-homotopy class of
\Phi_{0}
. Assume that

\mathbf{L}(\Pi)>\max_{x\in Z}\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(\Phi_{0}(x)\big).

Then there exists a pairwise disjoint collection of connected, closed, smoothly embedded, minimal surfaces
\Gamma=\cup_{i=1}^{N}\Gamma_{i}
and positive integers
\{m_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
, so that

\mathbf{L}(\Pi)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma_{i}).

and

(1)

if
\Gamma_{i}
is two-sided and unstable, then
m_{i}=1
;

(2)

if
\Gamma_{i}
is one-sided, then the connected double cover of
\Gamma_{i}
is stable.

Furthermore, if
M
is orientable, then

(7.1)

\sum_{i\in I_{O}}m_{i}\mathfrak{g}(\Gamma_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in I_{U}}m_{i}(\mathfrak{g}(\Gamma_{i})-1)\leq\mathfrak{g}_{0},

where
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is the genus associated with
\mathscr{E}
, and
I_{O}
(resp.
I_{U}
) is the collection of
i
such that
\Gamma_{i}
is orientable (resp. non-orientable).

Proof.

Suppose that
(M,g)
is bumpy. Then for a given constant
\Lambda
(e.g.
\Lambda:=\mathbf{L}(\Pi)+1
), let
\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
be the collection of closed embedded minimal surfaces
\Gamma
satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma)\leq\Lambda
and Property
(R’)
(
5.1
) for
L=L(m)
. Note that by Theorem
7.1
,
\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)
is a finite set since
g
is bumpy. Denote by
\{S_{1},\cdots,S_{\alpha}\}
the collection of those embedded minimal surfaces. Then we take
p_{1},\cdots,p_{\alpha}
and
q_{1},\cdots,q_{\alpha}
in
M
so that
p_{i},q_{i}\in S_{j}
if and only if
j=i
. Let
r>0
be a small number so that

•

B_{r}(p_{1}),\cdots,B_{r}(p_{\alpha})
,
B_{r}(q_{1}),\cdots,B_{r}(q_{\alpha})
are pairwise disjoint;

•

B_{r}(p_{i})\cup B_{r}(q_{i})
intersects
S_{j}
if and only if
j=i
;

•

B_{r}(p_{i})\cap S_{i}
and
B_{r}(q_{i})\cap S_{i}
are both embedded disks for all
i=1,\cdots,\alpha
.

Next we take a smooth function
h:M\to\mathbb{[}-1,1]
satisfying that for all
i=1,\cdots,\alpha
,

(1)

h=0
outside
\cup_{i}(B_{r}(p_{i})\cup B_{r}(q_{i}))
;

(2)

h>0
in
B_{r/2}(p_{i})\cap S_{i}
and
h<0
in
B_{r/2}(q_{i})\cap S_{i}
;

(3)

if
S_{i}
is two-sided, then
\int_{S_{i}}h\phi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}=0
, where
\phi_{i}
is the first eigenfunction of the Jacobi operator on
S_{i}
;

(4)

if
S_{i}
is one-sided, then
\int_{\widetilde{S}_{i}}h\phi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}=0
, where
\phi_{i}
is the first eigenfunction of the Jacobi operator on
\widetilde{S}_{i}
and
\widetilde{S}_{i}
is the connected double cover of
S_{i}
.

Now we choose
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
. Then for sufficiently large
k
, we have

\mathbf{L}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}(\Pi)>\max\left\{\max_{x\in Z}\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}\big(\Phi_{0}(x)\big),0\right\}.

Applying Theorem
2.4
to the
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}
-functional for each
k
, we obtain a min-max pair
(V_{k},\Omega_{k})\in\VC(M)
, which is a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{k}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}

(\varepsilon_{k}h)
-boundary with
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}(V_{k},\Omega_{k})=\mathbf{L}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}(\Pi)
. By Theorem
5.2
, up to subsequence,
V_{k}
(not relabelled) converges as varifolds to
V_{\infty}
with
V_{\infty}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}[\Gamma_{i}]
, where
\{\Gamma_{i}\}
is pairwise disjoint collection of connected, closed, embedded, minimal surfaces. Since
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbf{L}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}(\Pi)=\mathbf{L}(\Pi),

which yields that
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma_{i})\leq\Lambda
. Furthermore, by the discussion before Proposition
5.1
, we know that
\Gamma_{\infty}=\cup\Gamma_{i}
satisfies Property
(R’)
(
5.1
). Hence
\Gamma_{i}
is one of
S_{1},\cdots,S_{\alpha}
. By relabelling, we assume that
\Gamma_{i}=S_{i}
for
i=1,\cdots,N
. Observe that by the construction of
h
, the sign of
h
changes on
\Gamma_{i}
. Since the convergence is locally
C^{1,1}
away from finitely many points, and
(V_{k},\Omega_{k})
is strongly
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{k}h}
