-stationary, then by Proposition
6.4
, any two-sided connected component
\Gamma_{i}
with multiplicity
m_{i}\geq 2
admits a nontrivial and nonnegative
\varphi\in W^{1,2}(\Gamma_{i})
such that

\int_{\Gamma_{i}}\langle\nabla\varphi,\nabla f\rangle-\big(\Ric(\nu,\nu)+|A^{\Gamma_{i}}|^{2}\big)\varphi f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}\geq\int_{\Gamma_{i}}2chf\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n},\ \forall f\in C^{1}(\Gamma_{i})\text{ and }f\geq 0,

for some constant
c\geq 0
. Let
\phi_{i}
be the first eigenfunction of the Jacobi operator of
\Gamma_{i}
. By the choice of
h
,

\displaystyle 0=\int_{\Gamma_{i}}2ch\phi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\leq\int_{\Gamma_{i}}\varphi L_{\Gamma_{i}}\phi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}=\lambda_{1}(\Gamma_{i})\int_{\Gamma_{i}}\varphi\phi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}.

Recall that
\phi_{i}>0
everywhere and
\varphi\geq 0
with
\varphi>0
somewhere. It follows that
\int_{\Gamma_{i}}\varphi\phi_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}>0
. Thus we conclude that the first eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}(\Gamma_{i})\geq 0
, that is, if
\Gamma_{i}
is two-sided and
m_{i}\geq 2
, then
\Gamma_{i}
is stable. This proves the first item.

For one-sided connected component
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\spt\|V_{\infty}\|
, the same argument gives that the double cover of
\Gamma^{\prime}
is stable.

Note that by the choice of
h
, for each
\Gamma_{i}
, we have that
h=0
outside two disjoint balls
B_{r}(p_{i})
and
B_{r}(q_{i})
. Moreover,
B_{r}(p_{i})\cap\Gamma_{i}
and
B_{r}(q_{i})\cap\Gamma_{i}
are both disks. Hence the desired genus bound follows from Theorem
5.4
.

For the general case when
g
is not bumpy, one can take a sequence of bumpy metrics
g_{i}
converging to
g
in the sense of
C^{3}
. Then the theorem follows from the conclusion for bumpy metrics, Property
(R’)
, and standard compactness theorem of closed embedded minimal surfaces; see Theorem
7.1
or
[
40
]
.
∎

7.2.
Multiplicity one for classical Simon-Smith min-max theory

In this subsection, we use Theorem
7.2
together with the double cover lifting argument in
[
54
]
to prove a multiplicity one type theorem for the classical Simon-Smith min-max theory
[
42
,
6
]
. Here we will use the version of Simon-Smith theory for un-oriented smoothly embedded surfaces.

Let
(M,g)
be a three dimensional closed manifold and
\Sigma_{0}
be a connected closed surface of genus
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. Then denote

\mathscr{X}(\Sigma_{0}):=\{\phi(\Sigma_{0})\big|\phi:\Sigma_{0}\to M\text{ is a smooth separating embedding}\},

and

\mathscr{Y}(\Sigma_{0}):=\{\phi(\Sigma_{0})\big|\phi:\Sigma\to M\text{ is a smooth map whose image is a 0-or 1-dimensional graph}\}.

For simplicity, we denote

\overline{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})=\mathscr{X}(\Sigma_{0})\cup\mathscr{Y}(\Sigma_{0}).

We endow
\overline{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
with the un-oriented smooth topology for immersions. We sometime simply write
\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y},\overline{\mathscr{X}}
when there is no ambiguity.

Let
X\subset I(m,k_{0})
be a cubical complex, and
Z_{0}\subset X
a subcomplex.
Fix a continuous map:

\Phi_{0}:X\to\overline{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0}),\text{ such that }\Phi_{0}(Z_{0})\subset\mathscr{Y}(\Sigma_{0}).

We let
\Pi
be the set of all continuous maps
\Phi:X\to\overline{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
which is homotopic to
\Phi_{0}
relative to
\Phi_{0}|_{Z_{0}}
. We call such
\Phi

an
(X,Z_{0})
-sweepout by
\Sigma_{0}
, or simply
a sweepout
. Note that we always have

\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(\Phi(x)\big)=0,\quad\text{for any }x\in Z_{0}.

Define

\mathbf{L}(\Pi):=\inf_{\Phi\in\Pi}\sup_{x\in X}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Phi(x)).

Note that
\overline{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
or
{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
endowed with the oriented smooth topology (see (
2.1
)), denoted as
\widetilde{\overline{\mathscr{X}}}(\Sigma_{0})
or
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
, forms a nontrivial double cover over
\overline{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
or
{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
. Denote
\overline{\lambda}\in H^{1}\big(\overline{\mathscr{X}},\mathbb{Z}_{2}\big)
as the dual to the nontrivial element of
\pi_{1}(\overline{\mathscr{X}})
coming from the projection
\bm{\pi}:\widetilde{\overline{\mathscr{X}}}\to\overline{\mathscr{X}}
. Note that given any
\phi:S^{1}=[0,1]/\{0\sim 1\}\to\overline{\mathscr{X}}
,
\overline{\lambda}[\phi]\neq 0
if and only if the lifting of
\widetilde{\phi}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{\overline{\mathscr{X}}}
satisfies that
\widetilde{\phi}(1)
is
\widetilde{\phi}(0)
with the opposite orientation. In this case
\phi
forms a sweepout of
M
in the sense of Almgren-Pitts
[
30
]
*Definition 3.4.

Theorem 7.3

(Theorem
B
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed three dimensional Riemannian manifold. Suppose that
\Pi
is a homotopy class of
(X,Z_{0})
-sweepouts by
\Sigma_{0}
with

\mathbf{L}(\Pi)>\sup_{x\in Z_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(\Phi_{0}(x)\big)=0.

Then there exist a pairwise disjoint collection of connected, closed, embedded, minimal surfaces
\{\Gamma_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
and positive integers
m_{j}
so that

\mathbf{L}(\Pi)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}m_{j}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma_{j}),

and

(1)

if
\Gamma_{j}
is unstable and two-sided, then
m_{j}=1
;

(2)

if
\Gamma_{j}
is one-sided, then the connected double cover of
\Gamma_{i}
is stable.

Furthermore, if
M
is orientable, then the genus bound (
7.1
) holds with
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{g}(\Sigma_{0})
.

Proof.

The proof will follow in the same structure as the proof of
[
54
]
*Theorem 5.2. As we are using continuous sweepouts, the arguments here are simpler as compared with
[
54
]
. For sake of completeness, we will provide necessary details. Note that all notations related to min-max construction in Section
2
are valid in the current setting, that is,
h\equiv 0
.

We can assume that
g
is bumpy, and the general case follows by approximation in the same was as in Theorem
7.2
.

Following the same procedure as Theorem
2.7
, Theorem
3.8
and Theorem
2.4
, we can find a pull-tight minimizing sequence
\{\Phi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Pi
, such that every
V\in\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})
is stationary (for area), and moreover, if
V\in\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})
is almost minimizing (for area) in every
L=L(m)
-admissible collection of annuli w.r.t. some min-max subsequence, then
V
has support a closed, smoothly embedded, minimal surface
\Sigma
with
\|V\|(M)=\mathbf{L}(\Pi)
, and satisfies Property
(R’)
(
5.1
), that is, give any
L
-admissble collection of annuli
\mathscr{C}
,
\Sigma
is stable in at least one of them.
Lemma
3.10
implies such
V
always exists.

Step 1. We will do an extra tightening process to find another minimizing sequence, still denoted as
\{\Phi_{i}\}
, such that for
i
sufficiently large, either
\Phi_{i}(x)
is close to a smooth min-max minimal surface, or the area
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Phi_{i}(x))
is strictly less than
{\mathbf{L}}(\Pi)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the collection of all stationary 2-varifolds with mass lying in
[\mathbf{L}(\Pi)-1,\mathbf{L}(\Pi)+1]
, whose support is a closed, smoothly embedded, minimal surface satisfying Property
(R’)
(
5.1
) for
L=L(m)
. By the bumpiness assumption,
\mathcal{S}
is a finite set.

Choose a small
\overline{\epsilon}>0
, and let

Z_{i}=\{x\in X:\mathbf{F}(\Phi_{i}(x),\mathcal{S})\geq\overline{\epsilon}\},\text{ and }Y_{i}=\closure(X\setminus Z_{i}).

Clearly we can make
Z_{0}\subset Z_{i}
for
\overline{\epsilon}
small enough. Consider the sub-coordinating sequence
\{\Phi_{i}|_{Z_{i}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then we can define
\mathbf{L}(\{\Phi_{i}|_{Z_{i}}\})
and
\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}|_{Z_{i}}\})
in the same way as in Section
2.1
.

Lemma 7.4

(c.f.
[
54
]
*Lemma 5.7)
.

We have the following dichotomy:

i)

either no element
V\in\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}|_{Z_{i}}\})
is almost minimizing in every
L=L(m)
-admissible collection of annuli w.r.t. some min-max subsequence,

ii)

or
\mathbf{L}(\{\Phi_{i}|_{Z_{i}}\})<\mathbf{L}(\Pi)
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as
[
54
]
*Lemma 5.7 using the pull-tight and min-max regularity results, so we omit it.
∎

Let
\lambda_{i}=\Phi_{i}^{*}(\overline{\lambda})\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. Note that
\Phi_{i}(Y_{i})
lies in the
\overline{\epsilon}
-neighborhood of a finite set
\mathcal{S}
(in the
\mathbf{F}
-metric). When
\overline{\epsilon}
is small enough, we know that no continuous map
\phi
from
S^{1}
to such a neighborhood can form a sweepout of
M
, and hence must satisfy:
\overline{\lambda}[\phi]=0
by the discussion above this theorem.

Consider the inclusion maps
\iota_{i}:Y_{i}\to X
. When
\overline{\epsilon}
is small enough, we must have

(7.2)

\iota_{i}^{*}(\lambda_{i})=0\in H^{1}(Y_{i},\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

Lemma 7.5
.

As above, there exists another minimizing sequence
\{\Phi^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\Phi^{\prime}_{i}
homotopic to
\Phi_{i}
for each
i
, such that Lemma
7.4
(ii) holds true.

Proof.

We may assume
\mathbf{L}(\{\Phi_{i}|_{Z_{i}}\})=\mathbf{L}(\Pi)
, then Lemma
7.4
(i) must be true, that is, no element
V\in\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}|_{Z_{i}}\})
is almost minimizing in every
L=L(m)
-admissible collection of annuli w.r.t. some min-max subsequence. By the same argument in the proof Lemma
3.10
, we can find
\epsilon_{0}>0
, such that for any
\delta>0
,
i>1/\epsilon_{0}
, and any
x\in Z_{i}
satisfying:

\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(\Phi_{i}(x)\big)\geq\mathbf{L}(\Pi)-\epsilon_{0},

there exists an
L
-admissible collection
\mathscr{C}_{i,x}
such that
\Phi_{i}(x)
is not
(\epsilon_{0},\delta)
-almost minimizing in any annulus in
\mathscr{C}_{i,x}
. We can then follow the same deformation process as in the proof of Lemma
3.10
to deform
\Phi_{i}
using isotopies to some
\Phi^{\prime}_{i}:X\to\overline{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
, such that

\sup_{x\in Z_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(\Phi^{\prime}_{i}(x)\big)\leq\sup_{x\in Z_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(\Phi_{i}(x)\big)-\epsilon_{0}/4<\mathbf{L}(\Pi),

for
i_{0}
sufficiently large. This completes the proof.
∎

Since
\Phi_{i}\to\Phi^{\prime}_{i}
is a homotopic deformation, we know that (
7.2
) still holds true for
\Phi^{\prime}_{i}
.

Note that
\Phi_{i}(Y_{i})
lies in an
\overline{\epsilon}
-neighborhood of
\mathcal{S}
under the
\mathbf{F}
-metric, so each
\Phi_{i}(x)
,
x\in Y_{i}
, has area bounded uniformly away from zero, and hence
\Phi_{i}(Y_{i})
lies inside
\mathscr{X}(\Sigma_{0})
; (that is,
\Phi_{i}(Y_{i})
does not contain degenerate graphs). The deformation process used isotopies, so
\Phi_{i}^{\prime}(Y_{i})
also lies inside
\mathscr{X}(\Sigma_{0})
.

In the following, we will still write
\Phi^{\prime}_{i}
as
\Phi_{i}
.

Step 2. For
i
large enough, we can lift the maps
\Phi_{i}:Y_{i}\to{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
to its double cover
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i}:Y_{i}\to\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
, the existence of which is guaranteed by (
7.2
).

Note that
\partial Y_{i}\subset Z_{i}
, so we have
\sup_{x\in\partial Y_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(\widetilde{\Phi}_{i}(x)\big)<\mathbf{L}(\Pi)
for
i
large enough.
We claim that

Claim 3

(c.f.
[
54
]
*Lemma 5.8)
.

Let
\widetilde{\Pi}_{i}
be the
(Y_{i},\partial Y_{i})
-homotopy class associated with
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i}|_{Y_{i}}
in
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
, then we must have

{\mathbf{L}}(\widetilde{\Pi}_{i})\geq{\mathbf{L}}(\Pi)>\sup_{x\in\partial Y_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(\widetilde{\Phi}_{i}(x)\big).

The proof is essentially the same as that of
[
54
]
*Lemma 5.8, so we omit it.

Step 3. We apply Theorem
7.2
to
\widetilde{\Pi}_{i}
and finish the proof by taking
i\to\infty
.

Note that
\widetilde{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
can be identified with our total space
\mathscr{E}
(
2.1
) in Section
2.1
. Applying Theorem
7.2
to each
\widetilde{\Pi}_{i}
, we obtain a disjoint collection
\Gamma_{i}=\cup_{j=1}^{N_{i}}m_{i,j}\Gamma_{i,j}
of connected, closed, smoothly embedded, minimal surfaces with integer multiplicity
m_{i,j}
, satisfying all conclusions in Theorem
7.2
. By the proof therein,
\Gamma_{i}
also satisfies Property
(R’)
(
5.1
) for all
i
. Note that
{\mathbf{L}}(\widetilde{\Pi}_{i})\leq\sup_{x\in Y_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Phi_{i}(x))\to{\mathbf{L}}(\Pi)
. Since there are only finitely many such
\Gamma_{i}
, we know that for
i
sufficiently large
{\mathbf{L}}(\widetilde{\Pi}_{i})={\mathbf{L}}(\widetilde{\Pi}_{i+1})=\cdots={\mathbf{L}}(\Pi)
.
Hence we finish the proof of Theorem
7.3
.
∎

8.
Existence of minimal spheres

In this section, we apply the multiplicity one theorem in Riemannian three-spheres
(M,g)
and prove the existence of four distinct embedded minimal two-spheres if
(M,g)
does not contain degenerate-and-stable minimal two-spheres. Without loss of generality, we always assume that
(M,g)
contains only finitely many embedded minimal two-spheres.

Note that the three-sphere admits a nontrivial homotopy class of
[-1,1]\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.23608pt\hskip-4.23608pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}\hskip-4.23608pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{5.63335pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 4.23608pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.81944pt\hskip-3.81944pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}\hskip-3.81944pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{4.83336pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 3.81944pt}}}\mathbb{RP}^{k-1}
-sweepouts of two-spheres for each
k=1,2,3,4
. (Here
[-1,1]\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.23608pt\hskip-4.23608pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}\hskip-4.23608pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{5.63335pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 4.23608pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.81944pt\hskip-3.81944pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}\hskip-3.81944pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{4.83336pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 3.81944pt}}}\mathbb{RP}^{k-1}
is the twisted interval bundle over
\mathbb{RP}^{k-1}
.) If
(M,g)
has no stable minimal two-spheres, the Multiplicity One Theorem
7.3
applies directly to give an embedded minimal two-sphere for each homotopy class of
[-1,1]\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.23608pt\hskip-4.23608pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}\hskip-4.23608pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{5.63335pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 4.23608pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.81944pt\hskip-3.81944pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}\hskip-3.81944pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{4.83336pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 3.81944pt}}}\mathbb{RP}^{k-1}
-sweepouts. The well-known Lusternik–Schnirelmann theory implies that the min-max widths are strictly increasing in a bumpy metric, and hence these are distinct two-spheres. We will provide more details for this part in Section
8.3
.

If
(M,g)
contains stable minimal-two spheres, we will follow the strategy due to A. Song
[
44
]
to cut
M
along a disjoint collection of stable minimal two-spheres and consider the remaining compact manifolds glued with cylindrical ends. To develop the min-max theory in such a non-compact manifold, we approximate it by a sequence of compact domains with mean concave boundary and generalize the work of M. Li
[
22
]
for free boundary minimal surfaces with controlled topology.

In Section
8.1
, we introduce some notations from
[
22
]
and generalize our PMC min-max Theorem
2.4
to the free boundary setting when the prescribing function is supported in the interior of the compact manifolds. In Section
8.2
, we present the construction of non-compact manifolds with cylindrical ends and sequences of compact manifolds approximating them. Moreover, we will also prove that as the compact manifolds having enough long “tails”, the free boundary min-max theory will produce closed minimal surfaces with genus bounds. Next we prove the uniform upper bound of the first two widths in those compact manifolds in Section
8.4
. Finally, Section
8.5
is devoted to the proof of the main theorem.

8.1.
Min-max theory in compact manifolds with boundary

We now recall the min-max theory for free boundary minimal surfaces with controlled topology by M. Li
[
22
]
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian three-sphere and
N\subset M
be a compact domain with smooth boundary
\partial N
. Denote by
\mathfrak{Is}
the space of all isotopies on
M
. Define

\mathfrak{Is}^{\mathrm{out}}:=\big\{\{\varphi_{s}\}\in\mathfrak{Is};N\subset\varphi_{s}(N)\text{ for all }s\in[0,1]\big\}

to be the isotopies in
M
that can push points out of the compact set
N
, but not into
N
. Given an open subset
U\subset M
, we define
\mathfrak{Is}^{\mathrm{out}}(U)
to be those in
\mathfrak{Is}^{\mathrm{out}}
that is supported in
U
.

We will use Theorem
2.4
and the regularity theorem in
[
22
]
*Theorem 4.7 to prove that for such a compact manifold
N\subset M
, the relative min-max theory will produce free boundary minimal surfaces. We now generalize some notions to their free boundary counterparts.

Let
h:N\to\mathbb{R}
be a fixed smooth function, such that

h=0
in a neighborhood of
\partial N
.

Recall that
X\subset I(m,k_{0})
is a cubical complex and
Z\subset X
is a subcomplex, and
\mathscr{E}
is the space of embedded separating surfaces of genus
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
in
M
(
2.1
). A continuous map
\psi:X\to C^{\infty}(M,M)
is said to be
outward isotopic deformation
, if for each
x\in X
, there exists an outward isotopy
\{\varphi_{x,s}\}_{s\in[0,1]}\in\mathfrak{Is}^{\mathrm{out}}
such that
\psi_{x}:=\psi(x)
is equal to
\varphi_{x,1}
. Let
\Phi_{0}:X\to\mathscr{E}
be a continuous map. A family
\bm{\Xi}
of
(X,Z)
-sweepouts homotopic to
\Phi_{0}
relative to
\Phi_{0}|_{Z}
is said to be
saturated
, if for any
\Phi\in\bm{\Xi}
, and any outward isotopic deformation
\psi:X\to C^{\infty}(M,M)
with
\psi|_{Z}=\mathrm{Id}
,

\Phi^{\prime}(x):=(\psi_{x})_{\#}\Phi(x)\quad\text{also belongs to $\bm{\Xi}$.}

To produce free boundary solutions in
N
, we will only count the area and volume restricted to
N
. Precisely, given
(V,\Omega)\in\VC(M)
, we define

\mathcal{A}^{h}_{N}(V,\Omega)=\|V\|(N)-\int_{\Omega\cap N}h\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}.

For any saturated family
\bm{\Xi}
of
(X,Z)
-sweepouts, we define

\mathbf{L}^{h}_{N}(\bm{\Xi})=\inf_{\Phi\in\bm{\Xi}}\max_{x\in X}\mathcal{A}^{h}_{N}\big(\Phi(x)\big).

We also use
\mathcal{A}^{h}_{N}(V,\Omega;g)
and
\mathbf{L}^{h}_{N}(\bm{\Xi};g)
to indicate the metrics. We can adapt the notions related to min-max construction in Definition
2.2
in a straightforward manner to this setting. Given a fixed
\bm{\Xi}
, we can similarly define
minimizing sequences, min-max subsequences, and critical sets
. In particular, the critical set of a minimizing sequence
\{\Phi_{i}\}\subset\bm{\Xi}
is defined by

\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})=\left\{(V,\Omega)\in\VC(N)\left|\begin{aligned} &\ \exists\text{ a min-max subsequence }\{\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\}\text{ such }\\
&\text{ that }\mathscr{F}\big(\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}N,(V,\Omega)\big)\to 0\text{ as }j\to\infty\end{aligned}\right\}\right..

Note that the
\mathscr{F}
-metric is defined on
N
.

We can also extend the notion of
C^{1,1}
-almost embedded surfaces in Section
1.2
to the current setting. Let
\Sigma
be a compact smooth surface (2-dimensional manifold) with smooth boundary
\partial\Sigma
. A
C^{1,1}
-immersion
\phi:\Sigma\to N
with
\phi(\partial\Sigma)\subset\partial N
is called
almost embedded
if Definition
1.8
holds in the interior
\interior(N)
, and
[
21
]
*Definition 2.6
5
5

5

[
21
]
*Definition 2.6 refers to the notion of almost proper embedding, which says that
\Sigma
is an embedding into
M
near
\partial N
,
\phi(\partial\Sigma)\subset\partial N
, but
\phi(\interior(\Sigma))
may touch
\partial N
.
holds near
\partial N
. We will simply write
\phi(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma)
as
(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma)
. Similarly, we can define
C^{1,1}
boundary (c.f. Definition
1.10
) and
C^{1,1}
free
h
-boundary
(c.f. Definition
1.13
). To be precise, a
C^{1,1}
almost embedded surface
(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma)\subset(N,\partial N)
is called a
boundary
if there exists
\Omega\in\mathcal{C}(N)
, such that
\Sigma\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}\interior(N)=\partial\Omega\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}\interior(N)
6
6

6

Since
h=0
near
\partial N
, we only need this boundary structure in the interior
\interior(N)
.
, and a triple
(\Sigma,\partial\Sigma,\Omega)
is called a
free
h
-boundary
if for any vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(N,\Sigma)
7
7

7

This means
X(q)\in T_{q}(\partial N)
for all
q
in a neighborhood of
\partial\Sigma
in
\partial N
.
(see
[
21
]
*page 501), we have
\delta\mathcal{A}^{h}_{N}|_{\Sigma,\Omega}(X)=0
. By the first variation formula,
\Sigma
must meet
\partial N
orthogonally along
\partial\Sigma
, and
\interior(\Sigma)
is minimal near
\partial N
; see
[
21
]
*Definition 2.8. Note that since the touching set
\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
(where
\Sigma
touches with itself) lies in the interior
\interior(N)
, we can extend the
strong
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationarity
(Definition
1.15
) without any change to this setting.

We have the following extension of
[
22
]
*Theorem 4.7. Note that the proper embeddedness in the regularity statement claimed in
[
22
]
*Theorem 4.7 is incorrect; this part has been corrected in
[
21
]
*Theorem 5.2, and the corresponding statement is almost proper embeddedness.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
\bm{\Xi}
be a saturated family of
(X,Z)
-sweepouts relative to
\Phi_{0}|_{Z}
. Suppose

\mathbf{L}^{h}_{N}(\bm{\Xi})>\max\left\{\max_{x\in Z}\mathcal{A}^{h}_{N}\big(\Phi_{0}(x)\big),0\right\}.

Then there exist a minimizing sequence
\{\Phi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\bm{\Xi}
, and a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}
, free
h
-boundary
(\Sigma,\Omega)
lying in the critical set
\mathbf{C}(\{\Phi_{i}\})
, that is,
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is the
\mathscr{F}
-limit of some min-max subsequence
\{\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.75}{$\displaystyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\textstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptstyle\llcorner$}}{\scalebox{1.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llcorner$}}N\}
, and

\mathcal{A}^{h}_{N}(\Sigma,\Omega)=\mathbf{L}^{h}_{N}(\bm{\Xi}).

Moreover, the min-max sequence
\{\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
can be chosen so that there exist
\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j}\to 0
such that
\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing in any
L(m)
-admissible collection of annuli
\mathrm{An}(p;s_{1},r_{1}),\cdots,\mathrm{An}(p;s_{L},r_{L})\subset N\setminus\partial N
.

Proof.

The existence of the desired minimizing sequence follows by adapting to the free boundary setting the tightening process in Section
2.2
(in a similar way as
[
22
]
*Proposition 5.1) and the existence of almost minimizing pairs in Theorem
3.8
. Indeed, for any
L(m)
-admissible collection of annuli in
N\setminus\partial N
, the min-max sequence can be chosen so that
\Phi_{i_{j}}(x_{j})
is
(\mathcal{A}^{h},\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
-almost minimizing in at least one of them.

Now the interior regularity follows from Theorem
2.4
.

It remains to consider the regularity around
\partial N
. Note that
h\equiv 0
in a neighborhood of
\partial N
. Then by the free boundary min-max theory
[
22
]
*Theorem 4.7 and
[
21
]
*Theorem 5.2,
\Sigma
is a almost embedded free boundary minimal surface in a neighborhood of
\partial N
.
∎

8.2.
Construction of non-compact manifolds with cylindrical ends

We recall the construction of non-compact manifolds with cylindrical ends by A. Song in
[
44
]
*Section 2.2. Let
(N,\partial N,g)
be a compact three dimensional Riemannian manifold such that
\partial N
is a closed, embedded, stable minimal surface with
a contracting neighborhood
, that is, there is a map

\varphi:\partial N\times[0,\hat{t}]\to N,

so that
\varphi
is a diffeomorphism to its image,
\varphi(\partial N\times\{0\})=\partial N
, and for all
t\in(0,\hat{t}]
,
\varphi(\partial N\times\{t\})
has non-zero mean curvature vector
8
8

8

Here the mean curvature vector is defined as
-\mathrm{div}(\nu)\nu
for a choice of unit normal
\nu
.
pointing towards
\partial N
. We endow
\partial N\times[0,+\infty)
with the product metric. Let
\mathscr{C}(N)
be the non-compact manifold

N\cup(\partial N\times[0,+\infty))

by identifying
\partial N
with
\partial N\times\{0\}
. We endow it with the metric
\hat{g}
such that
\hat{g}=g
on
N
and is equal to the product metric on
\partial N\times[0,\infty)
. Note that
\hat{g}
is Lipschitz continuous.

Now we approximate
\mathscr{C}(N)
by compact manifolds as follows. Let
N_{\epsilon}:=N\setminus\varphi(\partial N\times[0,\epsilon))
. Denote by
\nu
the unit outward normal vector field of
\partial N_{\epsilon}
. For a small constant
\delta_{\epsilon}>0
, the map

\gamma_{\epsilon}:\partial N_{\epsilon}\times[-\delta_{\epsilon},0]\to N_{\epsilon},\quad(x,t)\longmapsto\exp(x,t\nu)

is well-defined and gives Fermi coordinates on one side of
\partial N_{\epsilon}
. Then by A. Song
[
44
]
*Section 2.2, there exist smooth metrics
g_{\epsilon}
on
N_{\epsilon}
satisfying Lemma 4, Lemma 5 and Lemma 7 in
[
44
]
, such that
(N_{\epsilon},g_{\epsilon})
approaches
(\mathscr{C}(N),\hat{g})
in appropriate sense. In particular,

(i)

g_{\epsilon}=g
in
N_{\epsilon}\setminus\gamma_{\epsilon}(\partial N_{\epsilon}\times[-\delta_{\epsilon},0])
;

(ii)

for
t\in[-\delta_{\epsilon},0]
, the slices
\gamma_{\epsilon}(\partial N_{\epsilon}\times\{t\})
have non-zero mean curvature vector pointing towards
\partial N_{\epsilon}
with respect to the new metric
g_{\epsilon}
;

(iii)

\gamma_{\epsilon}^{*}(g_{\epsilon})=\gamma_{\epsilon}^{*}(g)
on
\partial N_{\epsilon}\times\{t\}
for all
t\in[-\delta_{\epsilon},0]
.

Assume that
(N,\partial N,g)
is isometrically embedded into a closed three-manifold
(M,\widetilde{g})
. Then one can extend the metric
g_{\epsilon}
to a metric
\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon}
on
M
so that
\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon}=g_{\epsilon}
on
N_{\epsilon}
.

Given a continuous
\Phi_{0}:X\to\overline{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
(see Section
7.2
for notations), we can similarly define saturated families
\bm{\Xi}
of
(X,Z_{0})
-sweepouts in
\overline{\mathscr{X}}(\Sigma_{0})
homotopic to
\Phi_{0}
w.r.t. outward isotopic deformations for
N_{\epsilon}
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\epsilon_{k}\to 0
be a sequence of positive constants. With notions as above, we use
N_{k}
and
\widetilde{g}_{k}
to denote
N_{\epsilon_{k}}
and
\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon_{k}}
for simplicity. Suppose that

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\bm{\Xi};\widetilde{g}_{k})<\infty.

Then up to a subsequence, for sufficiently large
k
, there exist a collection of pairwise disjoint, connected, closed, embedded, minimal surfaces
\Gamma^{k}_{1},\cdots,\Gamma^{k}_{I_{k}}\subset(N\setminus\partial N,g)
and positive integers
m^{k}_{1},\cdots,m^{k}_{I_{k}}
, such that

\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\bm{\Xi};\widetilde{g}_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}m^{k}_{i}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma^{k}_{i}),

where
m^{k}_{i}=1
if
\Gamma^{k}_{i}
is unstable. Moreover, the varifold
\sum[\Gamma^{k}_{i}]
satisfies Property
(R’)
(
5.1
) for any
L(m)
-admissible collection of annuli in
N_{k}\setminus\partial N_{k}
.

Furthermore, if
M
is orientable, then the genus bound (
7.1
) holds with
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{g}(\Sigma_{0})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that for all
k
,

\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\bm{\Xi};\widetilde{g}_{k})\leq\Lambda_{0}<\infty.

We can assume that
\widetilde{g}_{k}
is bumpy, and the general case follows by approximating
\widetilde{g}_{k}
by bumpy metrics.
Denote by
\mathscr{D}_{k}:=\{S_{1},\cdots,S_{\beta_{k}}\}
the collection of connected, closed, embedded, minimal surface
S
in
(N_{k}\setminus\partial N_{k},\widetilde{g}_{k})
satisfying

\mathcal{H}^{2}(S)\leq\Lambda_{0}+1,

and Property
(R’)
(
5.1
) for
L=L(m)
.
Then using the monotonicity formula for minimal surfaces
[
44
]
*Lemma 2 and the mean concave foliation near
\partial N_{k}
, we know that there exists
d_{0}>0
independent of
k
, such that

\cup_{j=1}^{\beta_{k}}S_{j}\subset B(p,d_{0};\widetilde{g}_{k}),

where
p
is a fixed point in
N\setminus\partial N
.

Now we claim that one can adapt the argument in Theorem
7.3
to prove that for all sufficiently large
k
, there exists a stationary varifold
V_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}m^{k}_{i}[\Gamma^{k}_{i}]
that achieves
\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\bm{\Xi};\widetilde{g}_{k})
, where
\Gamma^{k}_{i}\in\mathscr{D}_{k}
. Indeed, by the same arguments in Step 1 and Step 2 in the proof of Theorem
7.3
, we have that for each sufficiently large
k
, one can approximate
\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\bm{\Xi};\widetilde{g}_{k})
by a sequence of widths of relative sweepouts of separating surfaces. Then it suffices to adapt the argument in Theorem
7.2
to the current free boundary settings.

We now indicate the modification in Theorem
7.2
. By the same strategy, we will choose a suitable prescribing function
h
on
M
and then approximate
\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\bm{\Xi};\widetilde{g}_{k})
by
\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}^{\varepsilon_{j}h}(\bm{\Xi};\widetilde{g}_{k})
. Note that the minimal surfaces in
\mathscr{D}_{k}
are all closed and lie in the interior. Then the prescribing function
h
can be chosen such that
\spt(h)\subset B(p,d_{0}+1;\widetilde{g}_{k})
. Next we replace Theorem
2.4
by Theorem
8.1
to obtain a strongly
\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon_{j}h}
-stationary,
C^{1,1}
, free
\varepsilon_{j}h
-boundary
(\Sigma_{k,j},\Omega_{k,j})

in
(N_{k},\widetilde{g}_{k})
for each homotopy class of relative sweepouts; moreover,
(\Sigma_{k,j},\Omega_{k,j})
satisfies Property
(R)
(
3.3
) for any
L=L(m)
-admissble collection of anuuli in
N_{k}\setminus\partial N_{k}
. Observe that
\gamma_{\epsilon_{k}}(\partial N_{k}\times\{t\})
has non-zero mean curvature vector pointing towards
\partial N_{k}
and
\partial N_{k}
is mean concave. Thus,
\Sigma_{k,j}
cannot contain
\partial N_{k}
(since
\interior(\Sigma_{k,j})
is minimal near
\partial N_{k}
), and by the area bound and the monotonicity formula,
\Sigma_{k,j}
can not touch
\partial N_{k}
, that is,
\Sigma_{k,j}
is closed, for all sufficiently large
k
. Then by letting
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0
, using the same argument as in Theorem
7.2
, we know that there exists a stationary varifold
V_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}m^{k}_{i}[\Gamma^{k}_{i}]
lying in
(N_{k}\setminus\partial N_{k},\widetilde{g}_{k})
that achieves
\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\bm{\Xi};\widetilde{g}_{k})
, where
\Gamma^{k}_{i}\in\mathscr{D}_{k}
, and

•

m^{k}_{i}=1
if
\Gamma^{k}_{i}
is unstable;

•

V_{k}
satisfies Property
(R’)
(
5.1
) for any
L(m)
-admissible collection of annuli in
N_{k}\setminus\partial N_{k}
;

•

if
M
is orientable, then the genus bound (
7.1
) holds with
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{g}(\Sigma_{0})
.

It remains to prove that
V_{k}
is stationary w.r.t. the metric
g
for all sufficiently large
k
. Indeed, by the same argument in
[
44
]
, the limit of
\cup_{i}\Gamma_{i}^{k}
is a minimal surface in
(N\setminus\partial N,g)
. Note that such a limit does not intersect
\partial N
. By the Hausdorff convergence of
\Gamma_{i}^{k}
, we conclude that
\Gamma_{i}^{k}\subset N_{k}\cap\{\widetilde{g}_{k}=g\}
for all sufficiently large
k
. In particular,
\Gamma_{i}^{k}
is a minimal surface in
(N\setminus\partial N,g)
.
This completes the proof of Proposition
8.2
.
∎

8.3.
Sweepouts in three-spheres

In this subsection, we will first recall the fact that the three-sphere always admits a nontrivial
k
-parameter (
k=1,2,3,4
) sweepout of two-spheres; see also
[
13
]
*Section 2. Then we apply the Multiplicity One Theorem for Simon-Smith min-max theory (Theorem
7.3
) to construct four distinct embedded minimal two-spheres if the manifold has no stable minimal two-spheres.

Let
\mathbb{S}^{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be the standard unit round three-sphere, and
x_{1},\cdots,x_{4}
be the four coordinate functions. Consider the spaces

\mathscr{X}:=\{\phi(\mathbb{S}^{2})\big|\phi:\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\mathbb{S}^{3}\text{ is a smooth embedding}\},

and

\mathscr{Y}:=\{\phi(\mathbb{S}^{2})\big|\phi:\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\mathbb{S}^{3}\text{ is a smooth map whose image is a point or an interval}\}.

Denote by
\overline{\mathscr{X}}=\mathscr{X}\cup\mathscr{Y}
endowed with un-oriented smooth topology.

For each
i=1,2,3,4
, let
\mathscr{P}_{i}
be the collection of continuous maps
\Phi:X\to\overline{\mathscr{X}}
,
with
\Phi(Z_{0})\subset\mathscr{Y}
, so that there exists some
\overline{\lambda}\in H^{1}(\overline{\mathscr{X}},\mathscr{Y};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, such that
\alpha=\Phi^{*}(\overline{\lambda})
satisfies

\alpha^{i}\neq 0\in H^{i}(X,Z_{0};\mathbb{Z}_{2}).

Next we describe four explicit sweepouts that belongs to
\mathscr{P}_{i}
for each
i=1,2,3,4
.
We use
[-1,1]\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.23608pt\hskip-4.23608pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}\hskip-4.23608pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{5.63335pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 4.23608pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.81944pt\hskip-3.81944pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}\hskip-3.81944pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{4.83336pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 3.81944pt}}}\mathbb{RP}^{3}
to denote the twisted
[-1,1]
-bundle over
\mathbb{RP}^{3}
, and
[a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}]
to denote a point in
[-1,1]\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.23608pt\hskip-4.23608pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}\hskip-4.23608pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{5.63335pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 4.23608pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.81944pt\hskip-3.81944pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}\hskip-3.81944pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{4.83336pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 3.81944pt}}}\mathbb{RP}^{3}
; that is
a_{0}\in[-1,1]
,
a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{4}^{2}=1
, and
(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})
is identified with
(-a_{0},-a_{1},-a_{2},-a_{3},-a_{4})
.
When
a_{0}\neq\pm 1
, we denote

\mathcal{G}([a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}]):=\{a_{1}x_{1}+a_{2}x_{2}+a_{3}x_{3}+a_{4}x_{4}=a_{0}\}\cap\mathbb{S}^{3};

when
a_{0}=\pm 1
,
\mathcal{G}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})
denotes a point given by
\pm(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})\in\mathbb{S}^{3}
.

We now define four maps:

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}:[-1,1]\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.23608pt\hskip-4.23608pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}\hskip-4.23608pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{5.63335pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 4.23608pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.81944pt\hskip-3.81944pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}\hskip-3.81944pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{4.83336pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 3.81944pt}}}\mathbb{RP}^{0}\to\overline{\mathscr{X}},\quad a_{0}\longmapsto\mathcal{G}(a_{0},1,0,0,0);

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}:[-1,1]\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.23608pt\hskip-4.23608pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}\hskip-4.23608pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{5.63335pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 4.23608pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.81944pt\hskip-3.81944pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}\hskip-3.81944pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{4.83336pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 3.81944pt}}}\mathbb{RP}^{1}\to\overline{\mathscr{X}},\quad[a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}]\longmapsto\mathcal{G}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},0,0);

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}:[-1,1]\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.23608pt\hskip-4.23608pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}\hskip-4.23608pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{5.63335pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 4.23608pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.81944pt\hskip-3.81944pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}\hskip-3.81944pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{4.83336pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 3.81944pt}}}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\to\overline{\mathscr{X}},\quad[a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}]\longmapsto\mathcal{G}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},0);

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}:[-1,1]\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.23608pt\hskip-4.23608pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}\hskip-4.23608pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{5.63335pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 4.23608pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.81944pt\hskip-3.81944pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}\hskip-3.81944pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{4.83336pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 3.81944pt}}}\mathbb{RP}^{3}\to\overline{\mathscr{X}},\quad[a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}]\longmapsto\mathcal{G}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}).

For simplicity, we use
\mathcal{X}_{i}
to denote
[-1,1]\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.99998pt\hskip-4.99998pt\hbox{$\textstyle\times$}\hskip-4.99998pt\hskip-35.56923pt\raisebox{6.83333pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-35.56923pt\hskip 4.99998pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.23608pt\hskip-4.23608pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\times$}\hskip-4.23608pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{5.63335pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 4.23608pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 3.81944pt\hskip-3.81944pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\times$}\hskip-3.81944pt\hskip-30.28427pt\raisebox{4.83336pt}{\hbox{${}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\textstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}{\scalebox{.9}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 1.0mu\sim$}}}$}}\hskip-30.28427pt\hskip 3.81944pt}}}\mathbb{RP}^{i-1}
, (and as compared with our definition of
\mathscr{P}_{i}
,
Z_{0}=\partial\mathcal{X}_{i}
for each
i
.) Let
f:[-1,1]\to\mathcal{X}_{i}
be an arbitrary embedding of a fiber of the
[-1,1]
-bundle.

We now show that
\Psi_{i}\in\mathscr{P}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3,4
. Denote by
\bm{\iota}:\overline{\mathscr{X}}\to\mathcal{Z}_{2}(S^{3};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
the natural inclusion map into the space of mod-2 integral cycles. Note that
\bm{\iota}(\mathscr{Y})=\{0\}
, that is, the image of each element in
\mathscr{Y}
is a zero cycle. Consider the chain of maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({[-1,1]},\partial{[-1,1]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{X}_{i},\partial\mathcal{X}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{\mathscr{X}},\mathscr{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{Z}_{2}(S^{3};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\{0\});}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\iota}}

the composition map
\bm{\iota}\circ\Psi_{i}\circ f:([-1,1],\partial{[-1,1]})\to(\mathcal{Z}_{2}(S^{3};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\{0\})
is then a sweepout in the sense of Almgren; see
[
3
]
,
[
29
, Definition 3.4]
and
[
53
, Theorem 5.8]
. Therefore, we know that
(\bm{\iota}\circ\Psi_{i}\circ f)^{*}:H^{1}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}(S^{3};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\{0\};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to H^{1}([-1,1],\partial{[-1,1]};\mathbb{Z}_{2})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is nontrivial, and by the chain of pull-back maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}(S^{3};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),\{0\};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\overline{\mathscr{X}},\mathscr{Y};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{i},\partial\mathcal{X}_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}([-1,1],\partial[-1,1];\mathbb{Z}_{2}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\iota}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{i}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

we also know that
\Psi_{i}^{*}:H^{1}(\overline{\mathscr{X}},\mathscr{Y};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to H^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{i},\partial\mathcal{X}_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is nontrivial. This together with the structure of the relative cohomology ring

H^{*}(\mathcal{X}_{i},\partial\mathcal{X}_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}[\alpha]/[\alpha^{i+1}]

implies that
\Psi_{i}\in\mathscr{P}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3,4
.

Recall that

\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{i}):=\inf_{\Phi\in\mathscr{P}_{i}}\sup_{x\in\mathrm{dom}\Phi}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Phi(x)).

The next result follows from Lusternik–Schnirelmann theory. We will provide a detailed proof in Appendix
E
, which is borrowed from
[
30
]
*Theorem 6.1 with minor modifications.

Lemma 8.3
.

Suppose that
(M,g)
contains only finitely many embedded minimal two-spheres. Then

0<\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{1})<\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{2})<\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{3})<\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{4}).

Remark 8.4
.

Haslhofer-Ketover
[
13
]
*Theorem 5.2 proved a similar result.

Theorem 8.5
.

Suppose that
(M,g)
is a Riemannian 3-sphere. If
(M,g)
does not contain any embedded stable minimal two-sphere, then
(M,g)
admits at least four distinct embedded minimal two-spheres.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
(M,g)
contains only finitely many embedded minimal 2-spheres. Thus by Lemma
8.3
,

0<\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{1})<\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{2})<\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{3})<\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{4}).

Note that
\mathscr{P}_{i}
may contain many different homotopy classes of
(X,Z_{0})
-sweepouts, but since there are only finitely many minimal
2
-spheres in
(M,g)
, the min-max values of these homotopy classes have to stabilize. Therefore, by the Multiplicity One Theorem for Simon-Smith min-max theory (Theorem
7.3
), each
\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{i})
is realized by a disjoint union of some closed minimal two-spheres with integer multiplicities. Since
(M,g)
has no stable minimal spheres, any two embedded minimal
2
-spheres have to intersect with each other. Thus
\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{i})
is achieved by some minimal two-sphere
\Gamma_{i}
with integer multiplicity
m_{i}
. Since
\Gamma_{i}
is unstable by assumption, we have
m_{i}=1
. Thus we conclude that
\Gamma_{1},\cdots,\Gamma_{4}
are four embedded minimal two-spheres with

0<\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma_{1})<\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma_{2})<\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma_{3})<\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma_{4}).

This finishes the proof of Theorem
8.5
.
∎

8.4.
Simon-Smith width upper bound

In this subsection, we will assume that
N
and
M
in Section
8.2
are diffeomorphic to the three-ball and the three-sphere, respectively.
Then the construction in Section
8.3
can be applied. In particular,
\mathscr{P}_{j}
is well-defined for
j=1,2,3,4
. The goal of this subsection is to give a uniform upper bound for
\mathbf{L}_{N_{\epsilon}}(\mathscr{P}_{j};\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon})
(independent of
\epsilon
), where
N_{\epsilon}
and
\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon}
are as in Section
8.2
.

We first introduce the following result which is from the mean curvature flow with surgery.

Lemma 8.6

(
[
13
]
*Theorem 8.1
[
25
]
*Proposition 3.6)
.

Let
(N,\partial N,g)
be a compact Riemannian three-ball whose boundary
\partial N
is a stable minimal sphere. If
(N\setminus\partial N,g)
does not contain any stable minimal spheres, there exists a minimal sphere
S
of index one and a smooth foliation
\{S_{t}\}_{t\in[-1,1]}
of
N
such that
S_{-1}
is a point,
S_{0}=S
,
S_{1}=\partial N
and

\mathcal{H}^{2}(S_{t})\leq\mathcal{H}^{2}(S).

Proof.

By
[
17
]
*Lemma 8.1 and the Simon-Smith min-max theory, there exists an embedded minimal sphere
S
with index one. Clearly,
N\setminus S
has two connected components
N^{+}
and
N^{-}
. Since
S
is unstable, then
S
has
an expanding neighborhood

U
in
N
, that is,
U
can be foliated by spheres and
\partial U
has non-zero mean curvature vector pointing away from
U
. Thus one can use the mean curvature flow with surgery to obtain a smooth foliation of
N\setminus U
. Combining with the foliation of
U
, we obtain a foliation of
N
. One can modify the foliation slightly around
\partial U
to get a smooth foliation satisfying all of the requirements. This completes the proof. ∎

Denote by
W
the area of
S
in Lemma
8.6
. Let
(N_{\epsilon},g_{\epsilon})
and
(M,\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon})
be the manifolds constructed in the previous Subsection
8.2
. Without loss of generality, we assume that there exists a smooth foliation
\{K_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of
M\setminus(N\setminus\partial N)
so that
K_{0}=\partial N
,
K_{1}=q^{\prime}\in M\setminus N
is a point, and

(8.1)

\mathcal{H}^{2}(K_{t})\leq\mathcal{H}^{2}(\partial N)+\epsilon.

Note that this can be done because one can arbitrarily deform the metric in
M\setminus N
.

Lemma 8.7
.

As above, if
(N\setminus\partial N,g)
does not contain any stable minimal spheres, then for all sufficiently small
\epsilon
, we have that

\mathbf{L}_{N_{\epsilon}}(\mathscr{P}_{1};\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon})\leq W;\quad\mathbf{L}_{N_{\epsilon}}(\mathscr{P}_{2};\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon})\leq 2W.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.6
, there exists a smooth foliation
\{S_{t}\}_{t\in[-1,1]}
such that
S_{-1}
is a point,
S_{1}=\partial N
and

\mathcal{H}^{2}(S_{t})\leq W.

Now we take a sufficiently small constant
\tau>0
, so that
S_{1-\tau}
is sufficiently close to
\partial N
(in the
C^{3}
topology) as smooth graphs. Fix this
\tau
. Note that for sufficiently small
\epsilon
,
\gamma_{\epsilon}(\partial N_{\epsilon}\times\{-2\delta_{\epsilon}\})
is also a smooth graph over
\partial N
. Then one can foliate the region between
S_{1-\tau}
and
\gamma_{\epsilon}(\partial N_{\epsilon}\times\{-2\delta_{\epsilon}\})
by spheres with area close to
\mathcal{H}^{2}(S_{1-\tau})<W
. Recall that (see
(iii)
in Section
8.2
)
\gamma_{\epsilon}(\partial N_{\epsilon}\times[-2\delta_{\epsilon},0])
has a natural foliation
\{\gamma_{\epsilon}(\partial N_{\epsilon}\times\{t\})\}_{t\in[-2\delta_{\epsilon},0]}
with

\mathcal{H}^{2}(\partial N_{\epsilon}\times\{t\};\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon})=\mathcal{H}^{2}(\partial N_{\epsilon}\times\{t\};g)<W.

Combining with the foliation on
M\setminus N
satisfying (
8.1
), we obtain a foliation of
M
and each leaf has area (w.r.t.
\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon}
) less than or equal to
W
. One can modify the foliation around
S_{1-\tau}
and
\partial N
slightly to get a smooth foliation
\{\widetilde{S}_{t}\}_{t\in[-1,1]}
(by reparametrization) of
(M,\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon})
. Since the area of
S_{1-\tau}
and
\partial N
is strictly less than
W
, the smooth foliation can be chosen so that

\mathcal{H}^{2}(\widetilde{S}_{t})\leq W.

This gives the first inequality. Then the second part follows from
[
13
]
*Theorem 4.1.
∎

8.5.
Existence of four minimal two-spheres

This subsection is devoted to prove the existence of four distinct minimal two-spheres in any Riemannian three-sphere
(M,g)
which does not contain any degenerate-and-stable minimal two-spheres. Note that this has been proven when
(M,g)
has no stable minimal spheres; see Theorem
8.5
. The next result gives that if a Riemannian three-ball
(N,g)
has a stable minimal two-sphere as its boundary, then
(N,g)
admits at least two unstable minimal two-spheres.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
(N,\partial N,g)
be a compact three-ball with smooth boundary. Suppose that
\partial N
is a stable minimal two-sphere with a contracting neighborhood in
N
. If
N\setminus\partial N
does not contain any stable minimal two-sphere,
N\setminus\partial N
admits at least two distinct embedded minimal two-spheres.

Proof.

The existence of the first minimal two-sphere follows from
[
17
]
*Lemma 8.1 and the Simon-Smith min-max theory. Denote by
W
the area of this minimal sphere. Without loss of generality, we assume that
N
contains only finitely many embedded minimal two-spheres. Let
(N_{\epsilon_{k}},g_{\epsilon_{k}})\subset(M,\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon_{k}})
be a sequence of domains constructed in Section
8.2
.
We use
N_{k}
and
\widetilde{g}_{k}
to denote
N_{\epsilon_{k}}
and
\widetilde{g}_{\epsilon_{k}}
, respectively. Then by Lemma
8.7
,

\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\mathscr{P}_{2};\widetilde{g}_{k})\leq 2W.

Thus for all sufficiently large
k
, Proposition
8.2
can be applied to produce embedded minimal two-spheres
\Sigma_{k}\subset(N\setminus\partial N,g)
(with multiplicity) that achieves
\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\mathscr{P}_{2};\widetilde{g}_{k})
. Since
(N\setminus\partial N,g)
has no stable minimal two-spheres,
\Sigma_{k}
is unstable, which yields that it has multiplicity one.

Summarizing that for all sufficiently large
k
,
\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\mathscr{P}_{2};\widetilde{g}_{k})
is achieved by an embedded minimal two-sphere with multiplicity one in
(N,\partial N)
. Then applying the Lusternik–Schnirelmann theory as in Lemma
8.3
, one can prove that

\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\mathscr{P}_{2};\widetilde{g}_{k})>\mathbf{L}_{N_{k}}(\mathscr{P}_{1};\widetilde{g}_{k}).

Thus we have proved that
(N\setminus\partial N,g)
contains at least two distinct embedded minimal two-spheres. This finishes the proof of Proposition
8.8
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the main theorem.

Theorem 8.9

(Theorem
A
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian three dimensional sphere so that
(M,g)
does not contain any degenerate-and-stable minimal two-spheres. Then
(M,g)
contains at least four distinct embedded minimal two-spheres. In particular, if
g
is bumpy or if
\mathrm{Ric}_{g}>0
, then
(M,g)
admits at least four distinct embedded minimal two-spheres.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
M
contains only finitely many embedded minimal two-spheres. If
(M,g)
does not contain any stable minimal two-sphere, then the conclusion follows from Theorem
8.5
.

Now we assume that
(M,g)
contains stable minimal embedded two-spheres. Then by assumption, those stable minimal two-spheres in
(M,g)
are non-degenerate, that is, strictly stable. Then one can cut
M
along a collection of pairwise disjoint stable embedded minimal two-spheres. Denote by
N_{1},\cdots,N_{k}
the metric completion of those connected components. Clearly, at least two of them (denoted by
N_{1},N_{2}
) are diffeomorphic to a three-ball. Then Proposition
8.8
can be applied to construct two distinct embedded minimal two-spheres in each
N_{i}\setminus\partial N_{i}
for
i=1,2
. It follows that
(M,g)
contains at least 5 embedded minimal two-spheres. This completes the proof of Theorem
8.9
.
∎

Remark 8.10
.

Finally, we remark that the min-max theory can produce degenerate-and-stable closed minimal surfaces with higher multiplicity by the authors’ earlier work
[
45
]
. Note that even the results in
[
45
]
were stated for the Almgren-Pitts theory, they also hold true for the Simon-Smith theory.

Appendix A
C^{1,1}
-estimates for multilayer
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary boundaries

We recall the
C^{1,1}
-regularity and estimates for multilayer
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary boundaries in
[
46
]
; see also
[
36
]
*Section 11. Fix
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
and an open subset
W\subset M
. Let
(\Sigma,\Omega)\in\VC(W)
be a
C^{1,\alpha}
-boundary
9
9

9

Note that Definition
1.10
can be straightforwardly adapted to the
C^{1,\alpha}
-setting.
in
W
. Suppose that
\Sigma
decomposes to finitely many ordered
C^{1,\alpha}
-surfaces
\Gamma^{1}\leq\cdots\leq\Gamma^{\ell}\subset W
, where
\partial\Gamma^{i}\cap W=\emptyset
for each
i=1,\cdots,\ell
.

Proposition A.1

(
[
46
]
*Theorem 1.4, Corollary 1.5)
.

Assume that
(\Sigma,\Omega)
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
W
, and each
\Gamma^{i}
has
\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}
-bounded first variation in
W
. Then each
\Gamma^{i}
is
C^{1,1}
in
W
.

Without loss of generality, for an arbitrary point
q\in\Gamma^{1}\cap\cdots\cap\Gamma^{\ell}\cap W
, we may choose a local coordinate system in a neighborhood of
q
, still denoted as
W
, such that each
\Gamma^{i}
is written as a graph over the unit disk
B_{2}
of the common tangent plane
P=T_{q}\Gamma^{i}
, (note that
\{\Gamma^{i}\}
meet tangentially at
q
by assumption).
By
[
46
, Remark 1.5]
, we can choose a minimal surface
B^{\prime}
(near
B_{2}
) containing
q
and rewrite
\Gamma^{i}
as graphs of
C^{1,\alpha}
-functions
u^{i}:B^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}

with

u^{1}\leq u^{2}\leq\cdots\leq u^{\ell}.

For simplicity, denote by
B^{\prime}_{r}
the geodesic ball in
B^{\prime}
centered at
q
.
We recall the
C^{1,1}_{loc}
-estimates of this system.

Proposition A.2

(
[
46
]
*Theorem 1.4, Corollary 1.5)
.

Under the same assumption as in Proposition
A.1
, assume further that
\|u^{i}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(B^{\prime}_{1})}<1
for each
i
, then we have

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\|u_{i}\|_{C^{1,1}(B^{\prime}_{1/2})}\leq C\big(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\|u_{i}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(B^{\prime}_{1})}+\|h\|_{C^{1}(B^{\prime}_{1}\times(-1,1))}\big),

for some constant
C>1
depending only on
\ell
,
\alpha
, and the metric
g
in
W
.

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
4.5

The proof is adapted from Colding-De Lellis
[
6
]
*§7.4 with minor modifications to include volume terms.

Denote by
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
the limit of
(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})
. Then
(V^{*},\Omega^{*})
is
\mathcal{A}^{h}
-stationary in
U
. Denote by
m_{0}
the upper bound of
\|V^{*}\|(M)
. Denote

d:=\operatorname{dist}_{M}(U^{\prime},\partial U).

We take
r_{1}
small enough so that for any integral varifold which has
c
-bounded first variation in
B_{r_{1}}(x)
, one has

2\frac{\|V\|(B_{r}(x))}{r^{2}}\geq\frac{\|V\|(B_{t}(x))}{t^{2}}\quad\text{ for all }r_{1}\geq r\geq t>0,\ x\in U^{\prime}.

Note that
r_{1}
depends only on
c
and
M
. Let
\rho_{0}<\min\{d/10,r_{1}\}
be a small constant which will be specified later. Then for all
B_{\rho}(x)\subset U^{\prime}
with
\rho<\rho_{0}

\|V^{*}\|(B_{2\rho}(x))\leq 2\|V^{*}\|(B_{d}(x))\cdot d^{-2}\cdot 4\rho^{2}\leq 8m_{0}d^{-2}\cdot\rho^{2}.

By taking sufficiently large
k
, we have

\|\Sigma_{k}\|(B_{2\rho}(x))<9m_{0}d^{-2}\cdot\rho^{2}.

Then by the slicing theorem
[
41
]
*§28.1, there exists
\tau_{k}\in(\rho,2\rho)
such that

(B.1)

\text{$\Sigma_{k}$ is transversal to $\partial B_{\tau_{k}}(x)$, and $\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{k}\cap\partial B_{\tau_{k}}(x))<9m_{0}d^{-2}\rho$}.

Since
\Sigma_{k}
is transversal to
\partial B_{\tau_{k}}(x)
, then
t\mapsto\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Sigma_{k}\cap\partial B_{t}(x))
is continuous at
t=\tau_{k}
. Hence there exists a small interval
(\sigma_{k},s_{k})\subset(\rho,2\rho)
around
\tau_{k}
, so that (
B.1
) holds for every
\tau\in(\sigma_{k},s_{k})
. Now we consider the radial isotopy
\psi\in\mathfrak{Is}\big(B_{s_{k}}(x)\big)
, so that for some
\eta\ll\sigma_{k}
to be specified later,

\psi\big(t,B_{s_{k}}(x)\big)=B_{s_{k}}(x)\text{ for all }t\in[0,1],\text{ and }\psi\big(1,B_{\sigma_{k}}(x)\big)=B_{\eta}(x).

By computation, we have

\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(\psi(t,\Sigma_{k})\big)\leq\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma_{k})+Cm_{0}d^{-2}\rho^{2},

which implies that

(B.2)

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\psi(t,\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\big)\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})+Cm_{0}d^{-2}\rho^{2}+C\rho^{3}.

Here
C
is a uniform constant which may change from line to line. For simplicity, denote

\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k}=\psi(1,\Sigma_{k}),\quad\widetilde{\Omega}_{k}=\psi(1,\Omega_{k}).

Note that any isotopy
\varphi\in\mathfrak{Is}\big(B_{\rho}(x)\big)
will correspond to a new isotopy
\widetilde{\varphi}:=\psi_{1}\circ\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}_{1}
of
\psi_{1}(B_{\rho}(x))\subset B_{\eta}(x)
, where
\psi_{1}(\cdot)=\psi(1,\cdot)
.
A direct computation gives that

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\widetilde{\varphi}(t,\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})\big)\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})+Cm_{1}\eta^{2}\rho^{-2}+C\eta^{3},

where
m_{1}:=\sup_{t}\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(\varphi(t,\Sigma_{k})\big)
. By taking sufficiently small
\eta
(depending on
C,m_{1},\rho
), we have

(B.3)

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\widetilde{\varphi}(t,\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})\big)\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k},\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})+\rho^{3}.

Now let

\hat{\Sigma}_{k}:=\widetilde{\varphi}(1,\widetilde{\Sigma}_{k})=\psi_{1}(\varphi(1,\Sigma_{k})),\quad\hat{\Omega}_{k}:=\widetilde{\varphi}(1,\widetilde{\Omega}_{k})=\psi_{1}(\varphi(1,\Omega_{k})).

Finally, we take the
\psi^{-1}
to deform
(\hat{\Sigma}_{k},\hat{\Omega}_{k})
. Then by the same argument as in (
B.2
),

(B.4)

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\psi^{-1}(t,\hat{\Sigma}_{k},\hat{\Omega}_{k})\big)\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\hat{\Sigma}_{k},\hat{\Omega}_{k}\big)+Cm_{0}d^{-2}\rho^{2}+C\rho^{3}.

Now we define an isotopy
\Phi
by concatenating
\psi
,
\widetilde{\varphi}
and
\psi^{-1}
. Notice that

\Phi(1,\cdot)=\varphi(1,\cdot).

Combining (
B.2
), (
B.3
) with (
B.4
), we obtain

\mathcal{A}^{h}\big(\Phi(t,\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})\big)\leq\mathcal{A}^{h}(\Sigma_{k},\Omega_{k})+Cm_{0}d^{-2}\rho^{2}+C\rho^{3}.

The lemma follows by taking
\rho_{0}>0
so that

Cm_{0}d^{-2}\rho_{0}^{2}+C\rho^{3}\leq\delta,\quad\rho_{0}\leq d/10,\quad\text{and }\rho_{0}\leq r_{1}.

Here
r_{1}
depends only on
M
and
c
. Hence
\rho_{0}
depends only on
M
,
c
,
m_{0}
,
\delta
and
\operatorname{dist}_{M}(U^{\prime},\partial U)
.

Appendix C
Calculations related to construction of Jacobi type fields

In this part, we use the notion of elliptic functionals to present the calculations regarding the PDE satisfied by the height difference of two small graphs surrounding an embedded minimal surface. The setups are as follows.

•

\Sigma^{n}
denotes a closed embedded minimal hypersurface with a unit normal
\nu
in a closed Riemannian manifold
(M^{n+1},g)
.

•

(x,z)\in\Sigma\times(-\delta_{0},\delta_{0})
denotes the Fermi coordinates induced by the normal exponential map:
(x,z)\mapsto\exp_{x}\big(z\nu(x)\big)
.

•

Given an open subset
\mathcal{U}\subset\Sigma
and a small
\delta_{0}>0
, we let
\mathcal{U}_{\delta_{0}}=\mathcal{U}\times(-\delta_{0},\delta_{0})
.

•

The area element is a function:

F:T\Sigma\times(-\delta_{0},\delta_{0})\to[0,\infty),

defined as follows: for any
(x,z,\bm{p})\in\Sigma\times(-\delta_{0},\delta_{0})\times T_{x}\Sigma
10
10

10

Here we choose to use
(x,z,\bm{p})
to denote a point in
T\Sigma\times(-\delta_{0},\delta_{0})
to be coherent with classical notations related to elliptic integrands.
, we use
P_{x,z,\bm{p}}
and
P_{x}
to denote respectively the
n
-dim parallelograms in
T_{(x,z)}M
and
T_{x}\Sigma
generated by

\displaystyle\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}\},\text{ where }e_{i}=\partial_{x^{i}}+\langle\bm{p},\partial_{x^{i}}\rangle_{\Sigma}\partial_{z},

\displaystyle\text{ and }\quad\{\partial_{x^{1}},\cdots,\partial_{x^{n}}\}

Then

F(x,z,\bm{p})=\frac{\text{$n$-volume of $P_{x,z,\bm{p}}$ under $g(x,z)$}}{\text{$n$-volume of $P_{x}$ under $g(x,0)$}}.

•

\sigma(x,z,\bm{p})
denotes the inner product (under
g(x,z)
) between
\partial_{z}
and the unit normal of
P_{x,z,\bm{p}}
multiplied with
F(x,z,\bm{p})
.

Lemma C.1
.

F(x,z,\bm{p})
and
\sigma(x,z,\bm{p})
are smooth functions over
(x,z,\bm{p})
. Moreover,

F(x,0,0)\equiv 1\quad\text{ and }\quad\sigma(x,0,0)\equiv 1.

•

Given a graph
\Graph_{u}\subset\mathcal{U}_{\delta_{0}}
of
u\in C^{1,1}(\mathcal{U})
, its
n
-dim area is given by

\mathrm{Area}(\Graph_{u})=\int_{\mathcal{U}}F\big(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x),

where
d\mathcal{H}^{n}
is the
n
-dim Hausdorff measure of
\Sigma
.

•

Since
\Graph_{u}
is a
C^{1,1}
-hypersurface, its generalized mean curvature exists almost everywhere; we denote the one w.r.t. upward unit normal (in the direction of
\partial_{z}
) by
H_{u}(x)\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{U})
.

The first variation formula of
\mathrm{Area}(\Graph_{u})
w.r.t. variations
t\mapsto\Graph_{u+tf}
for a fixed
f\in C^{1,1}_{c}(\mathcal{U})
is given by

(C.1)

\displaystyle\delta\mathrm{Area}_{u}(f)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{p}}F\big(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)\big)\cdot\nabla f+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}F\big(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)\big)\cdot f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}H_{u}(x)\cdot\sigma\big(x,u(x),\nabla u(x)\big)\cdot f(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x).

We now introduce the following notations:

\displaystyle\bm{A}(x,z,\bm{p})=\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bm{p}^{i}\partial\bm{p}^{j}}F\big(x,z,\bm{p}\big)\Big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n},

\displaystyle\bm{b}(x,z,\bm{p})=\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z\partial\bm{p}^{i}}F\big(x,z,\bm{p}\big)\Big)_{1\leq i\leq n},

\displaystyle d(x,z,\bm{p})=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}F\big(x,z,\bm{p}\big).

Given
\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\mathcal{U})
, the second variation formula is given by

(C.2)

\displaystyle\delta^{2}\mathrm{Area}_{u}(\varphi,f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\delta\mathrm{Area}_{u+t\varphi}(f)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}\bm{A}(x,u,\nabla u)\big(\nabla\varphi,\nabla f\big)+\bm{b}(x,u,\nabla u)\cdot(\varphi\nabla f+f\nabla\varphi)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+d(x,u,\nabla u)\cdot\varphi f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x).

Note that since
\Sigma
is a minimal hypersurface, the above equation (
C.2
) reduces to the classical second variation formula when
u\equiv 0
:

(C.3)

\delta^{2}\mathrm{Area}_{0}(\varphi,f)=\int_{\mathcal{U}}\langle\nabla\varphi,\nabla f\rangle-\big(\Ric(\nu,\nu)+|A^{\Sigma}|^{2}\big)\varphi\cdot f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x).

Now let
u^{+}
and
u^{-}
be two functions in
C^{1,1}(\mathcal{U})
. Then by subtracting (
C.1
) for
u^{+}
and
u^{-}
, we have

(C.4)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}}\Big[\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{p}}F\big(x,u^{+},

\displaystyle\nabla u^{+}\big)-\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{p}}F\big(x,u^{-},\nabla u^{-}\big)\Big]\cdot\nabla f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{U}}\Big[\frac{\partial}{\partial z}F\big(x,u^{+},\nabla u^{+}\big)-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}F\big(x,u^{-},\nabla u^{-}\big)\Big]\cdot f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}\Big[H_{u^{+}}\cdot\sigma\big(x,u^{+},\nabla u^{+}\big)-H_{u^{-}}\cdot\sigma\big(x,u^{-},\nabla u^{-}\big)\Big]\cdot f(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x).

Consider the linear interpolation
u_{t}:=tu^{+}+(1-t)u^{-}
,
t\in[0,1]
. Then we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{p}}F\big(x,u^{+},\nabla u^{+}\big)

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{p}}F\big(x,u^{-},\nabla u^{-}\big)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}\Big[\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{p}}F\big(x,u_{t},\nabla u_{t}\big)\Big]\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\bm{A}(x,u_{t},\nabla u_{t})\cdot\nabla(u^{+}-u^{-})+\bm{b}(x,u_{t},\nabla u_{t})(u^{+}-u^{-})\,\mathrm{d}t,

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z}F\big(x,u^{+},\nabla u^{+}\big)

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}F\big(x,u^{-},\nabla u^{-}\big)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}\Big[\frac{\partial}{\partial z}F\big(x,u_{t},\nabla u_{t}\big)\Big]\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\bm{b}(x,u_{t},\nabla u_{t})\cdot\nabla(u^{+}-u^{-})+d(x,u_{t},\nabla u_{t})\cdot(u^{+}-u^{-})\,\mathrm{d}t.

Let

\widetilde{\bm{A}}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}\bm{A}(x,u_{t},\nabla u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad\widetilde{\bm{b}}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}\bm{b}(x,u_{t},\nabla u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad\widetilde{d}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}d(x,u_{t},\nabla u_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t,

and

\bm{\alpha}(x)=\widetilde{\bm{A}}(x)-\bm{A}(x,0,0),\quad\bm{\beta}(x)=\widetilde{\bm{b}}(x)-\bm{b}(x,0,0),\quad\zeta(x)=\widetilde{d}(x)-d(x,0,0),

and

\sigma^{+}(x)=\sigma(x,u^{+},\nabla u^{+}),\quad\sigma^{-}(x)=\sigma(x,u^{-},\nabla u^{-}).

Since
F(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
is smooth, we know that
\widetilde{\bm{A}},\widetilde{\bm{b}},\widetilde{d},\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\zeta,\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-}
are all in
C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})
. Moreover, using Lemma
C.1
, we know that their norms satisfy:

(C.5)

\displaystyle\|\bm{\alpha}\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})}+\|\bm{\beta}\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})}+\|\zeta\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})}\leq C\big(\|u^{+}\|_{C^{1,1}(\mathcal{U})}+\|u^{-}\|_{C^{1,1}(\mathcal{U})}\big),

(C.6)

\displaystyle\|\sigma^{+}-1\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})}+\|\sigma^{-}-1\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{U})}\leq C\big(\|u^{+}\|_{C^{1,1}(\mathcal{U})}+\|u^{-}\|_{C^{1,1}(\mathcal{U})}\big).

for some uniform constant
C>0
.

Plugging everything back to (
C.4
), and writing
w=u^{+}-u^{-}
we have

(C.7)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}}\widetilde{\bm{A}}(x)\big(\nabla w,\nabla f\big)

\displaystyle+\widetilde{\bm{b}}(x)\cdot\big(w\nabla f+f\nabla w\big)+\widetilde{d}(x)w\cdot f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}\Big(H_{u^{+}}\cdot\sigma^{+}(x)-H_{u^{-}}\cdot\sigma^{-}(x)\Big)f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x).

Together with (
C.3
), we can rewrite (
C.7
) (by subtracting with (
C.2
) when
u\equiv 0
and
\varphi=w
) as

(C.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{U}}\langle\nabla w,\nabla f\rangle

\displaystyle-\big(\Ric(\nu,\nu)+|A^{\Sigma}|^{2}\big)w\cdot f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{U}}\bm{\alpha}(x)(\nabla w,\nabla f)+\bm{\beta}(x)\cdot(w\nabla f+f\nabla w)+\zeta(x)wf\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{U}}\big(H_{u^{+}}\cdot\sigma^{+}(x)-H_{u^{-}}\cdot\sigma^{-}(x)\big)\cdot f\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x).

Appendix D
Annuli picking argument

In this appendix, we present a general diagonal argument which has been used several times in this paper. The proof here follows that in
[
7
]
*Lemma A.3.

Recall that an
L
-admissible collection of annuli
consists of a collection of concentric geodesic annuli

\mathrm{An}(p;s_{1},r_{1}),\mathrm{An}(p;s_{2},r_{2}),\cdots,\mathrm{An}(p;s_{L},r_{L}),

so that
2r_{j+1}<s_{j}
for
j=1,\cdots,L-1
. Denote by
\mathfrak{A}
the collection of all annuli in
M
.

Proposition D.1
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2},\cdots\subset\mathfrak{A}
be countably many sub-collections so that if
\mathrm{An}\in\mathcal{P}_{i}
, then any sub-concentric annuli of
\mathrm{An}
also belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{i}
. Suppose that for any
L
-admissible collection of annuli,
\mathcal{P}_{k}
contains at least one of them. Then there exists a subsequence (still denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{k}
) so that for each
p\in M
, there exists
r_{p}>0
such that for all
s<r<r_{p}
,
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
for all sufficiently large
k
.

Proof.

We first prove a weaker version of the proposition.

Claim 4
.

Given
p\in M
, there exist a subsequence (still denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{k}
) and
\delta_{p}>0
such that for all
s<r<\delta_{p}
,
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
for all sufficiently large
k
.

Proof of Claim
4
.

Take
0<t_{1}<r_{1}
and a subsequence
\{\mathcal{P}_{k}^{1}\}\subset\{\mathcal{P}_{k}\}
so that
\mathcal{P}_{k}^{1}
contains
\mathrm{An}(p;t_{1},r_{1})
. Such
t_{1},r_{1}
exist because one can construct an
L
-admissible collection of annuli with outer radius less than any given positive number. We will continue to choose annuli by induction. Suppose that we have chosen
t_{j}
and
\{\mathcal{P}_{k}^{j}\}_{k}
such that
\mathrm{An}(p;t_{j},r_{1})\in\mathcal{P}^{j}_{k}
for all
k
. So long as there exists a subsequence
\{\mathcal{P}_{k}^{j+1}\}\subset\{\mathcal{P}_{k}^{j}\}
such that
\mathrm{An}(p;t_{j}/2,r_{1})\in\mathcal{P}_{k}^{j+1}
for all
k
, we let
t_{j+1}:=t_{j}/2
. Otherwise, let
s_{1}:=t_{j}/2
. Continuing the argument, we have two possibilities:

(1)

s_{1}>0
and there exists a subsequence
\{\mathcal{P}_{k}^{\prime}\}\subset\{\mathcal{P}_{k}\}
so that
\mathrm{An}(p;s_{1},r_{1})\notin\mathcal{P}_{k}^{\prime}
for all
k
;

(2)

there exist
t_{1},t_{2},\cdots\to 0
and
\{\mathcal{P}_{k}^{j}\}\subset\{\mathcal{P}_{k}^{j-1}\}\subset\cdots\subset\{\mathcal{P}_{k}\}
such that
\mathrm{An}(p;t_{j},r_{1})\in\mathcal{P}_{k}^{j}
for all
k
.

For the later case, Claim
4
follows from a diagonal argument. For the first case, then we can take
r_{2}<s_{1}/2
and use the same argument to find
s_{2}
. Continuing the argument, we have that either Claim
4
holds true or there exist
\mathrm{An}(p;s_{1},r_{1})
,
\mathrm{An}(p;s_{2},r_{2})
,
\cdots
and
\{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{1}\}\supset\{\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{2}\}\supset\cdots
such that for each
j\geq 1
,
\mathrm{An}(p;s_{j},r_{j})\notin\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{j}
for all
k
. This contradicts the assumption if
j\geq L
. Hence Claim
4
is proved.
∎

Now we will prove the proposition by taking a finite open cover. Given
p\in M
, let
r_{p}^{1}
be the supremum of
r
so that there exists a subsequence
\{\mathcal{P}_{k}^{\prime}\}\subset\{\mathcal{P}_{k}\}
such that for any
s<r
,
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)\in\mathcal{P}_{k}^{\prime}
for all sufficiently large
k
. By Claim
4
,
r_{p}^{1}>0
for each
p\in M
. Let

\mathfrak{r}_{1}:=\frac{1}{2}\sup\left\{r_{p}^{1}\,;\,p\in M\right\}.

Then we can take
p_{1}\in M
so that
r_{p_{1}}>\mathfrak{r}_{1}
, which implies that there exists a subsequence
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{1}\}\subset\{\mathcal{P}_{k}\}
such that for any given
0<s<r<\mathfrak{r}_{1}
,
\mathrm{An}(p_{1};s,r)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{1}
for all sufficiently large
k
. Now we define
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
,
p_{j}
and
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{j}\}_{k}
inductively. Suppose that we have chosen
\mathfrak{r}_{i}
,
p_{i}
and
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{i}\}_{k}
for
i=1,\cdots,j
. Then let
r_{p}^{j+1}
be the supremum of
r
so that there exists a subsequence
\{\mathcal{P}_{k}^{\prime}\}\subset\{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{j}\}
such that for all
0<s<r
,
\mathrm{An}(p;s,r)\in\mathcal{P}_{k}^{\prime}
for sufficiently large
k
. Define

\mathfrak{r}_{j+1}:=\frac{1}{2}\sup\left\{r_{p}^{j+1}\,;\,p\in M\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{j}B_{\mathfrak{r}_{i}}(p_{i})\right\}.

Then there exist
p_{j+1}
and
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{j+1}\}
so that for any given
0<s<\mathfrak{r}_{j+1}
,
\mathrm{An}(p_{j+1};s,\mathfrak{r}_{j+1})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{j+1}
for all
k
sufficiently large.
Since
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{j}\}\subset\{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{j-1}\}
, we have that
r_{p}^{j+1}\leq r_{p}^{j}
for all
p\in M
. It follows that
\mathfrak{r}_{j+1}\leq\mathfrak{r}_{j}
.

Next we will prove that
\{\mathfrak{r}_{j}\}
is finite. Suppose not, observing that
\{B_{\mathfrak{r}_{j}/2}(p_{j})\}
