are pairwise disjoint balls (since
\mathfrak{r}_{j+1}\leq\mathfrak{r}_{j}
), thus we have
\mathfrak{r}_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
. By the contradiction assumption, there exists
q\in M\setminus\cup_{j}B_{\mathfrak{r}_{j}}(p_{j})
. By the definition of
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
, we have
r_{q}^{j}\leq 3\mathfrak{r}_{j}
. This gives that there exist
\mathfrak{s}_{j}>0
and
k_{j}>0
such that
\mathrm{An}(q;\mathfrak{s}_{j},3\mathfrak{r}_{j})\notin\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{j}
for all
k\geq k_{j}
. Since
\mathfrak{r}_{j}\to 0
, then by possibly taking a subsequence of
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{1}\}_{k}
,
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{2}\}_{k}
,
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{3}\}_{k}
,
\cdots
(not relabelled), we have that
6\mathfrak{r}_{j+1}<\mathfrak{s}_{j}
. Observe that for
k\geq k_{L}
,
\mathrm{An}(q;\mathfrak{s}_{j},3\mathfrak{r}_{j})\notin\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{k}^{L}
for all
j=1,\cdots,L
. This leads to a contradiction. Hence
\{\mathfrak{r}_{j}\}
is finite.

This completes the proof of Proposition
D.1
.
∎

Appendix E
Proof of the Lusternik–Schnirelmann inequality

Proof of Lemma
8.3
.

We prove the last inequality and the others are similar.
By the definition of
\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{4})
, there exist a sequence of
\{\Phi_{i}:(X_{i},Z_{i})\to(\overline{\mathscr{X}},\mathscr{Y})\}\subset\mathscr{P}_{4}
such that

\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{4})=\lim_{i\to\infty}\max_{x\in X_{i}}\mathrm{Area}(\Phi_{i}(x)).

Denote by
\mathcal{S}
the collection of integral varifolds, with mass equal to
\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{4})
, whose support are disjoint union of embedded minimal spheres.
Given
\eta_{1}>0
, define

\displaystyle Y_{i}:=\{x\in X_{i}:\,\mathbf{F}(|\Phi_{i}(x)|,\mathcal{S})\geq\eta_{1}\};\quad K_{i}:=\overline{X_{i}\setminus Y_{i}}.

Note that
K_{i}\subset\interior(X_{i})
for small enough
\eta_{1}
. Denote by
\iota_{1}:K_{i}\to X_{i}
and
\iota_{2}:Y_{i}\to X_{i}
the two natural inclusion maps. Since
\Phi_{i}\in\mathscr{P}_{4}
, there exists
\bar{\lambda}\in H^{1}(\overline{\mathscr{X}},\mathscr{Y};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
such that
[\Phi_{i}^{*}(\bar{\lambda})]^{4}\neq 0\in H^{4}(X_{i},Z_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. Observe that the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\overline{\mathscr{X}},\mathscr{Y};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\overline{\mathscr{X}};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X_{i},K_{i}\cup Z_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X_{i},Z_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(K_{i}\cup Z_{i},Z_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\simeq H^{1}(K_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{j}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{i}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Phi_{i}^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}_{1}}

is commutative. Since
\mathcal{S}
is a finite set, one can take
\eta_{1}
small enough so that
\widetilde{\Phi_{i}^{*}}(H^{1}(\overline{\mathscr{X}};\mathbb{Z}_{2}))=\{0\}
. To see this, consider the chain of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\interior(X_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}_{2}(S^{3};\mathbb{Z}_{2}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\iota}}

where
\bm{\iota}:\mathscr{X}\to\mathcal{Z}_{2}(S^{3};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is the natural inclusion map. By the argument in
[
30
, Section 6]
and our choice of
K_{i}
, we know that
(\bm{\iota}\circ\Phi_{i}\circ\iota_{1})^{*}:H^{1}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}(S^{3};\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to H^{1}(K_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is trivial for small enough
\eta_{1}
. By the conclusion of the Smale’s conjecture
[
14
]
, we also know that
\bm{\iota}^{*}:H^{1}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}(S^{3};\mathbb{Z}_{2});\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to H^{1}(\mathscr{X};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is an isomorphism. All together imply that
(\Phi_{i}\circ\iota_{1})^{*}:H^{1}(\mathscr{X};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\to H^{1}(K_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is trivial, and this implies
\widetilde{\Phi_{i}^{*}}(H^{1}(\overline{\mathscr{X}};\mathbb{Z}_{2}))=\{0\}
.

It then follows that

\iota_{1}^{*}\circ\Phi_{i}^{*}(\bar{\lambda})=0.

Note that the sequence in the second line is exact. Hence there exists
\alpha\in H^{1}(X_{i},K_{i}\cup Z_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
such that
j_{1}^{*}(\alpha)=\Phi_{i}^{*}(\bar{\lambda})
. On the other hand, the following sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(X_{i},Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(X_{i},Z_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(Y_{i},Z_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}_{2}}

is also exact. Since
Y_{i}\cup K_{i}=X_{i}
, we have

j_{1}^{*}\big(H^{1}(X_{i},K_{i}\cup Z_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\big)\cup j_{2}^{*}\big(H^{3}(X_{i},Y_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})\big)\subset H^{4}(X_{i},X_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})=\{0\}.

Together with the fact that

j_{1}^{*}(\alpha)\cup[\Phi_{i}^{*}(\bar{\lambda})]^{3}=\Phi_{i}^{*}(\bar{\lambda})\cup[\Phi_{i}^{*}(\bar{\lambda})]^{3}\neq 0,

we then conclude that

[\Phi_{i}^{*}(\bar{\lambda})]^{3}\notin\mathrm{Im}j_{2}^{*}=\ker\iota_{2}^{*};

that is,
\iota_{2}^{*}[\Phi_{i}^{*}(\bar{\lambda})]^{3}\neq 0\in H^{3}(Y_{i},Z_{i};\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. Hence we have that
\{\Phi_{i}:(Y_{i},Z_{i})\to(\overline{\mathscr{X}},\mathscr{Y})\}\subset\mathscr{P}_{3}
. Then by the tightening process (see Section
2.2
), we can derive that

\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{3})<\mathbf{L}(\mathscr{P}_{4}).

This completes the proof.
∎
